Fixed questions (565)

Part: Combinatorial optimization problems (105)

1. Combinatorial optimization problems (15)

oc-f-001 · level facile · notes
How many distinct cyclic orderings do $n=6$ elements have, if a sequence, its rotations and its reverse are the same cyclic ordering?
  1. $64$
  2. $60$
  3. $720$
  4. $120$
Explanation. The number is $\frac{(n-1)!}{2}=\frac{5!}{2}=60$: the first element is fixed ($n$ rotations) and every sequence is identified with its reverse. $6!$ counts the orderings, $2^6$ the subsets.
oc-f-002 · level facile · notes
In a combinatorial optimization problem in maximization form, let $z(I)$ be the optimal value of instance $I$. The value $f(\bar S)$ of a feasible solution $\bar S$ is:
  1. a primal bound, i.e., a lower bound on $z(I)$
  2. a dual bound, i.e., an upper bound on $z(I)$
  3. a primal bound, i.e., an upper bound on $z(I)$
  4. a dual bound, i.e., a lower bound on $z(I)$
Explanation. A feasible solution gives a primal bound; in a maximization problem $f(\bar S)\le z(I)$, hence it is a lower bound. In minimization problems the roles are exchanged.
oc-f-003 · level facile · notes
For an instance of a combinatorial optimization problem in maximization form we know $\mathrm{LB}=16$ (value of a feasible solution) and $\mathrm{UB}=20$ (dual bound). What is the relative gap?
  1. $20\%$
  2. $4$
  3. $80\%$
  4. $25\%$
Explanation. The relative gap is $\frac{\mathrm{UB}-\mathrm{LB}}{|\mathrm{LB}|}=\frac{4}{16}=25\%$. The value $4$ is the absolute gap; dividing by $\mathrm{UB}$ gives $20\%$.
oc-f-004 · level teorema · notes
In a combinatorial optimization problem with objective function $f$, let $\beta$ be a dual bound on the optimal value $z(I)$ of an instance $I$ ($\beta\ge z(I)$ in maximization form, $\beta\le z(I)$ in minimization form). Which statement is correct?
  1. In maximization form $\lfloor\beta\rfloor$ is always a dual bound, even if $f$ does not take integer values
  2. If $f(S)\in\Z$ for every feasible solution $S$, in minimization form $\lfloor\beta\rfloor+1$ is also a dual bound
  3. If $f(S)\in\Z$ for every feasible solution $S$, in maximization form $\lfloor\beta\rfloor$ is also a dual bound
  4. If $f(S)\in\Z$ for every feasible solution $S$, in maximization form $\lceil\beta\rceil-1$ is also a dual bound
Explanation. With an integer objective, in a maximization problem $f(S)\le\beta$ implies $f(S)\le\lfloor\beta\rfloor$; in a minimization problem one rounds up, $\lceil\beta\rceil$. Without the integrality of $f$ the rounding is not allowed.
oc-f-005 · level facile · notes
In the knapsack problem (KP) with $n$ items and integer capacity $c$, an algorithm executes $\Theta(nc)$ steps. How is it classified with respect to the input size?
  1. Strongly polynomial, because it does not depend on the profits
  2. Pseudo-polynomial: polynomial in the value of $c$, not in the number of digits of $c$
  3. Polynomial: the number of steps is a polynomial in $n$ and $c$
  4. Exponential in $n$, because it examines the subsets of the items
Explanation. $c$ is written with $\log c$ digits: $\Theta(nc)$ is polynomial in the value of the largest number, not in the size of the instance. This is the definition of a pseudo-polynomial algorithm.
oc-f-147 · level facile · notes
For a combinatorial optimization problem in maximization form, with objective function $f$, what does its decision version ask?
  1. What the optimal value $z(I)$ of the instance is
  2. To find a feasible solution of maximum value
  3. Whether all feasible solutions have value at least $k$, for a given integer $k$
  4. Whether some feasible solution has value at least $k$, for a given integer $k$
Explanation. The decision version has a yes/no answer: «is there a solution with $f(S)\ge k$?». A solution of value at least $k$ is a certificate checkable in polynomial time.
oc-f-148 · level teorema · notes
An NP-hard problem is strongly NP-hard if it remains NP-hard even when all its numbers are bounded by a polynomial in the instance size. If $P\neq NP$, which statement is correct?
  1. A strongly NP-hard problem has a pseudo-polynomial algorithm, but no polynomial one
  2. A strongly NP-hard problem is polynomial if its numbers are bounded by a polynomial
  3. A strongly NP-hard problem has no pseudo-polynomial algorithm
  4. A strongly NP-hard problem has no exact algorithm
Explanation. With numbers bounded by a polynomial a pseudo-polynomial algorithm would become polynomial, and would solve a problem that is still NP-hard in that case. Exponential exact algorithms always exist (for example brute force).
oc-f-149 · level facile · notes
An NP-hard problem is weakly NP-hard if it admits a pseudo-polynomial algorithm (polynomial in the size and in the value of the largest number). Which of the following problems is only weakly NP-hard?
  1. The travelling salesman problem (TSP)
  2. The 0-1 knapsack problem (KP)
  3. Bin packing (BPP)
  4. Vertex coloring (VCP)
Explanation. The KP is solved by dynamic programming in $\Theta(nc)$, a pseudo-polynomial time; bin packing, coloring, maximum clique and the TSP are strongly NP-hard.
oc-f-150 · level facile · notes
The decisions of combinatorial optimization problems fall into five categories: IN/OUT, counting, assignment, ordering, configuration and partitioning. Which category does a bin packing solution (items in interchangeable bins of capacity $c$) belong to?
  1. Configuration and partitioning: a partition of the items
  2. IN/OUT: a subset of the items
  3. Ordering: a permutation of the items
  4. Counting: a vector of integer quantities
Explanation. The bins are interchangeable: only the grouping of the items matters, that is, a partition into groups respecting the capacity.
oc-f-151 · level facile · notes
In an assignment decision every element $i$ is assigned to exactly one of several distinct resources $j$ (for example tasks to different people). Which variables describe it naturally?
  1. $x_i\in\{0,1\}$, equal to $1$ if and only if element $i$ is selected
  2. $x_i\in\Z_{\ge0}$, the number of units of element $i$ to use
  3. A permutation $\sigma=(\sigma_1,\dots,\sigma_n)$ of the elements
  4. $x_{ij}\in\{0,1\}$, equal to $1$ if and only if $i$ goes to resource $j$, with $\sum_j x_{ij}=1$ for every $i$
Explanation. An assignment is a function from the elements to the resources: one binary variable per pair and one equality constraint per element. The others are the IN/OUT, counting and ordering variables.
oc-f-152 · level facile · notes
For $n=20$ elements four numbers of candidates are compared: $n^3$, the subsets $2^n$, the partitions $B(n)$ (Bell number) and the cyclic orderings $\frac{(n-1)!}{2}$. Which increasing order is correct?
  1. $2^n<n^3<B(n)<\frac{(n-1)!}{2}$
  2. $n^3<B(n)<2^n<\frac{(n-1)!}{2}$
  3. $n^3<2^n<B(n)<\frac{(n-1)!}{2}$
  4. $n^3<2^n<\frac{(n-1)!}{2}<B(n)$
Explanation. For $n=20$: $n^3=8000$, $2^n\approx 1.05\cdot10^6$, $B(n)\approx 5.17\cdot 10^{13}$ and $\frac{(n-1)!}{2}\approx 6.08\cdot10^{16}$. For small $n$ the order may differ (with $n=5$: $12<32<52$).
oc-f-153 · level facile · notes
Brute force for an IN/OUT problem evaluates all $2^n$ subsets of the $n$ elements. A computer evaluates $10^9$ of them per second. About how long does it take with $n=60$?
  1. About $36$ centuries
  2. About $36$ years
  3. About $18$ minutes
  4. About $13$ days
Explanation. $2^{60}\approx 1.15\cdot10^{18}$, hence about $1.15\cdot10^{9}$ seconds are needed, that is, about $36$ years. $18$ minutes and $13$ days are the times for $n=40$ and $n=50$: every $10$ more elements multiply the time by about one thousand.
oc-f-154 · level facile · notes
A combinatorial optimization problem is given by a ground set $\mathscr{U}=\{1,\dots,n\}$, a family $\mathcal{F}\subseteq 2^{\mathscr{U}}$ of feasible solutions and an objective function $f:\mathcal{F}\to\R$. Which property distinguishes it from a generic optimization problem?
  1. $\mathscr{U}$ is finite, hence the feasible solutions are finitely many
  2. The objective function $f$ is linear in the decision variables
  3. The family $\mathcal{F}$ contains all the subsets of $\mathscr{U}$
  4. The feasible solutions are vectors of real numbers
Explanation. Solutions are subsets of a finite set: there are at most $2^n$ of them, and an optimum always exists if $\mathcal{F}\ne\emptyset$. The objective may be any function and $\mathcal{F}$ describes the constraints.
oc-f-155 · level facile · notes
The 0-1 knapsack problem (items $\mathscr{U}=\{1,\dots,n\}$ with profits $p_j$, weights $w_j$, capacity $c$) is written as a combinatorial optimization problem with $f(S)=\sum_{j\in S}p_j$. What is the family $\mathcal{F}$ of feasible solutions?
  1. The subsets $S\subseteq\mathscr{U}$ with $\sum_{j\in S}w_j=c$
  2. All the $2^n$ subsets of $\mathscr{U}$
  3. The subsets $S\subseteq\mathscr{U}$ with $\sum_{j\in S}p_j\le c$
  4. The subsets $S\subseteq\mathscr{U}$ with $\sum_{j\in S}w_j\le c$
Explanation. The capacity constraint bounds the total weight, it does not fix it: $\mathcal{F}$ also contains the empty set. The equality describes subset sum.
oc-f-156 · level facile · notes
A set has $n=6$ elements. How many of its subsets have exactly $3$ elements, and how many subsets does it have in total?
  1. $20$ and $720$
  2. $18$ and $64$
  3. $20$ and $64$
  4. $120$ and $64$
Explanation. The subsets with $3$ elements are $\binom{6}{3}=20$; in total there are $\sum_k\binom{6}{k}=2^6=64$. $120=6\cdot5\cdot4$ counts the ordered triples, $720=6!$ the permutations.

2. Knapsack problems (17)

oc-f-006 · level facile · notes
The subset sum problem (SSP) asks whether, given integers $w_1,\dots,w_n$ and $c$, some subset sums to exactly $c$. How is it obtained as a special case of the knapsack problem (KP: profits $p_j$, weights $w_j$, capacity $c$)?
  1. With $w_j=1$ for every $j$: the answer is yes if and only if the optimal value is $c$
  2. With $c=\sum_j w_j$: the answer is yes if and only if the optimal value is $\sum_j p_j$
  3. With $p_j=w_j$ for every $j$: the answer is yes if and only if the optimal value is $c$
  4. With $p_j=1$ for every $j$: the answer is yes if and only if the optimal value is $n$
Explanation. With $p_j=w_j$ the KP looks for the largest total weight not exceeding $c$; the subset sum problem asks whether the total weight $c$ can be reached exactly, i.e., whether the optimal value is $c$.
oc-f-007 · level facile · notes
In the multiple-choice knapsack problem (MCKP) the items, each with a profit and a weight, are partitioned into $q$ classes, and the knapsack has capacity $c$. Which constraint concerns the classes?
  1. All the items of a single class are chosen, without exceeding the capacity
  2. Exactly one item is chosen from every class, without exceeding the capacity
  3. At most one item is chosen from every class, without exceeding the capacity
  4. At least one item is chosen from every class, without exceeding the capacity
Explanation. In the MCKP exactly one item is selected from each class: confusing it with «at most one» is a typical mistake of the chapter.
oc-f-008 · level teorema · notes
In the knapsack problem (KP: profits $p_j$, weights $w_j$, capacity $c$, optimal value $z(I)$), let $\bar k$ be the largest integer such that the $\bar k$ lightest items weigh at most $c$ in total. Which statement is correct?
  1. Every feasible solution has at most $\bar k$ items, hence $z(I)$ is at most the sum of the $\bar k$ largest profits
  2. Every feasible solution has at most $\bar k$ items, hence $z(I)$ is at most the sum of the profits of the $\bar k$ lightest items
  3. Every optimal solution has exactly $\bar k$ items, hence $z(I)$ is the sum of the $\bar k$ largest profits
  4. Every feasible solution has at most $\bar k$ items, hence $z(I)$ is at least the sum of the $\bar k$ largest profits
Explanation. This is the cardinality bound: a feasible subset has at most $\bar k$ items, and any $\bar k$ items have total profit at most the $\bar k$ largest profits. It is a dual bound.
oc-f-009 · level facile · notes
Knapsack problem (KP) with capacity $c=9$, profits $p=(6,5,8,9,6,7)$ and weights $w=(2,3,6,7,5,9)$. The cardinality bound is the sum of the $\bar k$ largest profits, where $\bar k$ is the largest number of lightest items that fit together in the knapsack. What is its value?
  1. $11$
  2. $24$
  3. $15$
  4. $17$
Explanation. The two lightest items weigh $2+3=5\le 9$, the three lightest $2+3+5=10>9$: $\bar k=2$. The two largest profits are $9+8=17$. $15$ is the optimal value, not a bound; $11$ uses the profits of the lightest items.
oc-f-157 · level facile · notes
In the bounded knapsack problem (BKP) there are $n$ item types: type $j$ has profit $p_j$, weight $w_j$ and $b_j$ available copies; the capacity is $c$. What is the domain of the variable $x_j$, the number of chosen copies of type $j$?
  1. $x_j\in\{0,1\}$
  2. $x_j\in\Z_{\ge0}$, without upper bound
  3. $x_j\in\{0,1,\dots,b_j\,w_j\}$
  4. $x_j\in\{0,1,\dots,b_j\}$
Explanation. From $0$ to $b_j$ copies of type $j$ can be taken. With $b_j=1$ it is the 0/1 KP, without upper bound the unbounded knapsack.
oc-f-158 · level facile · notes
In the bounded knapsack problem (BKP) type $j$ has profit $p_j$, weight $w_j$ and $b_j$ available copies. How is a BKP instance transformed into an equivalent 0-1 knapsack (KP) instance?
  1. Type $j$ is replaced by $b_j$ items of profit $p_j/b_j$ and weight $w_j/b_j$
  2. One item per type is kept and the capacity is multiplied by $\max_j b_j$
  3. Type $j$ is replaced by $b_j$ identical items of profit $p_j$ and weight $w_j$
  4. Type $j$ is replaced by one item of profit $b_jp_j$ and weight $b_jw_j$
Explanation. Every copy becomes a 0/1 item: choosing $d$ copies is choosing $d$ of the $b_j$ identical items. A single aggregated item would only allow $0$ or $b_j$ copies.
oc-f-159 · level facile · notes
In the knapsack problem with setups (KPS) the items are partitioned into classes; an item of class $h$ can be chosen only if the class is activated ($y_h=1$), and the activation costs a profit $f_h$ and a weight $s_h$. Which objective is maximized?
  1. $\sum_j p_jx_j-\sum_h s_hy_h$
  2. $\sum_j p_jx_j-\sum_h f_hy_h$
  3. $\sum_j p_jx_j+\sum_h f_hy_h$
  4. $\sum_j p_jx_j-\sum_h f_h\sum_{j\in\mathscr{J}_h}x_j$
Explanation. The setup cost $f_h$ is paid once per activated class, not per item; the setup weight $s_h$ enters the capacity constraint $\sum_j w_jx_j+\sum_h s_hy_h\le c$.
oc-f-160 · level facile · notes
Knapsack with setups (KPS) with capacity $c=10$ and a single class, with setup cost $f=4$ and setup weight $s=3$; the class contains the items $(p_j,w_j)=(6,4)$ and $(5,3)$. What is the optimal value (profit minus setup cost)?
  1. $7$
  2. $11$
  3. $2$
  4. $1$
Explanation. Activating the class leaves $10-3=7$ units of capacity: both items fit, $6+5-4=7$. Without activating it the value is $0$. $11$ forgets the setup cost.
oc-f-161 · level facile · notes
Multidimensional knapsack (MDKP) with $2$ dimensions and capacities $\vet c=(5,4)$; the items have profit and weights $(p_j;w_{1j},w_{2j})$: $A=(4;3,1)$, $B=(3;2,3)$, $C=(3;2,1)$. A subset is feasible if in every dimension the total weight does not exceed the capacity. What is the optimal value?
  1. $10$
  2. $6$
  3. $4$
  4. $7$
Explanation. $\{A,B\}$ weighs $(5,4)$ and $\{A,C\}$ weighs $(5,2)$: both are worth $7$. $\{A,B,C\}$ weighs $(7,5)$ and is infeasible; $10$ ignores the capacities.
oc-f-162 · level facile · notes
In the multidimensional knapsack problem (MDKP) every item $j$ has a weight $w_{dj}$ in each of the $m$ dimensions and the knapsack a capacity $c_d$ per dimension; a chosen item consumes capacity in all dimensions. What is obtained with $m=1$?
  1. The bounded knapsack (BKP)
  2. Subset sum (SSP)
  3. The 0-1 knapsack (KP)
  4. The multiple knapsack (MKP)
Explanation. With a single dimension only one capacity constraint is left: it is the KP. In the multiple knapsack, instead, an item consumes the capacity of a single knapsack.
oc-f-163 · level facile · notes
Multiple-choice knapsack (MCKP: exactly one item per class is chosen, without exceeding the capacity) with $c=7$ and classes $\mathscr{J}_1=\{(3,2),(5,4)\}$, $\mathscr{J}_2=\{(4,3),(6,5)\}$, where every pair is $(p_j,w_j)$. What is the optimal value?
  1. $10$
  2. $9$
  3. $11$
  4. $7$
Explanation. The feasible pairs are $(3,2)+(4,3)$ ($7$, weight $5$), $(3,2)+(6,5)$ ($9$, weight $7$) and $(5,4)+(4,3)$ ($9$, weight $7$); $(5,4)+(6,5)$ weighs $9>7$. $11$ ignores the capacity.
oc-f-164 · level facile · notes
In the multiple knapsack problem (MKP) there are $m$ knapsacks of capacities $c_1,\dots,c_m$ and $n$ items with profit $p_j$ and weight $w_j$. Which statement describes the problem?
  1. The profit of the inserted items is maximized, and every item goes into at most one knapsack
  2. All items are inserted, minimizing the number of knapsacks used
  3. The profit is maximized, and every item can be split among several knapsacks
  4. The profit is maximized, and every item goes into exactly one knapsack
Explanation. In the MKP items may stay out; each one lies entirely in a single knapsack. Inserting all items minimizing the bins is bin packing.
oc-f-165 · level facile · notes
Multiple knapsack (MKP) with $2$ knapsacks of capacity $5$ and $5$ and items $(p_j,w_j)=(6,4),(5,3),(4,3),(3,2)$; every item goes into at most one knapsack. What are the optimal value and the value of the KP with a single knapsack of capacity $10$?
  1. $15$ and $15$
  2. $14$ and $14$
  3. $13$ and $15$
  4. $14$ and $15$
Explanation. With two knapsacks: $\{(6,4)\}$ and $\{(5,3),(3,2)\}$ give $14$; the three most profitable items weigh $4,3,3$ and cannot be split into two groups of weight at most $5$. With capacity $10$ the items $(6,4),(5,3),(4,3)$ fit: $15$, only an upper bound for the MKP.
oc-f-166 · level facile · notes
Subset sum (SSP): given the integers $w=(3,5,7,11)$ and $c=15$, is there a subset summing to exactly $15$?
  1. Yes: $\{3,11\}$
  2. No: the sum closest to $15$ is $14$
  3. Yes: $\{3,5,7\}$
  4. Yes: $\{5,11\}$
Explanation. $3+5+7=15$. Instead $5+11=16$ and $3+11=14$.
oc-f-167 · level facile · notes
Unbounded knapsack (UKP: unlimited copies of every type) with capacity $c=10$ and two types $(p_j,w_j)=(7,4)$ and $(5,3)$. What is the optimal value?
  1. $15$
  2. $17$
  3. $14$
  4. $12$
Explanation. One copy of $(7,4)$ and two of $(5,3)$ weigh $10$ and are worth $17$. Two copies of the most efficient type give $14$ (greedy), three of the second $15$; $12$ is the 0/1 KP.
oc-f-168 · level facile · notes
In the unbounded knapsack problem (UKP) the variable $x_j\in\Z_{\ge0}$ is the number of copies of type $j$ (integer weight $w_j\ge1$), with capacity $c$. Which upper bound holds anyway for $x_j$?
  1. $x_j\le\lfloor c/w_j\rfloor$, because every copy weighs $w_j$
  2. $x_j\le 1$, because every type is chosen once
  3. $x_j\le n$, the number of types
  4. $x_j\le\lfloor c/p_j\rfloor$, because every copy is worth $p_j$
Explanation. $x_j$ copies weigh $w_jx_j\le c$. This is why the UKP is a special case of the BKP with $b_j=\lfloor c/w_j\rfloor$.
oc-f-564 · level ipotesi · notes
In the 0-1 knapsack (capacity $c$), let $\bar k$ be the largest integer such that the $\bar k$ lightest items weigh in total at most $c$. Why does every feasible solution contain at most $\bar k$ items?
  1. Any $\bar k+1$ items weigh at least as much as the $\bar k+1$ lightest ones, which already exceed $c$
  2. Because the lightest items are also the most efficient
  3. Because the greedy by weight always finds the optimal solution
  4. Because every item weighs at most $c/\bar k$
Explanation. The sum of the weights of $\bar k+1$ items is minimum when they are the lightest, and by definition of $\bar k$ this sum exceeds $c$. Hence the cardinality bound: the sum of the $\bar k$ largest profits.

3. Packing problems (13)

oc-f-010 · level facile · notes
Bin packing (BPP): three items of weight $6$ must be packed into the minimum number of bins of capacity $c=10$. What are the weight bound $\left\lceil\sum_j w_j/c\right\rceil$ and the optimal value $z(I)$?
  1. Weight bound $1$, $z(I)=2$
  2. Weight bound $2$, $z(I)=3$
  3. Weight bound $2$, $z(I)=2$
  4. Weight bound $3$, $z(I)=3$
Explanation. $\lceil 18/10\rceil=2$, but two items of weight $6$ do not fit together: $3$ bins are needed. The weight bound is not always attained.
oc-f-011 · level facile · notes
In bin packing (BPP) $n$ items of weight $w_j$ must be packed into the minimum number of bins of capacity $c$. In cutting stock (CSP) item $j$ also has a demand $d_j\in\Z_{\ge1}$. How are the two problems related?
  1. The CSP asks to pack $d_j$ identical copies of item $j$; the BPP is the case $d_j=1$ for every $j$
  2. The CSP has bins of different capacities $d_j$; the BPP is the case with all capacities equal
  3. The CSP allows splitting an item into $d_j$ parts; the BPP is the case without splitting
  4. The CSP asks to fill $d_j$ bins with item $j$; the BPP is the case with a single bin
Explanation. The CSP asks to pack $d_j$ copies of every item $j$ into the minimum number of bins of capacity $c$; with $d_j=1$ for every $j$ it is the BPP. Items are not split and the capacity is unique.
oc-f-012 · level facile · notes
In bin packing (BPP) a solution is a partition of the $n$ items into bins, and the order of the bins does not matter. How many candidate partitions of $n$ items are there, before checking the capacities ($B(n)$ is the $n$-th Bell number)?
  1. $2^n$
  2. $n!$
  3. $\frac{(n-1)!}{2}$
  4. $B(n)$
Explanation. The partitions of a set of $n$ elements are $B(n)$ (Bell numbers); the order of the bins does not matter. $2^n$ counts the subsets, $n!$ the orderings.
oc-f-169 · level facile · notes
In bin packing (BPP) $n$ items of weight $w_j$ must be packed into the minimum number of bins of capacity $c$. Why is $w_j\le c$ assumed for every item?
  1. Otherwise two items could share the same bin
  2. Otherwise the number of bins could exceed $n$
  3. Otherwise the item fits in no bin and there are no feasible solutions
  4. Otherwise the weight bound $\lceil\sum_j w_j/c\rceil$ would not be an integer
Explanation. An item heavier than the capacity cannot be packed: the instance would have no solution. With $w_j\le c$ putting every item in its own bin is always feasible.
oc-f-170 · level facile · notes
Bin packing (BPP: minimum number of bins of capacity $c$) with $c=10$ and weights $w=(7,6,5,4,3,2)$. What is the optimal value $z(I)$?
  1. $6$
  2. $3$
  3. $4$
  4. $2$
Explanation. $\sum_j w_j=27$, hence at least $\lceil 2.7\rceil=3$ bins are needed; $\{7,3\}$, $\{6,4\}$, $\{5,2\}$ is a solution with $3$ bins, hence optimal.
oc-f-171 · level teorema · notes
In bin packing (weights $w_j$, capacity $c$, optimal value $z(I)$) the weight bound is $L_1=\left\lceil\sum_j w_j/c\right\rceil$. Which statement is correct?
  1. $L_1\le z(I)$ for every instance, and $L_1<z(I)$ is possible
  2. $L_1=z(I)$ for every instance
  3. $L_1\ge z(I)$ for every instance, because it is rounded up
  4. $L_1\le z(I)$ only if all weights are smaller than $c/2$
Explanation. $z(I)$ bins contain at most $z(I)\,c$ units of weight, hence $z(I)\ge\sum_jw_j/c$, rounded up because $z(I)$ is an integer. With three items of weight $6$ and $c=10$: $L_1=2<3=z(I)$.
oc-f-172 · level facile · notes
In bin packing with capacity $c$, two items of weight larger than $c/2$ never share a bin. With $c=10$ and weights $w=(6,6,7,2,2,2)$, which lower bound on the number of bins does this observation give, and which the weight bound $\lceil\sum_j w_j/c\rceil$?
  1. $3$ and $2$
  2. $2$ and $3$
  3. $6$ and $3$
  4. $3$ and $3$
Explanation. The three items of weight $6,6,7$ exceed $c/2=5$: $3$ different bins are needed. $\sum_jw_j=25$ and $\lceil 25/10\rceil=3$. Here the two bounds coincide, and $\{6,2,2\},\{6,2\},\{7\}$ is optimal.
oc-f-173 · level facile · notes
In bin packing a solution with $k$ bins can be written by giving every item the number $1,\dots,k$ of its bin. How many assignment vectors describe the same partition of the items, if all $k$ bins are non-empty?
  1. $k$, one for every starting bin
  2. $k^n$, one for every assignment of the $n$ items
  3. $k!$, one for every way of numbering the bins
  4. $2^k$, one for every subset of bins
Explanation. Renumbering the bins does not change the solution: every partition corresponds to $k!$ vectors. This is the symmetry that weakens the models with numbered bins.
oc-f-174 · level facile · notes
Bin packing (BPP: items of weight $w_j$ into the minimum number of bins of capacity $c$) is strongly NP-hard. What does this imply, if $P\neq NP$?
  1. Every instance with more than $50$ items is intractable
  2. There is no pseudo-polynomial algorithm, for example of time $O(nc)$
  3. There is no exact algorithm at all for the BPP
  4. There is an $O(nc)$ algorithm by DP, as for the knapsack
Explanation. Strongly NP-hard means it stays hard even with small numbers: an algorithm polynomial in $n$ and in the value of $c$ is not expected. Exponential exact algorithms exist, and many large instances are solved in practice.
oc-f-175 · level facile · notes
Cutting stock (CSP): rolls of length $c=10$; $d_1=5$ pieces of length $4$ and $d_2=4$ pieces of length $3$ are needed, and the number of cut rolls is minimized. What is the weight bound $\left\lceil\sum_j d_jw_j/c\right\rceil$?
  1. $4$
  2. $3$
  3. $5$
  4. $9$
Explanation. $\sum_j d_jw_j=5\cdot4+4\cdot3=32$ and $\lceil 3.2\rceil=4$. The bound is attained: two rolls $4+3+3$, one $4+4$ and one $4$. $9$ is the number of pieces.
oc-f-176 · level facile · notes
In cutting stock (CSP) $d_j$ identical copies of piece $j$ are needed, cut from rolls of length $c$. Why is a solution described by cutting patterns (how many copies of each type are cut from a roll)?
  1. Cutting patterns allow cutting one piece from two rolls
  2. Cutting patterns make every roll different from the others
  3. Cutting patterns remove the length constraint $c$
  4. Copies of the same piece are identical: only how many each roll contains matters
Explanation. Treating every copy as a distinct item multiplies the equivalent solutions; a pattern only states how many copies of each type the roll contains and satisfies $\sum_j a_jw_j\le c$.
oc-f-177 · level facile · notes
A cutting stock instance (pieces $j=1,\dots,n$ with demand $d_j$ and length $w_j$, rolls of length $c$) can be seen as bin packing with $\sum_j d_j$ items. What is the drawback of this transformation?
  1. The resulting bin packing has bins of different capacities
  2. The transformation works only if all $d_j$ equal $1$
  3. The number of items grows with the values $d_j$, and identical copies create many symmetric solutions
  4. The resulting bin packing has an optimal value different from the CSP one
Explanation. The transformation is correct (same optimal value), but the instance becomes large when the demands are large, and exchanging identical copies gives equivalent solutions.
oc-f-563 · level ipotesi · notes
In bin packing ($n$ items of weight $w_j$, bins of capacity $c$) every feasible solution uses at least $\lceil\sum_j w_j/c\rceil$ bins. Which facts are needed to prove it?
  1. A solution with $k$ bins has $\sum_j w_j\le kc$, since every bin carries at most $c$; and $k$ is integer
  2. Every item weighs at most $c/2$; and $k$ is integer
  3. The items are sorted by non-increasing weight; and every bin is full
  4. At most one bin is less than half full; and $k$ is integer
Explanation. Adding up the loads, $\sum_j w_j\le kc$, i.e., $k\ge\sum_j w_j/c$; since $k$ is integer it is rounded up. No assumption on the weights or on the order is needed.

4. Coloring problems (12)

oc-f-013 · level teorema · notes
For a graph $G=(V,E)$ with $n$ vertices let $\omega(G)$ be the cardinality of a maximum clique, $\alpha(G)$ that of a maximum stable set and $\chi(G)$ the chromatic number (minimum number of colors of a vertex coloring). Which pair of inequalities holds for every graph?
  1. $\chi(G)\le\omega(G)$ and $\chi(G)\le\left\lceil n/\alpha(G)\right\rceil$
  2. $\omega(G)\le\chi(G)$ and $\chi(G)\le\left\lceil n/\alpha(G)\right\rceil$
  3. $\alpha(G)\le\chi(G)$ and $\omega(G)\le\chi(G)$
  4. $\omega(G)\le\chi(G)$ and $\left\lceil n/\alpha(G)\right\rceil\le\chi(G)$
Explanation. A clique needs pairwise different colors (clique bound); every color class is a stable set, with at most $\alpha(G)$ vertices, hence at least $\lceil n/\alpha(G)\rceil$ colors are needed. Both are dual bounds.
oc-f-014 · level ipotesi · notes
For a graph $G$ with $n$ vertices, let $\chi(G)$ be the chromatic number and $\alpha(G)$ the cardinality of a maximum stable set. Which facts are needed to prove the bound $\left\lceil n/\alpha(G)\right\rceil\le\chi(G)$?
  1. The graph contains a clique with $\chi(G)$ vertices; every stable set has at most $\alpha(G)$ vertices; $\chi(G)$ is an integer
  2. Every vertex has degree at most $\Delta(G)$ (maximum degree); there are at most $\Delta(G)+1$ color classes; $\chi(G)$ is an integer
  3. Every color class is a stable set, hence it has at most $\alpha(G)$ vertices; the classes cover the $n$ vertices; $\chi(G)$ is an integer
  4. Every color class is a clique, hence it has at most $\omega(G)$ vertices; the classes cover the $n$ vertices; $\chi(G)$ is an integer
Explanation. With $\chi(G)$ stable classes, each with at most $\alpha(G)$ vertices, we have $\chi(G)\,\alpha(G)\ge n$; since $\chi(G)$ is an integer, we round up.
oc-f-015 · level facile · notes
The Petersen graph has $n=10$ vertices, maximum cliques of $\omega(G)=2$ vertices and maximum stable sets of $\alpha(G)=4$ vertices. What do the clique bound $\omega(G)$ and the stable set bound $\lceil n/\alpha(G)\rceil$ give on the chromatic number $\chi(G)$?
  1. Clique bound $2$, stable set bound $5$: hence $\chi(G)\ge 5$
  2. Clique bound $2$, stable set bound $3$: hence $\chi(G)\ge 3$
  3. Clique bound $2$, stable set bound $2$: hence $\chi(G)\ge 2$
  4. Clique bound $4$, stable set bound $3$: hence $\chi(G)\ge 4$
Explanation. $\omega(G)=2$ and $\lceil 10/4\rceil=3$. The clique bound leaves a gap; the stable set bound closes it, because a coloring with $3$ colors exists.
oc-f-016 · level facile · notes
In the vertex coloring problem (VCP) on a graph $G$ every vertex gets a color, adjacent vertices get different colors, and the number of colors is minimum. A solution can be seen as:
  1. a partition of the vertices into stable sets, up to renaming the colors
  2. a partition of the vertices into cliques, up to renaming the colors
  3. a partition of the edges into matchings, up to renaming the colors
  4. a maximum stable set, plus a different color for every other vertex
Explanation. Every color class is a stable set (non-adjacent vertices can share a color); permuting the colors does not give different solutions.
oc-f-186 · level facile · notes
In vertex coloring (VCP) we minimize the number of colors, with different colors on adjacent vertices; $\chi(G)$ is the chromatic number and $\omega(G)$ the size of a maximum clique. For the cycle $C_5$ (5 vertices on a circle), what are $\omega(C_5)$, the bound $\lceil n/\alpha\rceil$ (with $\alpha=2$ the maximum stable set) and $\chi(C_5)$?
  1. $3$, $3$ and $3$
  2. $2$, $2$ and $3$
  3. $2$, $3$ and $3$
  4. $2$, $3$ and $2$
Explanation. $C_5$ has no triangle ($\omega=2$), has $\alpha=2$ and hence $\lceil 5/2\rceil=3$; an odd cycle cannot be colored with $2$ colors, and $3$ suffice: $\chi(C_5)=3$.
oc-f-187 · level facile · notes
In vertex coloring (VCP), a solution is a partition of the vertices into stable sets, up to renaming the colors. How many partitions does a set of $4$ vertices have, i.e., candidate solutions before checking the edges (Bell number $B(4)$)?
  1. $16$
  2. $15$
  3. $24$
  4. $256$
Explanation. $B(4)=15$: one part ($1$), two parts ($7$), three parts ($6$), four parts ($1$). $4^4=256$ counts the assignments of $4$ named colors, $4!=24$ the orderings, $2^4$ the subsets.
oc-f-188 · level facile · notes
In vertex coloring (VCP), for a graph $G$ we find by hand a clique with $3$ vertices and a feasible coloring with $4$ colors. What do we know about the chromatic number $\chi(G)$?
  1. $3\le\chi(G)\le 4$: the clique gives a dual bound, the coloring a primal bound
  2. $\chi(G)=4$: a coloring found by hand is always optimal on small graphs
  3. $\chi(G)=3$: the clique bound always equals the chromatic number
  4. $4\le\chi(G)$: the coloring gives a lower bound, the clique an upper bound
Explanation. The vertices of the clique need different colors, hence $\chi(G)\ge 3$; the coloring with $4$ colors proves $\chi(G)\le 4$. Without more information the chromatic number can be $3$ or $4$.
oc-f-189 · level facile · notes
In a simple graph $G=(V,E)$, the degree of a vertex is the number of its neighbours. A graph with $6$ vertices has degrees $(3,3,2,2,2,2)$. How many edges does it have?
  1. $14$
  2. $6$
  3. $15$
  4. $7$
Explanation. Every edge has two endpoints, so the sum of the degrees is $2m$: $14=2m$ and $m=7$. The value $15=\binom62$ is the number of edges of the complete graph.
oc-f-190 · level facile · notes
For a graph $G=(V,E)$ and a subset $S\subseteq V$, what is the induced subgraph $G[S]$?
  1. The graph with vertices $V\setminus S$ and all the edges of $G$ among these vertices
  2. The graph with vertices $S$ and only the edges of a spanning tree of $G$
  3. The graph with vertices $S$ and all the edges of $G$ with both endpoints in $S$
  4. The graph with vertices $S$ and all the edges of $G$ with at least one endpoint in $S$
Explanation. $G[S]$ keeps the vertices of $S$ and the edges of $G$ between two vertices of $S$. For example $S$ is a clique if and only if $G[S]$ is complete, and it is stable if and only if $G[S]$ has no edge.
oc-f-191 · level facile · notes
In vertex coloring (VCP), every color class is a stable set, so it has at most $\alpha(G)$ vertices. A graph has $n=9$ vertices and $\alpha(G)=2$. Which lower bound on the chromatic number $\chi(G)$ follows?
  1. $2$
  2. $5$
  3. $4$
  4. $7$
Explanation. With classes of at most $2$ vertices at least $\lceil 9/2\rceil=5$ classes are needed. The value $4$ forgets to round up, $2$ mistakes $\alpha$ for the bound.
oc-f-192 · level facile · notes
In vertex coloring (VCP), the wheel $W_5$ has a hub adjacent to all the $5$ vertices of a cycle $C_5$. What are the size $\omega(W_5)$ of a maximum clique and the chromatic number $\chi(W_5)$?
  1. $\omega=3$ and $\chi=4$
  2. $\omega=3$ and $\chi=3$
  3. $\omega=4$ and $\chi=4$
  4. $\omega=2$ and $\chi=3$
Explanation. The hub and two consecutive vertices of the cycle form a triangle, and there is no clique with $4$ vertices: $\omega=3$. The odd cycle needs $3$ colors and the hub a fourth one: $\chi=4>\omega$.
oc-f-193 · level facile · notes
In vertex coloring (VCP) of the complete graph $K_6$ ($6$ pairwise adjacent vertices), what is the chromatic number, and which bound certifies it?
  1. $2$, because every graph with an edge has $\chi\ge 2$
  2. $3$, certified by the stable set bound $\lceil 6/2\rceil$
  3. $5$, because the largest degree is $\Delta=5$
  4. $6$, certified by the clique bound $\omega(K_6)=6$
Explanation. All the vertices are adjacent, so $6$ different colors are needed: $\omega(K_6)=6\le\chi(K_6)\le 6$. The largest degree $5$ gives only the upper bound $\Delta+1=6$.

5. Clique problems (12)

oc-f-017 · level teorema · notes
Let $\overline{G}$ be the complement of the graph $G$ (same vertices; two vertices are adjacent in $\overline{G}$ if and only if they are not adjacent in $G$), and let $\omega$ and $\alpha$ be the cardinalities of a maximum clique and of a maximum stable set. Which statement holds for every graph $G$ with $n$ vertices?
  1. $K$ is stable in $G$ if and only if $V\setminus K$ is a clique of $G$; hence $\omega(G)+\alpha(G)=n$
  2. $K$ is a clique of $G$ if and only if it is a stable set of $\overline{G}$; hence $\omega(G)=\alpha(\overline{G})$
  3. $K$ is a clique of $G$ if and only if it is a stable set of $G$; hence $\omega(G)=\alpha(G)$
  4. $K$ is a clique of $G$ if and only if it is a clique of $\overline{G}$; hence $\omega(G)=\omega(\overline{G})$
Explanation. The edges of $\overline{G}$ are the non-adjacent pairs of $G$: pairwise adjacent vertices in $G$ are pairwise non-adjacent in $\overline{G}$. Hence the MCP on $G$ is the MSSP on $\overline{G}$.
oc-f-018 · level facile · notes
A graph $G$ has $m=10$ edges. A clique of $k$ vertices contains $k(k-1)/2$ edges, hence the cardinality $\omega(G)$ of a maximum clique satisfies $\omega(G)(\omega(G)-1)/2\le m$. What is the smallest upper bound on $\omega(G)$ that follows?
  1. $5$
  2. $4$
  3. $6$
  4. $10$
Explanation. With $\omega=5$ we get $5\cdot 4/2=10\le 10$; with $\omega=6$ we would get $15>10$. Hence $\omega(G)\le 5$.
oc-f-019 · level facile · notes
A graph $G$ has $n=7$ vertices and $m=12$ edges. The complement $\overline{G}$ has the same vertices, and two vertices are adjacent in $\overline{G}$ if and only if they are not adjacent in $G$. How many edges does $\overline{G}$ have?
  1. $12$
  2. $30$
  3. $37$
  4. $9$
Explanation. $|\overline{E}|=\binom{n}{2}-m=21-12=9$: the edges of $\overline{G}$ are the pairs that are not edges of $G$.
oc-f-020 · level facile · notes
In a graph, a clique is a set of pairwise adjacent vertices. What is the difference between a maximal clique and a maximum clique?
  1. It is maximal if it contains a maximum clique; it is maximum if no vertex can be added to it
  2. They are synonyms: no vertex can be added to it and no clique has more vertices
  3. It is maximal if no vertex can be added to it; it is maximum if no clique has more vertices
  4. It is maximum if no vertex can be added to it; it is maximal if no clique has more vertices
Explanation. Every maximum clique is maximal, but not conversely: a maximal clique can be smaller than $\omega(G)$.
oc-f-178 · level teorema · notes
In the maximum clique problem (MCP) on a graph $G$, let $\Delta$ be the largest degree of a vertex and $\omega(G)$ the size of a maximum clique. Which bound holds for every graph, and why?
  1. $\omega(G)\le\Delta+1$: in a clique with $k$ vertices every vertex has at least $k-1$ neighbours
  2. $\omega(G)\le\Delta$: in a clique with $k$ vertices every vertex has at least $k$ neighbours
  3. $\omega(G)\ge\Delta+1$: a vertex of degree $\Delta$ and its neighbours form a clique
  4. $\omega(G)\le\Delta-1$: in a clique with $k$ vertices every vertex has at most $k-2$ neighbours
Explanation. A vertex of a clique $K$ is adjacent to the other $|K|-1$ vertices of $K$, hence $|K|-1\le\Delta$. The neighbours of a vertex are not pairwise adjacent in general, so they do not form a clique.
oc-f-179 · level facile · notes
In the maximum clique problem (MCP), $k^{\max}$ is the largest integer $k$ such that at least $k$ vertices have degree at least $k-1$, and $\omega(G)\le k^{\max}$. A graph has degrees $(4,3,3,2,2,1)$. What is $k^{\max}$?
  1. $5$
  2. $4$
  3. $2$
  4. $3$
Explanation. For $k=4$ we need $4$ vertices of degree at least $3$, but there are only $3$; for $k=3$ we need $3$ vertices of degree at least $2$, and there are $5$. Hence $k^{\max}=3$; the degree bound $\Delta+1$ would give $5$.
oc-f-180 · level teorema · notes
In the maximum clique problem (MCP), let $\Delta$ be the largest degree and $k^{\max}$ the largest $k$ such that at least $k$ vertices have degree at least $k-1$. How are the two upper bounds $k^{\max}$ and $\Delta+1$ on $\omega(G)$ related?
  1. $k^{\max}=\Delta+1$ for every graph: the two bounds always coincide
  2. They are not comparable: on some graphs one wins, on others the other
  3. $k^{\max}\le\Delta+1$ for every graph: the degree count bound is never weaker
  4. $k^{\max}\ge\Delta+1$ for every graph: the degree bound is never weaker
Explanation. If $k$ vertices have degree at least $k-1$, then $\Delta\ge k-1$; hence $k^{\max}\le\Delta+1$. For example with degrees $(4,3,3,2,2,1)$ we get $k^{\max}=3<5=\Delta+1$.
oc-f-181 · level facile · notes
In the maximum clique problem (MCP) on a graph with $n=10$ vertices, a candidate solution is any subset of the vertices, to be checked pair by pair afterwards. How many candidate solutions are there?
  1. $512$
  2. $1024$
  3. $45$
  4. $3\,628\,800$
Explanation. The subsets of $10$ vertices are $2^{10}=1024$. $\binom{10}{2}=45$ counts the pairs of vertices, $10!$ the orderings; $2^9$ forgets half of the subsets.
oc-f-182 · level facile · notes
The decision version of the maximum clique problem (MCP) asks whether a graph with $n$ vertices has a clique with $k$ vertices. What can be said if $k$ is a fixed constant (for example $k=3$: is there a triangle)?
  1. It is decided in $O(n^k k^2)$ steps, polynomial for fixed $k$; with $k$ part of the input it stays NP-complete
  2. It stays NP-complete even for $k=3$, because the number of subsets is $2^n$
  3. It is decided in $O(2^k n)$ steps, polynomial even when $k$ is part of the input
  4. It is decided in $O(n^2)$ steps for every $k$, checking all the pairs only once
Explanation. The subsets with $k$ vertices are $\binom nk\le n^k$, and each is checked in $O(k^2)$: polynomial for fixed $k$, but the exponent grows with $k$. With $k$ given in the input the problem is one of Karp's 21 NP-complete problems.
oc-f-183 · level facile · notes
In the maximum clique problem (MCP), the vertices of a graph $G$ are partitioned into $4$ stable sets (sets of pairwise non-adjacent vertices). What follows for the size $\omega(G)$ of a maximum clique?
  1. $\omega(G)\ge 4$, because a clique contains at least one vertex of each stable set
  2. $\omega(G)=4$, because the classes of a partition into stable sets are cliques
  3. Nothing: stable sets give bounds only on the maximum stable set
  4. $\omega(G)\le 4$, because a clique contains at most one vertex of each stable set
Explanation. Two vertices of the same stable set are not adjacent, so a clique takes at most one of them: $\omega(G)\le 4$. It is the coloring bound $\omega(G)\le\chi(G)$, since the partition is a coloring with $4$ colors.
oc-f-184 · level facile · notes
In the weighted variants of the maximum clique, every vertex $v$ has a weight $w_v$ and every edge $\{u,v\}$ a weight $r_{uv}$; $w(K)$ and $r(K)$ add up the weights of the vertices and of the edges of the clique $K$. For the triangle $K=\{1,2,3\}$ with $w=(3,5,2)$, $r_{12}=4$, $r_{13}=1$, $r_{23}=6$, what are $w(K)$ and $r(K)$?
  1. $w(K)=10$ and $r(K)=21$
  2. $w(K)=10$ and $r(K)=6$
  3. $w(K)=10$ and $r(K)=11$
  4. $w(K)=11$ and $r(K)=10$
Explanation. $w(K)=3+5+2=10$ and $r(K)=4+1+6=11$. The value $21$ adds vertices and edges together, $6$ takes only the heaviest edge.
oc-f-185 · level facile · notes
In the edge-weighted maximum clique problem (EWMCP) we look for a clique $K$ maximizing $r(K)$, the sum of the weights $r_{uv}$ of its edges. Why, with all the weights $r_{uv}=1$, is the EWMCP the maximum clique problem (MCP)?
  1. Because $r(K)=1$ for every clique, and all cliques are optimal
  2. Because $r(K)=\binom{|K|}{2}$, which grows with the number $|K|$ of vertices
  3. Because $r(K)=|K|$, the number of vertices of the clique
  4. Because $r(K)=2|K|$, since every vertex has two edges
Explanation. A clique with $k$ vertices has $\binom k2$ edges, and $\binom k2$ is increasing in $k$: maximizing $r(K)$ is the same as maximizing $|K|$.

6. Routing problems (12)

oc-f-021 · level facile · notes
In the symmetric traveling salesman problem (TSP) ($d_{ij}=d_{ji}$) a tour visits every city once and returns to the start; two tours are equal if they use the same edges. How many distinct tours are there with $n=7$ cities?
  1. $5040$
  2. $2520$
  3. $360$
  4. $720$
Explanation. The tours are $\frac{(n-1)!}{2}=\frac{720}{2}=360$: neither the starting city nor the direction changes the tour. In the asymmetric case they would be $(n-1)!=720$.
oc-f-022 · level facile · notes
In the symmetric TSP with integer distances, let $a_i$ and $b_i$ be the two smallest distances from city $i$ to the others. In an instance with $5$ cities the sums $a_i+b_i$ are $5,7,6,8,9$. Which lower bound is obtained on the length $z(I)$ of the shortest tour?
  1. $17.5$
  2. $18$
  3. $35$
  4. $17$
Explanation. $z(I)\ge\left\lceil\frac12\sum_i(a_i+b_i)\right\rceil=\lceil 35/2\rceil=18$: every edge of the tour is counted by both its endpoints, and the lengths are integer.
oc-f-023 · level ipotesi · notes
In the symmetric TSP let $a_i$ and $b_i$ be the two smallest distances from city $i$ to the others and $z(I)$ the length of the shortest tour. Which facts are needed to prove $z(I)\ge\left\lceil\frac12\sum_{i}(a_i+b_i)\right\rceil$?
  1. In a tour every city has two edges, of length at least $a_i$ and $b_i$; every edge has two endpoints; distances are integer
  2. The triangle inequality, which allows shortcutting the tour; every edge has two endpoints; distances are integer
  3. A minimum spanning tree is shorter than the optimal tour; every edge has two endpoints; distances are integer
  4. In a tour every city has two edges; the nearest neighbour tour is optimal; distances are integer
Explanation. Summing over all the cities the lengths of their two tour edges counts every edge twice: $2z(I)\ge\sum_i(a_i+b_i)$; with integer distances we round up. The triangle inequality is not needed.
oc-f-024 · level facile · notes
In the TSP with distances $d_{ij}\ge 0$ between the cities, when is an instance called metric?
  1. When $d_{ij}\ge d_{ik}+d_{kj}$ for all triples of distinct cities $i,j,k$
  2. When $d_{ij}\le d_{ik}+d_{kj}$ for at least one triple of distinct cities $i,j,k$
  3. When $d_{ij}=d_{ji}$ for all pairs of distinct cities $i,j$
  4. When $d_{ij}\le d_{ik}+d_{kj}$ for all triples of distinct cities $i,j,k$
Explanation. The metric TSP requires the triangle inequality on all triples; holding for one triple is not enough, and symmetry alone is not enough.
oc-f-212 · level teorema · notes
In the asymmetric TSP (ATSP) the distance $d_{ij}$ from $i$ to $j$ may differ from $d_{ji}$, so a tour and its reverse are different solutions. How many distinct tours does an ATSP instance with $n=5$ cities have?
  1. $12$
  2. $120$
  3. $60$
  4. $24$
Explanation. Fixing the starting city leaves $(n-1)!=24$ orderings, and in the asymmetric case the direction matters. In the symmetric case one divides by $2$: $12$.
oc-f-213 · level facile · notes
In the TSP the length of the tour $(\sigma_1,\dots,\sigma_n)$ is $\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}d_{\sigma_k\sigma_{k+1}}+d_{\sigma_n\sigma_1}$. With $d_{12}=3$, $d_{23}=4$, $d_{34}=5$, $d_{41}=6$, how long is the tour $(1,2,3,4)$?
  1. $15$
  2. $21$
  3. $18$
  4. $12$
Explanation. $3+4+5+6=18$. The value $12$ forgets the return edge $d_{41}$, which is part of the tour.
oc-f-214 · level facile · notes
Symmetric TSP with $4$ cities: $d_{12}=2$, $d_{13}=5$, $d_{14}=4$, $d_{23}=3$, $d_{24}=6$, $d_{34}=1$. The nearest neighbour rule starts from city $1$, goes each time to the nearest unvisited city and finally returns to $1$. How long is the tour?
  1. $12$
  2. $10$
  3. $6$
  4. $14$
Explanation. $1\to2$ ($2$), $2\to3$ ($3$), $3\to4$ ($1$), return $4\to1$ ($4$): $10$. Without the return one gets $6$; the tour $(1,2,4,3)$ has length $14$.
oc-f-215 · level facile · notes
Symmetric TSP with $4$ cities: $d_{12}=2$, $d_{13}=5$, $d_{14}=4$, $d_{23}=3$, $d_{24}=6$, $d_{34}=1$. With $a_i,b_i$ the two smallest distances from city $i$, the lower bound is $\left\lceil\frac12\sum_i(a_i+b_i)\right\rceil$. What is it?
  1. $10$
  2. $20$
  3. $9$
  4. $5$
Explanation. The sums $a_i+b_i$ are $6,5,4,5$, total $20$, and half of it is $10$. The tour $(1,2,3,4)$ has length $10$: the bound proves it optimal.
oc-f-216 · level teorema · notes
In the TSP, a closed walk through all the cities that repeats some of them is turned into a tour by skipping the cities already visited (shortcuts). Under which assumption on the distances $d_{ij}$ is the resulting tour not longer than the closed walk?
  1. Symmetry $d_{ij}=d_{ji}$ for all pairs of cities
  2. Positive integer distances, $d_{ij}\in\Z_{\ge 1}$
  3. All distances equal, $d_{ij}=d$ for every pair
  4. The triangle inequality $d_{ij}\le d_{ik}+d_{kj}$ for all triples
Explanation. Skipping city $k$ replaces $d_{ik}+d_{kj}$ by $d_{ij}$, which is not larger by the triangle inequality; repeating for every skip the walk does not get longer. It is the key step of the double tree and Christofides algorithms.
oc-f-217 · level facile · notes
A TSP instance is metric if $d_{ij}\le d_{ik}+d_{kj}$ for all triples of distinct cities. On $3$ cities with $d_{12}=3$, $d_{23}=4$, $d_{13}=8$, is the instance metric?
  1. Yes: $d_{12}+d_{13}\ge d_{23}$
  2. No: $d_{12}<d_{23}$
  3. No: $d_{13}=8>d_{12}+d_{23}=7$
  4. Yes: all distances are positive
Explanation. One violating triple is enough: going from $1$ to $3$ through $2$ costs $7<8$. Positive distances are not enough, and the order between $d_{12}$ and $d_{23}$ does not matter.
oc-f-218 · level facile · notes
In the symmetric TSP with $n$ cities the distinct tours are $(n-1)!/2$. Why is listing all of them impossible already with $n=20$ cities?
  1. $20!\approx 2\cdot 10^{18}$ tours: the division by $2$ does not apply to the symmetric case
  2. $19!/2\approx 6\cdot 10^{16}$ tours: even at a billion tours per second it takes about two years
  3. $2^{20}\approx 10^{6}$ tours: too many to store in a table
  4. $20^2=400$ tours, but each tour needs exponential time for its length
Explanation. $(n-1)!/2$ with $n=20$ is about $6\cdot10^{16}$: at $10^9$ tours per second it takes $6\cdot10^7$ seconds, almost two years. The length of a tour is computed in linear time.
oc-f-219 · level facile · notes
A symmetric TSP instance ($d_{ij}=d_{ji}$) can be seen as an asymmetric TSP (ATSP) instance. How are the tours of the two problems related?
  1. Every symmetric tour corresponds to two ATSP tours, one per direction, of the same length
  2. Every symmetric tour corresponds to one ATSP tour only, because the distances coincide
  3. The ATSP tours are $n$ times more, one for every starting city
  4. The ATSP has fewer tours, because some arcs cannot be travelled
Explanation. In the ATSP the two directions are different solutions; with symmetric distances they have the same length. This is why the tours are $(n-1)!$ in the ATSP and $(n-1)!/2$ in the symmetric TSP.

7. Scheduling problems (12)

oc-f-025 · level facile · notes
In scheduling on identical machines $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ one assigns $n$ jobs to $m$ identical machines minimizing the maximum load; a schedule is a partition of the jobs into at most $m$ sets. How many distinct schedules are there with $n=4$ and $m=2$?
  1. $8$
  2. $16$
  3. $7$
  4. $24$
Explanation. There are $m^n=16$ assignments, but renaming the machines gives the same schedule: the partitions are $S(4,1)+S(4,2)=1+7=8$.
oc-f-026 · level teorema · notes
In scheduling $P\,||\,C_{\max}$, $n$ jobs of integer duration $t_j$ are assigned to $m$ identical machines minimizing the makespan (maximum load); let $z(I)$ be the optimal makespan. Which lower bound is valid for every instance?
  1. $z(I)\ge\max_j t_j+\left\lceil\sum_j t_j/m\right\rceil$
  2. $z(I)\ge\min_j t_j+\left\lceil\sum_j t_j/m\right\rceil$
  3. $z(I)\ge\sum_j t_j-\max_j t_j$
  4. $z(I)\ge\max\left\{\max_j t_j,\ \left\lceil\sum_j t_j/m\right\rceil\right\}$
Explanation. The machine of the longest job has load at least $\max_j t_j$, and the maximum load is at least the average $\sum_j t_j/m$, rounded up. The sums are not valid: with $m=2$ and $t=(1,1)$ we have $z(I)=1$; with $m=3$ and $t=(1,1,1)$ we have $z(I)=1<3-1$.
oc-f-027 · level facile · notes
In bin packing (BPP) the items go into bins of capacity $c$; in scheduling $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ the jobs go onto $m$ identical machines. In what sense is one problem the «mirror» of the other?
  1. In both the number of bins (machines) is fixed and the maximum load is minimized; only the names of the data change
  2. In the BPP the number of bins is minimized; $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ is the case of the BPP with a single machine of capacity $c$
  3. In the BPP the capacity is fixed and the number of bins is minimized; in $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ the number of machines is fixed and the maximum load is minimized
  4. In the BPP the number of bins is fixed and the maximum load is minimized; in $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ the capacity is fixed and the number of machines is minimized
Explanation. Jobs and items, machines and bins correspond: in one problem the capacity is fixed, in the other the number of «bins», and the other quantity is optimized.
oc-f-203 · level facile · notes
In the three-field notation $\alpha\,|\,\beta\,|\,\gamma$ of parallel machine scheduling, the field $\alpha$ describes the machines: $P$, $Q$ or $R$. What does $Q\,||\,C_{\max}$ mean?
  1. Unrelated machines: job $j$ takes an arbitrary time $t_{ij}$ on machine $i$
  2. A fixed number of identical machines, not part of the input
  3. Machines with different speeds $s_i$: job $j$ takes $t_j/s_i$ on machine $i$
  4. Identical machines: job $j$ takes $t_j$ on every machine
Explanation. $P$ means identical machines, $Q$ uniform machines with speeds $s_i$ (times $t_j/s_i$), $R$ unrelated machines with arbitrary times $t_{ij}$; $C_{\max}$ in the third field is the makespan to minimize.
oc-f-204 · level facile · notes
In scheduling on identical machines $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ ($n$ jobs, $m$ machines), an assignment says on which machine every job goes. How many assignments are there with $n=5$ jobs and $m=3$ machines?
  1. $41$
  2. $243$
  3. $125$
  4. $15$
Explanation. Every job chooses one of the $3$ machines: $3^5=243$ assignments. Since the machines are identical, many assignments give the same schedule: the distinct schedules are $\sum_{k\le 3}S(5,k)=1+15+25=41$.
oc-f-205 · level facile · notes
In scheduling $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ the machines are identical, so a schedule is a partition of the jobs into at most $m$ sets; $S(n,k)$ is the Stirling number of the second kind (partitions of $n$ elements into $k$ sets). How many distinct schedules are there with $n=5$ and $m=2$?
  1. $16$
  2. $32$
  3. $15$
  4. $31$
Explanation. $S(5,1)+S(5,2)=1+15=16$. The assignments are $2^5=32$; every schedule using two machines corresponds to two assignments, and so does the one using a single machine ($1+15$ partitions).
oc-f-206 · level facile · notes
In scheduling $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ the load of a machine is the sum of the processing times of its jobs and the makespan is the largest load. Times $t=(5,3,4,2,6)$ on $m=3$ machines, with jobs $\{1,2\}$, $\{3,4\}$, $\{5\}$. What is the makespan?
  1. $20$
  2. $7$
  3. $6$
  4. $8$
Explanation. The loads are $5+3=8$, $4+2=6$ and $6$: the makespan is $8$. $20$ is the total load, $7=\lceil 20/3\rceil$ is the average lower bound, not the makespan of this schedule.
oc-f-207 · level teorema · notes
In scheduling $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ ($m=2$ identical machines) the processing times are $t=(4,4,3,3,2)$. The schedule $\{4,4\}$, $\{3,3,2\}$ has makespan $8$. What can be concluded with the lower bound $\max\{\max_j t_j,\lceil\sum_j t_j/m\rceil\}$?
  1. The bound is $8$, but a bound never proves optimality
  2. The bound is $16$, hence the schedule is infeasible
  3. The bound is $8$, hence the schedule is optimal
  4. The bound is $4$, hence the schedule can be improved
Explanation. $\sum_j t_j=16$ and $\lceil 16/2\rceil=8\ge\max_j t_j=4$: the bound is $8$. A schedule whose makespan equals a lower bound is optimal.
oc-f-208 · level facile · notes
In scheduling $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ with $m=2$ machines, every job is assigned, in the given order, to the least loaded machine (ties: the one with smaller index). Processing times in order $t=(3,5,2,4,6)$. Which makespan is obtained?
  1. $12$
  2. $11$
  3. $10$
  4. $9$
Explanation. Loads: $(3,0)\to(3,5)\to(5,5)\to(9,5)\to(9,11)$: makespan $11$. The schedule $\{6,4\}$, $\{5,3,2\}$ has makespan $10=\lceil 20/2\rceil$, so the rule does not give the optimum.
oc-f-209 · level facile · notes
Bin packing (BPP) asks for the minimum number of bins of capacity $C$; scheduling $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ asks for the minimum makespan with $m$ machines. How does the question «is there a schedule with makespan at most $C$?» read in bin packing terms?
  1. Do the items of weight $t_j$ fit into $m$ bins of capacity $C$?
  2. Do the items of weight $t_j$ fit into $C$ bins of capacity $m$?
  3. Is the weight bound $\lceil\sum_j t_j/C\rceil$ at most $C$?
  4. Is there a bin of capacity $C$ containing all the items?
Explanation. The machines are the bins, the makespan is the capacity: a schedule with makespan at most $C$ exists if and only if the jobs fit into $m$ bins of capacity $C$. In the BPP the capacity is fixed, in $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ the number of bins.
oc-f-210 · level teorema · notes
In scheduling $P\,||\,C_{\max}$, the lower bound $\mathrm{LB}=\max\{\max_j t_j,\lceil\sum_j t_j/m\rceil\}$ is always valid. With $m=2$ machines and three jobs of processing time $t=(2,2,2)$, what are $\mathrm{LB}$ and the optimal makespan $z(I)$?
  1. $\mathrm{LB}=3$ and $z(I)=3$: the bound is always attained
  2. $\mathrm{LB}=2$ and $z(I)=4$: the bound is the longest job
  3. $\mathrm{LB}=4$ and $z(I)=4$: the bound rounds up to a multiple of $t_j$
  4. $\mathrm{LB}=3$ and $z(I)=4$: the bound is not always attained
Explanation. $\lceil 6/2\rceil=3>2$, hence $\mathrm{LB}=3$; but jobs cannot be split, and one machine gets at least two jobs: $z(I)=4$. The bound is valid but not always attained.
oc-f-211 · level facile · notes
In parallel machine scheduling, what distinguishes $R\,||\,C_{\max}$ (unrelated machines) from $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ (identical machines)?
  1. In $R\,||\,C_{\max}$ the number of machines is fixed and not part of the input
  2. In $R\,||\,C_{\max}$ jobs can be interrupted and resumed on another machine
  3. In $R\,||\,C_{\max}$ the time $t_{ij}$ of job $j$ depends on machine $i$ arbitrarily
  4. In $R\,||\,C_{\max}$ the machines have speeds $s_i$ and job $j$ takes $t_j/s_i$
Explanation. $R$ means unrelated machines, with arbitrary times $t_{ij}$; the speeds $s_i$ are the case $Q$, and $P$ is the case $t_{ij}=t_j$ for every machine.

8. Lot sizing problems (12)

oc-f-028 · level facile · notes
Capacitated lot sizing (CLS): $5$ periods, production capacities $cap=(40,30,30,20,30)$, total demand $D=110$, setup costs $sc=(50,60,40,70,55)$ ($sc_t$ is paid if production occurs in period $t$). The combinatorial lower bound sums the $k$ smallest setup costs, where $k$ is the minimum number of periods whose capacities cover $D$. What is its value?
  1. $235$
  2. $275$
  3. $205$
  4. $145$
Explanation. The largest capacities give $40+30+30=100<110$ and $40+30+30+30=130\ge 110$: at least $k=4$ setups are needed. The $4$ smallest setup costs sum to $40+50+55+60=205$.
oc-f-029 · level facile · notes
In capacitated lot sizing (CLS) every period $t\in T$ has demand $d_t$, production capacity $cap_t$, setup cost $sc_t$ and final inventory $s_t$. What is uncapacitated lot sizing (ULS)?
  1. The CLS with a single period, i.e., $T=\{1\}$ and capacity $cap_1\ge d_1$
  2. The CLS in which the capacities are not binding, e.g., $cap_t\ge\sum_{u\in T}d_u$ for every $t$
  3. The CLS in which setups are free, i.e., $sc_t=0$ for every period $t\in T$
  4. The CLS in which inventory is forbidden, i.e., $s_t=0$ for every period $t\in T$
Explanation. In the ULS every period can produce as much as it wants; setup, variable and holding costs remain.
oc-f-030 · level facile · notes
In lot sizing (demand $d_t$, production $x_t$ and final inventory $s_t$ in every period $t$; the setup is paid when $x_t>0$), which statement is correct?
  1. Without capacities some optimal plan produces only with zero incoming inventory; with capacities it may not exist
  2. With or without capacities some optimal plan produces only with zero incoming inventory
  3. Without capacities every optimal plan produces in all periods with positive demand; with capacities not
  4. With or without capacities the production of a period can cover the demand of an earlier period
Explanation. The Wagner-Whitin property (zero-inventory ordering) holds without capacities; with capacities there are instances in which every optimal plan produces with positive inventory. Production never covers past demand.
oc-f-194 · level ipotesi · notes
In capacitated lot sizing (CLS: periods $t$ with demand $d_t$, capacity $cap_t$, setup cost $sc_t$ paid if we produce in $t$; zero initial stock), let $D=\sum_t d_t$. Why does every feasible plan have at least $k$ setups, with $k$ the minimum number of periods whose capacities cover $D$?
  1. We produce only in periods with a setup, at most $cap_t$ in each, and the total production must cover $D$
  2. Every period with positive demand needs a setup, and the periods with demand are at least $k$
  3. Setup costs are positive, so an optimal plan uses the $k$ cheapest setup periods
  4. The final stock must be zero, so we produce exactly $d_t$ in every period with a setup
Explanation. Without initial stock the total production is at least $D$, and the setup periods produce at most the sum of their capacities; hence at least $k$ setup periods are needed. The bound then adds the $k$ smallest setup costs.
oc-f-195 · level teorema · notes
In capacitated lot sizing (CLS), the combinatorial lower bound adds the $k$ smallest setup costs, where $k$ is the minimum number of periods whose capacities $cap_t$ cover the total demand $D$. How is $k$ computed?
  1. Sorting the capacities in non-decreasing order: $k$ is the smallest number of smallest capacities summing to at least $D$
  2. Sorting the setup costs in non-decreasing order and adding the capacities of those periods until they cover $D$
  3. As $\lceil D/\min_t cap_t\rceil$, the number of periods with the minimum capacity needed
  4. Sorting the capacities in non-increasing order: $k$ is the smallest number of largest capacities summing to at least $D$
Explanation. To get the minimum number of periods one takes the largest capacities. The bound is valid because every plan has at least $k$ setups, whose cost is at least the sum of the $k$ smallest costs, even if in different periods.
oc-f-196 · level facile · notes
In capacitated lot sizing (CLS) over $m$ periods, the combinatorial part of the decision is the choice of the setup periods (those in which we produce). With $m=6$ periods, how many choices are there, before checking the capacities?
  1. $36$
  2. $63$
  3. $64$
  4. $720$
Explanation. Every period has a setup or not: $2^6=64$ subsets. Only some of them are feasible, and each can carry many different production plans.
oc-f-197 · level facile · notes
Lot sizing over $3$ periods: demands $d=(3,2,4)$, setup cost $50$ in every period with production, cost $10$ per unit of stock at the end of a period, zero variable cost, non-binding capacities. What is the cost of the plan $x=(5,0,4)$, which produces in period $1$ also the demand of period $2$?
  1. $140$
  2. $120$
  3. $150$
  4. $100$
Explanation. Two setups ($100$) and a stock of $2$ units at the end of period $1$ ($20$): total $120$. The lot-for-lot plan $(3,2,4)$ costs $150$, with three setups and no stock.
oc-f-198 · level facile · notes
In capacitated lot sizing (CLS) the stock at the end of a period is $s_t=\sum_{u\le t}(x_u-d_u)$ (zero initial stock) and must be non-negative. With demands $d=(3,2,4)$ and capacity $5$ in every period, which plan $x$ is feasible?
  1. $x=(3,4,2)$
  2. $x=(2,3,4)$
  3. $x=(6,0,3)$
  4. $x=(3,2,3)$
Explanation. $(3,4,2)$ gives stocks $(0,2,0)$. $(2,3,4)$ has $s_1=-1$ (it produces late), $(6,0,3)$ exceeds the capacity $5$, $(3,2,3)$ leaves one unit of period $3$ uncovered.
oc-f-199 · level facile · notes
Lot sizing over $3$ periods with demands $d=(2,2,2)$, setup cost $sc_t=10$, variable cost $vc_t=1$ per unit produced and holding cost $hc_t=2$ per unit of stock at the end of a period. What is the cost of the plan $x=(4,0,2)$?
  1. $26$
  2. $34$
  3. $36$
  4. $30$
Explanation. Setups in periods $1$ and $3$: $20$; production of $6$ units: $6$; stocks $(2,0,0)$: $4$. Total $30$. Forgetting the stock gives $26$, charging a setup also in period $2$ gives $40$.
oc-f-200 · level facile · notes
In lot sizing with unsatisfied demand (LS-UD), part of the demand $d_{pt}$ of product $p$ in period $t$ may be left unsatisfied, at a penalty $cu_{pt}$ per unit. When is it convenient to leave a unit unsatisfied?
  1. When the penalty $cu_{pt}$ is higher than the cost of producing and holding that unit
  2. Only in the last period, because its demand creates no stock
  3. When the penalty $cu_{pt}$ is lower than the cost of producing and holding that unit
  4. Never: the demand must always be met, the penalty only measures the service level
Explanation. The model compares the penalty with the cost of serving the unit: if serving it costs more (setup, production, stock), paying $cu_{pt}$ is cheaper.
oc-f-201 · level facile · notes
In lot sizing with several products and setup times (MLS), the products $p\in P$ share the time $cap_t$ available in period $t$; a unit of $p$ takes $vt_{pt}$ units of time, and the setup of $p$ has a cost and a setup time. What changes with respect to the single-product CLS?
  1. The demands of the products add up and the problem becomes a single-product CLS
  2. Setups consume capacity: more setups in a period leave less time for production
  3. Setups have no cost: only the production time $vt_{pt}$ is paid
  4. Every product has its own capacity, so the products are independent problems
Explanation. With setup times the capacity of a period is shared by production and setups of the products: the products are not independent, and opening a setup also costs time.
oc-f-202 · level facile · notes
In lot sizing with overtime (LS-OT) normal working time costs $cw_t$ per unit up to $cap^{N}_t$ and overtime costs $co_t$ per unit up to $cap^{O}_t$, with $co_t>cw_t$. How much can be produced at most in period $t$, and when is overtime used?
  1. Up to $cap^{N}_t+cap^{O}_t$; overtime only after normal time is used up
  2. Up to $cap^{N}_t$; overtime replaces normal time when it is cheaper
  3. Up to $\max\{cap^{N}_t,cap^{O}_t\}$; only one of the two modes is chosen
  4. Without limit; overtime is paid only above the demand of the period
Explanation. The capacity is not fixed: it reaches $cap^{N}_t+cap^{O}_t$. Since overtime is more expensive, an optimal plan uses it only when normal time is exhausted.

Part: Formulations and their properties (91)

9. Formulations of knapsack problems (19)

oc-f-031 · level teorema · notes
In the knapsack problem (KP, weights $w_j\le c$) let $z(\mathrm{ILP}_{\mathrm{KP}})$ be the optimal value and $z(\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{KP}})$ that of the LP relaxation (with $0\le x_j\le 1$). Which statement on their ratio is correct?
  1. $z(\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{KP}})/z(\mathrm{ILP}_{\mathrm{KP}})\le \frac{3}{2}$ for every instance, and the value $\frac32$ is reached asymptotically
  2. $z(\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{KP}})/z(\mathrm{ILP}_{\mathrm{KP}})$ is unbounded: it grows with the number $n$ of items
  3. $z(\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{KP}})/z(\mathrm{ILP}_{\mathrm{KP}})\le 2$ for every instance, and the value $2$ is reached asymptotically for $c\to+\infty$
  4. $z(\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{KP}})/z(\mathrm{ILP}_{\mathrm{KP}})\le 2$ for every instance, and some instance has ratio exactly $2$
Explanation. Propositions 9.7 and 9.8: the ratio is at most $2$, and the family with $c=2\vartheta$ and two items of weight $\vartheta+1$ has ratio $\frac{2\vartheta}{\vartheta+1}\to 2$, without attaining it.
oc-f-032 · level ipotesi · notes
In the KP (weights $w_j\le c$), with the items sorted by non-increasing efficiency $p_j/w_j$, let $s$ be the split item (the first one that does not fit entirely) and $\mathit{rc}=c-\sum_{j<s}w_j$. Which facts are needed to prove $z(\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{KP}})\le 2\,z(\mathrm{ILP}_{\mathrm{KP}})$?
  1. Weak duality between $\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{KP}}$ and its dual; items sorted by non-decreasing weight; $w_s\le c$
  2. Items $1,\dots,s-1$ form a feasible solution; item $s$ alone is feasible ($w_s\le c$); $z(\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{KP}})\le\sum_{j<s}p_j+p_s$
  3. Items $1,\dots,s-1$ form an optimal solution; $\mathit{rc}<w_s$; $z(\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{KP}})\le\sum_{j<s}p_j+p_s$
  4. Profits are integer, hence $z(\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{KP}})$ is rounded down; item $s$ alone is feasible; $\mathit{rc}<w_s$
Explanation. The two lower bounds $\sum_{j<s}p_j\le z(\mathrm{ILP}_{\mathrm{KP}})$ and $p_s\le z(\mathrm{ILP}_{\mathrm{KP}})$, added, exceed $z(\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{KP}})=\sum_{j<s}p_j+p_s\,\mathit{rc}/w_s$. The integrality of the profits is needed for the Dantzig bound, not here.
oc-f-033 · level ipotesi · notes
In the KP (profits $p_j$, weights $w_j$, capacity $c$), with the items sorted by efficiency, let $s$ be the split item and $\mathit{rc}=c-\sum_{j<s}w_j$ the residual capacity. What is needed to prove that $\mathrm{UB}_{\mathrm{D}}=\sum_{j<s}p_j+\left\lfloor p_s\,\mathit{rc}/w_s\right\rfloor$ is an upper bound on the optimal value?
  1. The order by non-increasing efficiency, the formula of $z(\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{KP}})$ and integer profits
  2. The order by non-increasing efficiency, the formula of $z(\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{KP}})$ and integer weights
  3. The order by non-increasing profit, strong duality and integer profits
  4. The greedy solution, the item of maximum profit and integer profits
Explanation. $\mathrm{UB}_{\mathrm{D}}=\lfloor z(\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{KP}})\rfloor$: the formula of $z(\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{KP}})$ holds with the items sorted by efficiency, and rounding is allowed because $z(\mathrm{ILP}_{\mathrm{KP}})$ is an integer ($p_j\in\Z_{\ge1}$).
oc-f-034 · level facile · notes
Knapsack problem (KP) with capacity $c=9$; the item of maximum efficiency $p_j/w_j$ has $p_1=6$ and $w_1=2$. What is the relaxation $z(\mathrm{LP}\text{-}0_{\mathrm{KP}})$, which keeps the capacity constraint and only the constraints $x_j\ge 0$ (without $x_j\le 1$)?
  1. $6$
  2. $\frac{49}{3}$
  3. $54$
  4. $27$
Explanation. Without the constraints $x_j\le 1$ all the capacity goes to the most efficient item: $z=p_1\,c/w_1=6\cdot 9/2=27$. It is a much weaker bound than $z(\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{KP}})$.
oc-f-035 · level teorema · notes
In the KP let $D(\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{KP}})$ be the dual of the LP relaxation, with variable $\pi$ for the capacity constraint and $\mu_j$ for $x_j\le 1$. With the items sorted by non-increasing efficiency $p_j/w_j$ and $s$ the split item (the first one that does not fit entirely), which dual solution is optimal?
  1. $\tilde\pi=\frac{p_1}{w_1}$, $\tilde\mu_j=p_j-w_j\frac{p_1}{w_1}$ for $j<s$ and $\tilde\mu_j=0$ for $j\ge s$
  2. $\tilde\pi=0$, $\tilde\mu_j=p_j$ for $j<s$ and $\tilde\mu_j=0$ for $j\ge s$
  3. $\tilde\pi=\frac{p_s}{w_s}$, $\tilde\mu_j=p_j-w_j\frac{p_s}{w_s}$ for $j<s$ and $\tilde\mu_j=0$ for $j\ge s$
  4. $\tilde\pi=\frac{p_s}{w_s}$, $\tilde\mu_j=0$ for $j<s$ and $\tilde\mu_j=p_j-w_j\frac{p_s}{w_s}$ for $j\ge s$
Explanation. The capacity multiplier is the efficiency of the split item, and the items taken entirely have $\tilde\mu_j=p_j-w_j\,p_s/w_s\ge 0$ (Proposition 9.5). With $\tilde\pi=p_1/w_1$ the $\tilde\mu_j$ would be negative; with $\tilde\pi=0$ the dual constraints of items $j\ge s$ are violated.
oc-f-220 · level facile · notes
The $\mathrm{ILP}_{\mathrm{SSP}}$ model of subset sum maximizes $\sum_j w_jx_j$ subject to $\sum_j w_jx_j\le c$ and $x_j\in\{0,1\}$; the answer is yes if and only if the optimal value is $c$. With weights $w=(2,3,6,7,5,9)$ and $c=9$, what is $z(\mathrm{ILP}_{\mathrm{SSP}})$?
  1. $7$, the largest weight fitting alone
  2. $9$, hence the answer is yes
  3. $8$, hence the answer is no
  4. $32$, the sum of all the weights
Explanation. For example $\{2,7\}$, $\{3,6\}$ or $\{9\}$ sum to $9=c$, hence $z(\mathrm{ILP}_{\mathrm{SSP}})=9$ and the answer is yes.
oc-f-221 · level facile · notes
In the $\mathrm{ILP}_{\mathrm{SSP}}$ model of subset sum (the KP with profits $p_j=w_j$, capacity $c$, and $\sum_j w_j>c$), why does the LP relaxation, with $0\le x_j\le 1$, not help to answer?
  1. All items have efficiency $1$, so the relaxation always equals $c$
  2. The relaxation is unbounded, because profits equal weights
  3. The relaxation always equals $0$, because the split item is the first one
  4. The relaxation always equals $\sum_j w_j$, because no constraint is active
Explanation. With $p_j/w_j=1$ for every item, the relaxation fills the knapsack fractionally and equals $c$: the bound $c$ does not distinguish yes-instances from no-instances.
oc-f-222 · level facile · notes
Bounded knapsack (BKP): the model has integer variables $x_j\in\{0,\dots,b_j\}$ (copies of item $j$). Capacity $c=9$, profits $p=(6,5,8,9,6,7)$, weights $w=(2,3,6,7,5,9)$, copies $b=(2,2,1,1,1,1)$. What is the optimal value?
  1. $15$
  2. $24$
  3. $17$
  4. $18$
Explanation. Two copies of item $1$ and item $5$ weigh $9$ and are worth $12+6=18$. With one copy per item (KP) one gets $15$, with unlimited copies (UKP) $24$ (four copies of item $1$).
oc-f-223 · level facile · notes
A bounded knapsack (BKP) with available copies $b=(2,2,1,1,1,1)$ can be rewritten as a 0-1 knapsack (KP) by replacing every item $j$ with $b_j$ identical items. How many binary variables does the resulting model have?
  1. $12$
  2. $64$
  3. $8$
  4. $6$
Explanation. The copies are $\sum_j b_j=2+2+1+1+1+1=8$. The $\mathrm{ILP}_{\mathrm{BKP}}$ model uses $6$ integer variables instead, one per item type.
oc-f-224 · level facile · notes
Unbounded knapsack (UKP): variables $x_j\in\Z_{\ge0}$, capacity $c=9$, profits $p=(6,5,8,9,6,7)$, weights $w=(2,3,6,7,5,9)$; item $1$ has the largest efficiency $p_j/w_j$, namely $3$. What are the optimal value and the LP relaxation $c\,p_1/w_1$?
  1. $18$ and $27$
  2. $24$ and $27$
  3. $27$ and $27$
  4. $24$ and $24$
Explanation. Four copies of item $1$ weigh $8$ and are worth $24$, and no combination does better; the relaxation uses $4.5$ copies and is worth $27$. $18$ is the value of the BKP with $b_1=2$.
oc-f-225 · level facile · notes
In the $\mathrm{ILP}_{\mathrm{UKP}}$ model of the unbounded knapsack ($x_j\in\Z_{\ge0}$ copies of item $j$, weights $w_j$, capacity $c$) there is no explicit upper bound on the variables. Which bound is implied anyway by the capacity constraint?
  1. $x_j\le\lfloor c/w_j\rfloor$
  2. $x_j\le 1$
  3. $x_j\le\lceil c/w_j\rceil$
  4. $x_j\le c$
Explanation. From $w_jx_j\le\sum_i w_ix_i\le c$ and the integrality of $x_j$ we get $x_j\le\lfloor c/w_j\rfloor$: the UKP is a BKP with $b_j=\lfloor c/w_j\rfloor$.
oc-f-226 · level facile · notes
Multidimensional knapsack (MDKP) with capacities $c_1=10$, $c_2=12$: profits $p=(6,7,5,9)$, weights in dimension 1 $w_{1\cdot}=(4,5,3,7)$ and in dimension 2 $w_{2\cdot}=(6,4,5,3)$; every item is taken at most once. What is the optimal value?
  1. $13$
  2. $16$
  3. $21$
  4. $14$
Explanation. $\{3,4\}$ weighs $(10,8)$ and is worth $14$. $\{2,4\}$ would be worth $16$ but weighs $12>10$ in dimension 1; $21$ is obtained looking at dimension 2 only.
oc-f-227 · level facile · notes
In the multidimensional knapsack (MDKP) model, with one constraint $\sum_j w_{dj}x_j\le c_d$ per dimension $d$, all the constraints are added into a single one, $\sum_j(\sum_d w_{dj})x_j\le\sum_d c_d$. What does the optimal value of the resulting 0-1 knapsack give?
  1. The MDKP optimal value, because the sum of the constraints is equivalent to them
  2. A feasible MDKP solution, because it respects the total capacity
  3. An upper bound on the MDKP optimal value, because the new problem is a relaxation
  4. A lower bound on the MDKP optimal value, because the new problem is more constrained
Explanation. Every MDKP solution satisfies the sum of the constraints, so the new problem has more feasible solutions: its optimum is an upper bound. Its optimal solution may violate a single dimension.
oc-f-228 · level facile · notes
The $\mathrm{ILP}_{\mathrm{MKP}}$ model of the multiple knapsack has a binary variable $x_{jk}$ for every item $j$ and knapsack $k$, one capacity constraint per knapsack and one constraint $\sum_k x_{jk}\le 1$ per item. With $n=5$ items and $m=2$ knapsacks, how many variables and linear constraints does it have?
  1. $5$ variables and $2$ constraints
  2. $10$ variables and $7$ constraints
  3. $10$ variables and $10$ constraints
  4. $7$ variables and $10$ constraints
Explanation. The variables are $nm=10$; the constraints are $m=2$ capacity constraints plus $n=5$ assignment constraints, i.e., $7$ (not counting the variable domains).
oc-f-229 · level facile · notes
In the $\mathrm{ILP}_{\mathrm{MKP}}$ model of the multiple knapsack ($x_{jk}=1$ if item $j$ goes into knapsack $k$), why is the constraint of every item $\sum_k x_{jk}\le 1$ and not $\sum_k x_{jk}=1$?
  1. An item may stay out of all the knapsacks
  2. An item may be split among several knapsacks
  3. The equality constraint would make the model non-linear
  4. The constraint is needed only for empty knapsacks
Explanation. As in the KP, we choose the items to insert: with the equality every item would have to be inserted, and the instance might have no feasible solution.
oc-f-230 · level facile · notes
Multiple-choice knapsack (MCKP): capacity $c=15$, classes $\mathscr{J}_1=\{1,2\}$, $\mathscr{J}_2=\{3,4\}$, $\mathscr{J}_3=\{5,6\}$, profits $p=(6,8,5,7,4,9)$, weights $w=(4,6,5,8,3,7)$; exactly one item per class is chosen. What is the optimal value?
  1. $15$
  2. $24$
  3. $20$
  4. $17$
Explanation. $\{1,4,5\}$ weighs $15$ and is worth $17$, as $\{2,3,5\}$ (weight $14$). $24=8+7+9$ takes the best item of each class ignoring the capacity; $\{1,3,5\}$ is worth $15$.
oc-f-231 · level facile · notes
In the $\mathrm{ILP}_{\mathrm{MCKP}}$ model of the multiple-choice knapsack (classes $\mathscr{J}_1,\dots,\mathscr{J}_q$, $x_j=1$ if item $j$ is chosen) the class constraints are $\sum_{j\in\mathscr{J}_h}x_j=1$. What would change writing $\le 1$?
  1. Two items of the same class could be chosen
  2. The model would become non-linear
  3. A class could contribute nothing: another problem would be solved
  4. Nothing: in an optimum every class gives an item anyway, since profits are positive
Explanation. With $\le 1$ a class may stay empty, and if the knapsack is too small for all the classes the optimum leaves one empty: it is a different problem from the MCKP, which imposes exactly one item per class.
oc-f-232 · level facile · notes
In the $\mathrm{ILP}_{\mathrm{KPS}}$ model of the knapsack with setups, $x_j=1$ if item $j$ is chosen and $y_h=1$ if class $h$ is activated (with setup cost $f_h$ and setup weight $s_h$). Which pair (objective, capacity) is correct?
  1. $\max\sum_j (p_j-f_h)x_j$ with $\sum_j (w_j+s_h)x_j\le c$
  2. $\max\sum_j p_jx_j-\sum_h f_hy_h$ with $\sum_j w_jx_j+\sum_h s_hy_h\le c$
  3. $\max\sum_j p_jx_j+\sum_h f_hy_h$ with $\sum_j w_jx_j\le c+\sum_h s_hy_h$
  4. $\max\sum_j p_jx_j-\sum_h f_hy_h$ with $\sum_j w_jx_j\le c$
Explanation. The setup of an activated class is paid once, in the profit ($-f_h$) and in the capacity ($+s_h$). The last option would pay it once for every chosen item.
oc-f-233 · level facile · notes
In the $\mathrm{ILP}_{\mathrm{KPS}}$ model of the knapsack with setups ($x_j$ item chosen, $y_h$ class activated, $h(j)$ the class of item $j$), what are the constraints $x_j\le y_{h(j)}$ for?
  1. To forbid choosing an item without paying the setup of its class
  2. To impose that all the items of an activated class are chosen
  3. To impose that at most one class is activated
  4. To forbid activating a class without choosing items from it
