Explanation. Without these constraints, since setups cost, the optimum would set $y_h=0$ and choose the items anyway. An activated but empty class is feasible, but not convenient.

10. Formulations of packing problems (12)

oc-f-036 · level teorema · notes
Bin packing ($n$ items of weight $w_j$, bins of capacity $c$): $\mathrm{ILP}_{\mathrm{BPP}}$ has variables $y_b$ (bin $b$ used) and $x_{jb}$ (item $j$ in bin $b$), and $\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{BPP}}$ is its LP relaxation. Which statement is correct?
  1. $z(\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{BPP}})=\sum_j w_j/c$ and $z(\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{BPP}})/z(\mathrm{ILP}_{\mathrm{BPP}})\ge\frac12$, with $\frac12$ reached asymptotically
  2. $z(\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{BPP}})=\sum_j w_j/c$ and $z(\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{BPP}})=z(\mathrm{ILP}_{\mathrm{BPP}})$ for every instance with integer weights
  3. $z(\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{BPP}})=\sum_j w_j/c$ and $z(\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{BPP}})/z(\mathrm{ILP}_{\mathrm{BPP}})\ge\frac{10}{17}$, with $\frac{10}{17}$ reached asymptotically
  4. $z(\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{BPP}})=n/2$ and $z(\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{BPP}})/z(\mathrm{ILP}_{\mathrm{BPP}})\ge\frac12$, with $\frac12$ reached asymptotically
Explanation. Corollary 10.5 and Propositions 10.6–10.7: the value of the relaxation is the total weight divided by the capacity, and it is at least half of the integer optimum; the ratio $\frac12$ is attained as $c\to+\infty$.
oc-f-037 · level ipotesi · notes
In bin packing (capacity $c$), the LP relaxation of the model $\mathrm{ILP}_{\mathrm{BPP}}$ has value $z(\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{BPP}})=\sum_j w_j/c$. Which key fact is used to prove $z(\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{BPP}})/z(\mathrm{ILP}_{\mathrm{BPP}})\ge\frac12$?
  1. In an optimal solution every item has weight not exceeding $c/2$
  2. In an optimal solution the bins are sorted by non-increasing total weight
  3. The dual of the LP relaxation always has an integer optimal solution
  4. In an optimal solution at most one bin has total weight not exceeding $c/2$
Explanation. If two bins weighed at most $c/2$ each, their contents would fit in one bin, against optimality: so almost all the bins are more than half full and $\sum_j w_j/c$ exceeds about $z/2$.
oc-f-038 · level facile · notes
The bin packing model $\mathrm{ILP}_{\mathrm{BPP}}$ with $m$ available bins has $y_b=1$ if bin $b$ is used, $x_{jb}=1$ if item $j$ goes into bin $b$, and minimizes $\sum_b y_b$. Which statement is correct?
  1. $m$ must be at least the number of bins of an optimal solution; the constraints force $y_b=0$ for every empty bin
  2. $m$ must be at least $n$; permuting the bins gives solutions with different objective values
  3. $m$ must be at least the number of bins of an optimal solution; an empty bin may have $y_b=1$, but not in an optimum
  4. $m$ may be the weight bound $\lceil\sum_j w_j/c\rceil$; an empty bin may have $y_b=1$, but not in an optimum
Explanation. With $m$ too small the model may have no feasible solution; an empty bin may have $y_b=1$, but not in an optimal solution. Permuting the bins gives equivalent solutions (symmetry).
oc-f-234 · level facile · notes
The $\mathrm{ILP}_{\mathrm{BPP}}$ model of bin packing with $n$ items and $m$ available bins has binary variables $y_b$ (bin used) and $x_{jb}$ (item $j$ in bin $b$), constraints $\sum_b x_{jb}=1$ and $\sum_j w_jx_{jb}\le c\,y_b$. With $n=5$ and $m=3$, how many variables and linear constraints does it have?
  1. $18$ variables and $15$ constraints
  2. $8$ variables and $18$ constraints
  3. $18$ variables and $8$ constraints
  4. $15$ variables and $8$ constraints
Explanation. The variables are $m+nm=3+15=18$; the constraints are $n=5$ assignment and $m=3$ capacity constraints, i.e., $8$.
oc-f-235 · level facile · notes
Bin packing with capacity $c=10$ and three items of weight $6$. The LP relaxation of the $\mathrm{ILP}_{\mathrm{BPP}}$ model equals $\sum_j w_j/c$. What are $z(\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{BPP}})$, its round-up and the optimal value?
  1. $\frac{18}{5}$, $4$ and $3$
  2. $\frac95$, $2$ and $3$
  3. $\frac95$, $2$ and $2$
  4. $3$, $3$ and $3$
Explanation. $\sum_j w_j/c=18/10=9/5$ and $\lceil 9/5\rceil=2$; but two items of weight $6$ do not fit together, so $3$ bins are needed.
oc-f-236 · level teorema · notes
In bin packing ($n$ items of weight $w_j$, capacity $c$, $m$ available bins), which solution is optimal for the LP relaxation of the $\mathrm{ILP}_{\mathrm{BPP}}$ model (assignment variables $x_{jb}$, usage variables $y_b$, objective $\min\sum_b y_b$)?
  1. $\tilde x_{jb}=\frac1m$ for all $j,b$ and $\tilde y_b=\frac{\sum_j w_j}{mc}$ for all $b$
  2. $\tilde x_{jb}=\frac1m$ for all $j,b$ and $\tilde y_b=1$ for all $b$
  3. $\tilde x_{jb}=\frac1n$ for all $j,b$ and $\tilde y_b=\frac{\sum_j w_j}{nc}$ for all $b$
  4. $\tilde x_{j1}=1$ for all $j$ and $\tilde y_1=\frac{\sum_j w_j}{c}$, the other $\tilde y_b=0$
Explanation. Every item is split equally among the $m$ bins, and every $y_b$ equals the fractional load divided by $c$; the sum is $\sum_j w_j/c$. The last option has $\tilde y_1>1$ if the total weight exceeds $c$, so it violates $y_b\le 1$.
oc-f-237 · level teorema · notes
In bin packing the LP relaxation of the $\mathrm{ILP}_{\mathrm{BPP}}$ model equals $\sum_j w_j/c$. Which family of instances shows that the ratio $z(\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{BPP}})/z(\mathrm{ILP}_{\mathrm{BPP}})$ tends to $\frac12$ as $c\to+\infty$?
  1. Two items of weight $\frac c2$: the relaxation is $1$ and $2$ bins are needed
  2. One item of weight $c$: the relaxation is $1$ and $1$ bin is needed
  3. $c$ items of weight $1$: the relaxation is $1$ and $1$ bin is needed
  4. Two items of weight $\frac c2+1$: the relaxation is $\frac{c+2}{c}\to 1$ and $2$ bins are needed
Explanation. Two items of weight $\frac c2+1$ do not fit together, so $z(\mathrm{ILP})=2$, while $z(\mathrm{LP})=\frac{c+2}{c}$; the ratio $\frac{c+2}{2c}$ tends to $\frac12$. Two items of weight $\frac c2$ fit into one bin.
oc-f-238 · level teorema · notes
In bin packing, also the rounded bound $\lceil z(\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{BPP}})\rceil=\lceil\sum_j w_j/c\rceil$ has a ratio to the optimum tending to $\frac12$, as $n\to+\infty$. Which family of instances shows it?
  1. $n$ items of weight $1$: the bound is $\lceil n/c\rceil$ and $n$ bins are needed
  2. $n$ items of weight $c$: the bound is $n$ and $n$ bins are needed
  3. $n$ items of weight $\frac c2+1$: the bound is about $\frac n2$ and $n$ bins are needed
  4. $n$ items of weight $\frac c2$: the bound is $\frac n2$ and $\frac n2$ bins are needed
Explanation. Items heavier than $\frac c2$ go one per bin, so the optimum is $n$; their total weight divided by $c$ is a bit more than $\frac n2$, and the ratio tends to $\frac12$.
oc-f-239 · level facile · notes
In the LP relaxation of the $\mathrm{ILP}_{\mathrm{BPP}}$ bin packing model (constraints $\sum_b x_{jb}=1$ and $\sum_j w_jx_{jb}\le c\,y_b$, $0\le x,y\le 1$), why is the optimal value only $\sum_j w_j/c$?
  1. The constraint $\sum_b x_{jb}=1$ is relaxed into $\sum_b x_{jb}\ge 0$
  2. Fractional $y_b$ pay only for the occupied part of a bin, as if the items were divisible
  3. The $x_{jb}$ stay binary, but bins can be filled beyond their capacity
  4. The relaxation uses a single bin, of capacity $\sum_j w_j$
Explanation. With $y_b=\sum_j w_jx_{jb}/c$ the cost of a bin is proportional to its load: the relaxation measures only the total weight, and ignores that items cannot be split.
oc-f-240 · level facile · notes
In the $\mathrm{ILP}_{\mathrm{CSP}}$ model of cutting stock, item $j$ has demand $d_j$ and the integer variable $x_{jb}$ counts its copies in bin $b$. Which constraint guarantees the demand?
  1. $\sum_b x_{jb}=d_j$ for every item $j$
  2. $\sum_b x_{jb}=1$ for every item $j$
  3. $\sum_j x_{jb}=d_j$ for every bin $b$
  4. $x_{jb}\le d_j\,y_b$ for all $j,b$
Explanation. The copies of item $j$ distributed among the bins must be exactly $d_j$. With $d_j=1$ the constraint becomes the assignment constraint of bin packing.
oc-f-241 · level facile · notes
Cutting stock with $n=4$ items, demands $d=(2,1,3,1)$ and $m=3$ available bins. The $\mathrm{ILP}_{\mathrm{CSP}}$ model uses one integer variable $x_{jb}$ per item and bin; the bin packing model on the copies uses one binary variable per copy and bin. How many assignment variables does each have?
  1. $12$ integer and $12$ binary
  2. $7$ integer and $21$ binary
  3. $21$ integer and $12$ binary
  4. $12$ integer and $21$ binary
Explanation. $\mathrm{ILP}_{\mathrm{CSP}}$: $nm=12$; bin packing on the $\sum_j d_j=7$ copies: $7\cdot 3=21$. The copies of an item are identical, and a single integer variable counts them.
oc-f-242 · level facile · notes
In the $\mathrm{ILP}_{\mathrm{CSP}}$ model of cutting stock (items with demand $d_j$) the number $m$ of available bins must be fixed. Which value of $m$ is always valid?
  1. $m=\lceil\sum_j d_jw_j/c\rceil$, the weight bound
  2. $m=\max_j d_j$, the largest demand
  3. $m=\sum_j d_j$, or the number of bins of any feasible solution
  4. $m=n$, the number of distinct items, whatever the demands
Explanation. With one bin per copy every instance is solved, and the number of bins of a known solution is enough too. The weight bound is a lower bound: with $m$ smaller than the optimum the model has no solution.

11. Formulations of coloring problems (12)

oc-f-039 · level teorema · notes
The vertex coloring model $\mathrm{ILP}_{\mathrm{VCP}}$ has $x_{vc}=1$ if vertex $v$ takes color $c$, $y_c=1$ if color $c$ is used, and the constraints $x_{uc}+x_{vc}\le y_c$ for every edge $\{u,v\}$. What is its LP relaxation value $z(\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{VCP}})$ on a graph with at least one edge (at least $2$ available colors)?
  1. $\omega(G)$, the cardinality of a maximum clique
  2. $\lceil n/\alpha(G)\rceil$, with $\alpha(G)$ the maximum stable set size
  3. $2$, for every graph with at least one edge
  4. $\chi(G)$, the chromatic number of the graph
Explanation. Putting $\frac12$ on two colors for all the vertices is feasible and costs $2$; the dual solution on an edge $\{u,v\}$ certifies optimality. The bound is very weak: on the complete graph $\chi=n$.
oc-f-040 · level facile · notes
The vertex coloring model $\mathrm{ILP}_{\mathrm{VCP}}$ with $k$ available colors has the variables $x_{vc}$ (vertex $v$ takes color $c$) and $y_c$ (color $c$ is used). How many variables does it have for a graph with $n=5$ vertices and $k=5$?
  1. $35$
  2. $30$
  3. $25$
  4. $10$
Explanation. There are $k\cdot n=25$ variables $x_{vc}$ and $k=5$ variables $y_c$: $30$ in total. The value $k=n$ is always valid.
oc-f-041 · level facile · notes
In the vertex coloring model $\mathrm{ILP}_{\mathrm{VCP}}$ of $G$ (variables $x_{vc}$ and $y_c$ for $k$ available colors), what happens if $k$ is smaller than the chromatic number $\chi(G)$?
  1. The model has no feasible solution
  2. The model still finds a coloring with $\chi(G)$ colors
  3. The LP relaxation of the model becomes unbounded
  4. The optimal value becomes $k$, with two adjacent vertices of the same color
Explanation. With fewer than $\chi(G)$ colors no coloring is feasible: $k$ must be at least $\chi(G)$, and $k=n$ always works.
oc-f-243 · level facile · notes
The $\mathrm{ILP}_{\mathrm{VCP}}$ coloring model with $k$ colors has one constraint $\sum_c x_{vc}=1$ per vertex and one constraint $x_{uc}+x_{vc}\le y_c$ per edge and color. How many linear constraints does it have for a graph with $n=5$ vertices, $m=6$ edges and $k=3$?
  1. $18$
  2. $30$
  3. $23$
  4. $11$
Explanation. $n=5$ assignment constraints plus $mk=18$ edge constraints: $23$. The value $11=n+m$ forgets that edge constraints are written for every color.
oc-f-244 · level teorema · notes
In the LP relaxation of the $\mathrm{ILP}_{\mathrm{VCP}}$ coloring model (constraints $\sum_c x_{vc}=1$ and $x_{uc}+x_{vc}\le y_c$), why is the solution $x_{v1}=x_{v2}=\frac12$ for every vertex, $y_1=y_2=1$ and the rest zero feasible on every graph?
  1. Because the edge constraints are relaxed into $x_{uc}+x_{vc}\ge 0$
  2. For every edge and color $x_{uc}+x_{vc}\le\frac12+\frac12=1=y_c$, and every vertex has total color $1$
  3. Because the graph is bipartite, and two colors suffice also in the integer model
  4. Because every vertex has degree at most $2$ when the variables are fractional
Explanation. The two halves of color respect every edge constraint, whatever the graph; the value is $y_1+y_2=2$. This is why $z(\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{VCP}})=2$ on every graph with at least one edge.
oc-f-245 · level facile · notes
In vertex coloring, the LP relaxation of the $\mathrm{ILP}_{\mathrm{VCP}}$ model equals $2$ on every graph with at least one edge. What is the ratio $z(\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{VCP}})/z(\mathrm{ILP}_{\mathrm{VCP}})$ on the complete graph $K_{10}$?
  1. $\frac15$
  2. $\frac12$
  3. $1$
  4. $\frac1{10}$
Explanation. $\chi(K_{10})=10$ and the relaxation equals $2$: the ratio is $\frac{2}{10}=\frac15$. On large complete graphs the ratio tends to $0$: the bound is arbitrarily weak.
oc-f-246 · level teorema · notes
For the $\mathrm{ILP}_{\mathrm{VCP}}$ coloring model two relaxations are compared: $\mathrm{LP}\text{-}0_{\mathrm{VCP}}$, with only $x,y\ge 0$, and $\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{VCP}}$, with also $x_{vc}\le 1$ and $y_c\le 1$. Which statement is correct, for a graph with at least one edge?
  1. $\mathrm{LP}\text{-}0_{\mathrm{VCP}}$ equals $1$ and $\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{VCP}}$ equals $2$
  2. $\mathrm{LP}\text{-}0_{\mathrm{VCP}}$ is unbounded and $\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{VCP}}$ equals $2$
  3. $\mathrm{LP}\text{-}0_{\mathrm{VCP}}$ equals $2$ and $\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{VCP}}$ equals $\omega(G)$
  4. Both equal $2$: the upper bounds do not strengthen the relaxation
Explanation. The same solution with two halves of color is optimal for both, with value $2$ (Corollaries 11.2 and 11.5): adding the upper bounds does not change the bound.
oc-f-247 · level facile · notes
In the $\mathrm{ILP}_{\mathrm{VCP}}$ coloring model with $k$ available colors (variables $x_{vc}$ and $y_c$), an optimal coloring with $q$ colors corresponds to how many optimal model solutions, if $k=q$?
  1. $2^q$, one per subset of colors
  2. $q^n$, one per assignment of the colors
  3. $q!$, one per permutation of the colors
  4. $1$, because the colors are numbered
Explanation. Renaming the colors gives another model solution with the same value: $q!$ equivalent solutions. This symmetry, as in bin packing, makes the model hard for branch-and-bound.
oc-f-248 · level facile · notes
In the $\mathrm{ILP}_{\mathrm{VCP}}$ coloring model the number $k$ of available colors must be fixed. Which choice is always valid and does not weaken the model needlessly?
  1. Always $k=2$, because the LP relaxation equals $2$
  2. The number of colors of a heuristic coloring, for example of $DSATUR$
  3. The size of a clique found by hand, because it is a lower bound
  4. The stable set bound $\lceil n/\alpha(G)\rceil$
Explanation. We need $k\ge\chi(G)$: the number of colors of a feasible coloring works, and a small $k$ reduces variables and symmetry. A lower bound may be smaller than $\chi(G)$ and make the model infeasible.
oc-f-249 · level facile · notes
The $\mathrm{ILP}_{\mathrm{VCP}}$ coloring model and the $\mathrm{ILP}_{\mathrm{BPP}}$ bin packing model are assignment models. Which correspondence between the two is correct?
  1. Vertex ↔ item, color ↔ bin, edge constraints ↔ capacity constraints
  2. Vertex ↔ bin, color ↔ item, edge constraints ↔ assignment constraints
  3. Edge ↔ item, color ↔ bin, vertex constraints ↔ capacity constraints
  4. Vertex ↔ item, color ↔ bin, edge constraints ↔ assignment constraints
Explanation. A vertex receives a color as an item goes into a bin, and $y_c$ counts the colors as $y_b$ the bins; what can stay together is decided by the edges instead of the capacity.
oc-f-250 · level facile · notes
In the $\mathrm{ILP}_{\mathrm{VCP}}$ coloring model ($x_{vc}=1$ if vertex $v$ has color $c$, $y_c=1$ if color $c$ is used), what do the constraints $x_{uc}+x_{vc}\le y_c$, written for every edge $\{u,v\}$ and color $c$, forbid?
  1. Two non-adjacent vertices with the same color
  2. A vertex with two different colors
  3. A color used by more than two vertices
  4. Two adjacent vertices with the same color, and using a color with $y_c=0$
Explanation. With $y_c=1$ at most one endpoint of the edge has color $c$; with $y_c=0$ neither does. One color per vertex is imposed by the constraints $\sum_c x_{vc}=1$.
oc-f-286 · level facile · notes
In vertex coloring the LP relaxation of the $\mathrm{ILP}_{\mathrm{VCP}}$ model equals $2$ on every graph with at least one edge. On which graphs does this bound equal the chromatic number $\chi(G)$?
  1. On complete graphs, where $\chi(G)=n$
  2. On odd cycles, where $\chi(G)=3$
  3. On all planar graphs, where $\chi(G)\le 4$
  4. On bipartite graphs with at least one edge, for example even cycles
Explanation. The bound is exact only when $\chi(G)=2$, i.e., on bipartite graphs with at least one edge. Already on an odd cycle it is $2<3$, and on $K_n$ it is $2<n$.

12. Formulations of clique problems (12)

oc-f-042 · level teorema · notes
In the maximum clique problem (MCP) on $G$ with $n$ vertices, the edge formulation $\mathrm{ILP}_{\mathrm{MCP}}$ maximizes $\sum_v x_v$ with $x_u+x_v\le 1$ for the non-adjacent pairs; $\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{MCP}}$ is its LP relaxation and $\omega(G)$ the optimal value. Which statement holds for every graph?
  1. $z(\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{MCP}})\le 2\,\omega(G)$, hence the ratio is at most $2$
  2. $z(\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{MCP}})\ge n/2$, hence the ratio $z(\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{MCP}})/\omega(G)$ is unbounded
  3. $z(\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{MCP}})\le n/2$, hence the ratio $z(\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{MCP}})/\omega(G)$ is at most $n/4$
  4. $z(\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{MCP}})=\omega(G)$, hence the LP relaxation is always exact
Explanation. The point $x_v=\frac12$ satisfies all the constraints $x_u+x_v\le 1$: the bound is at least $n/2$ even when $\omega(G)=2$.
oc-f-043 · level facile · notes
In the maximum clique problem (MCP) on a graph $G$, the edge formulation uses $x_v=1$ if vertex $v$ is in the clique and constraints $x_u+x_v\le 1$. On which pairs of vertices are they written?
  1. On every pair of non-adjacent vertices of $G$, i.e., on every edge of $\overline{G}$
  2. On every pair of adjacent vertices of $G$, i.e., on every edge of $G$
  3. On every pair of distinct vertices, adjacent or not in $G$
  4. On every pair of vertices at distance exactly two in $G$
Explanation. Two non-adjacent vertices cannot belong to the same clique. Written on the edges of $G$, the same constraints would describe the stable sets of $G$.
oc-f-044 · level ipotesi · notes
In the maximum clique problem on $G$, let $\mathrm{LP}^{\mathrm{CL}}_{\mathrm{MCP}}$ be the LP relaxation of the formulation with the clique inequalities ($\sum_{v\in S}x_v\le 1$ for every maximal stable set $S$). What is used to prove $\omega(G)\le z(\mathrm{LP}^{\mathrm{CL}}_{\mathrm{MCP}})\le\chi(G)$?
  1. It is a relaxation of the problem; its dual is the LP relaxation of the edge formulation; strong duality
  2. The point $x_v=\frac12$ is feasible; the graph is perfect; strong duality
  3. The $DSATUR$ algorithm is optimal; $\omega(G)=\alpha(\overline{G})$; weak duality
  4. It is a relaxation of the problem; its dual is the LP relaxation of a coloring model on the stable sets; weak duality
Explanation. The dual has one variable per stable set: an optimal coloring is an integer feasible solution of value $\chi(G)$, and by weak duality $z(\mathrm{LP}^{\mathrm{CL}}_{\mathrm{MCP}})\le\chi(G)$.
oc-f-251 · level facile · notes
In the maximum clique problem, the LP relaxation $\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{MCP}}$ of the edge formulation ($x_u+x_v\le 1$ for every non-adjacent pair, $0\le x_v\le 1$) always admits the point $x_v=\frac12$. On a cycle with $n=10$ vertices (maximum clique $\omega=2$), which bound does this point give?
  1. $z(\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{MCP}})\ge 5$, against $\omega=2$
  2. $z(\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{MCP}})\ge 2$, equal to $\omega$
  3. $z(\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{MCP}})\ge 10$, against $\omega=2$
  4. $z(\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{MCP}})\ge\frac52$, against $\omega=2$
Explanation. The point $x_v=\frac12$ satisfies every constraint and is worth $\frac n2=5$. On large sparse graphs the relaxation stays at least $\frac n2$ while $\omega$ is small: the ratio is unbounded.
oc-f-252 · level teorema · notes
In the maximum clique problem on $G$ ($x_v=1$ if vertex $v$ is in the clique), let $Q$ be a stable set of $G$ (pairwise non-adjacent vertices). Why is the clique inequality $\sum_{v\in Q}x_v\le 1$ valid?
  1. A stable set contains at most one vertex of every maximum clique
  2. It is the sum of the constraints $x_u+x_v\le 1$ on the pairs of $Q$
  3. It holds only for perfect graphs, where $\omega(G)=\chi(G)$
  4. A clique contains at most one vertex of a stable set
Explanation. Two vertices of $Q$ are not adjacent, so they cannot be in the same clique. Adding the pair constraints gives $\sum_{v\in Q}x_v\le\frac{|Q|}2$, weaker for $|Q|\ge 3$.
oc-f-253 · level facile · notes
In the maximum clique problem, the formulation with clique inequalities has $\sum_{v\in S}x_v\le 1$ for every maximal stable set $S$ of $G$. On the cycle $C_5$ (maximum clique $2$, chromatic number $3$) the maximal stable sets are the $5$ pairs of non-adjacent vertices. What is the LP relaxation value?
  1. $3$
  2. $5$
  3. $\frac52$
  4. $2$
Explanation. The point $x_v=\frac12$ satisfies the $5$ inequalities and is worth $\frac52$; adding the $5$ inequalities (every vertex is in two pairs) gives $2\sum_v x_v\le 5$. Hence $2\le\frac52\le 3$.
oc-f-254 · level facile · notes
In the maximum clique problem, why does the formulation with one inequality $\sum_{v\in S}x_v\le 1$ per maximal stable set $S$ not need the constraints $x_u+x_v\le 1$ on non-adjacent pairs?
  1. Because the number of maximal stable sets is always smaller than the number of pairs
  2. Every non-adjacent pair lies in a maximal stable set, whose inequality implies that of the pair
  3. Because the pair constraints are already implied by the bounds $x_v\le 1$
  4. Because non-adjacent pairs are edges of the graph, and edges give no constraint
Explanation. A non-adjacent $\{u,v\}$ is a stable set, so it lies in a maximal one $S$, and $x_u+x_v\le\sum_{w\in S}x_w\le 1$ with $x\ge 0$. Maximal stable sets may instead be exponentially many.
oc-f-255 · level facile · notes
The edge formulation of the maximum clique writes $x_u+x_v\le 1$ for every pair of non-adjacent vertices of $G$. How many such constraints does it have for a graph with $n=6$ vertices and $m=9$ edges?
  1. $6$
  2. $9$
  3. $15$
  4. $36$
Explanation. The non-adjacent pairs are the edges of the complement: $\binom62-m=15-9=6$. The $9$ edges of $G$ give no constraint.
oc-f-256 · level facile · notes
For the maximum stable set problem (MSSP) on $G$, with $x_v=1$ if vertex $v$ is in the set, the same formulation as for the maximum clique is used, written on another graph. What are the constraints?
  1. $x_u+x_v\le 1$ for every non-adjacent pair of $G$
  2. $x_u+x_v\ge 1$ for every edge $\{u,v\}$ of $G$
  3. $x_u+x_v=1$ for every edge $\{u,v\}$ of $G$
  4. $x_u+x_v\le 1$ for every edge $\{u,v\}$ of $G$
Explanation. A stable set of $G$ is a clique of $\overline G$: the clique constraints on $\overline G$ are the non-adjacent pairs of $\overline G$, i.e., the edges of $G$. $x_u+x_v\ge 1$ would describe an edge cover.
oc-f-257 · level facile · notes
In the maximum clique problem, the dual of the LP relaxation with clique inequalities is the LP relaxation of a coloring model with one variable $y_Q$ per stable set $Q$. On a graph with $\omega(G)=4$ and $\chi(G)=5$, in which interval does the relaxation $z(\mathrm{LP}^{\mathrm{CL}}_{\mathrm{MCP}})$ lie?
  1. In $[5,+\infty)$
  2. It equals exactly $4$
  3. In $[4,5]$
  4. In $[\frac52,4]$
Explanation. The relaxation is an upper bound on $\omega(G)=4$, and by weak duality it is at most the value of every dual solution, in particular of a coloring with $\chi(G)=5$ colors.
oc-f-258 · level facile · notes
In the vertex-weighted maximum clique problem (VWMCP: weight $w_v>0$ for every vertex) the $\mathrm{ILP}^{\mathrm{VW}}_{\mathrm{MCP}}$ model is obtained from the edge formulation of the maximum clique. What changes?
  1. The constraints are written on the edges of $G$ instead of the non-adjacent pairs
  2. Only the objective function, which becomes $\max\sum_v w_vx_v$
  3. Only the constraints, which become $w_ux_u+w_vx_v\le 1$
  4. One variable per edge is added, with weight $w_u+w_v$
Explanation. The feasible solutions are still the cliques of $G$: only the weight of a vertex in the objective changes. With $w_v=1$ we get back the maximum clique model.
oc-f-259 · level facile · notes
In the $\mathrm{ILP}^{\mathrm{EW}}_{\mathrm{MCP}}$ model of the edge-weighted maximum clique (weights $r_{uv}>0$), the binary variable $y_{uv}$ is $1$ if both endpoints of the edge are in the clique, and $\sum r_{uv}y_{uv}$ is maximized. Why are the constraints $y_{uv}\le x_u$ and $y_{uv}\le x_v$ enough?
  1. With positive weights, in an optimum $y_{uv}$ takes the largest allowed value, $\min\{x_u,x_v\}$
  2. Because $y_{uv}=x_ux_v$ is already linear when the variables are binary
  3. Because the constraint $y_{uv}\ge x_u+x_v-1$ would make the model infeasible
  4. Because every edge already has a constraint $x_u+x_v\le 1$ in the model
Explanation. The two constraints impose $y_{uv}=1\Rightarrow x_u=x_v=1$; the opposite implication is not needed, since leaving $y_{uv}=0$ with both endpoints chosen is never optimal with $r_{uv}>0$.

13. Formulations of routing problems (12)

oc-f-045 · level facile · notes
In the MTZ (Miller-Tucker-Zemlin) model of the TSP with $n=6$ cities ($x_{ij}=1$ if arc $(i,j)$ is in the tour), city $1$ is the start and every other city $i$ has a position variable $u_i$. How many MTZ constraints link the $u_i$ to the arcs?
  1. $30$
  2. $26$
  3. $36$
  4. $20$
Explanation. The MTZ constraints are written for the ordered pairs of cities different from city $1$: $(n-1)(n-2)=5\cdot 4=20$. The DFJ model would have $2^{n-1}-n=26$ subtour elimination constraints.
oc-f-046 · level teorema · notes
For the TSP ($x_{ij}=1$ if arc $(i,j)$ is in the tour), the MTZ model eliminates subtours with position variables and $O(n^2)$ constraints, the DFJ model with one constraint for every proper subset of cities. Which statement is correct?
  1. Both are correct, and the two LP relaxations always have the same value
  2. Only DFJ is correct: MTZ admits integer solutions containing subtours
  3. Both are correct, and the LP relaxation of DFJ is stronger than that of MTZ
  4. Both are correct, and the LP relaxation of MTZ is stronger than that of DFJ
Explanation. Propositions 13.1 and 13.2: both models exclude the subtours. MTZ has few constraints but a weak relaxation (Proposition 13.3); DFJ has exponentially many constraints, generated when needed, and a stronger relaxation.
oc-f-047 · level facile · notes
In the DFJ model of the TSP ($x_{ij}=1$ if arc $(i,j)$ is in the tour, $\mathscr{V}$ set of cities) the subtour elimination constraints are $\sum_{i,j\in S}x_{ij}\le |S|-1$. Why are they not written for $S=\mathscr{V}$?
  1. City $1$ has no position variable and must be excluded from $S$
  2. A tour uses $|\mathscr{V}|$ arcs with endpoints in $\mathscr{V}$: the constraint would exclude every tour
  3. The constraint would be redundant: it is already implied by the degree constraints
  4. The constraint is already guaranteed by the MTZ constraints on the positions $u_i$
Explanation. The sets $S$ are proper subsets with at least two cities: for $S=\mathscr{V}$ the constraint would cut off all the feasible solutions.
oc-f-260 · level facile · notes
In the DFJ model of the TSP with $n$ cities, the subtour elimination constraints $\sum_{i,j\in S}x_{ij}\le|S|-1$ are written for the subsets $S$ excluding city $1$, with $|S|\ge 2$. How many are there for $n=5$?
  1. $11$
  2. $16$
  3. $26$
  4. $12$
Explanation. The subsets of $\{2,\dots,5\}$ with at least two cities are $2^{4}-1-4=11=2^{n-1}-n$. $16=2^4$ also counts the empty set and the singletons.
oc-f-261 · level facile · notes
In the MTZ model of the TSP, $u_i$ is the position of city $i\ne 1$ after leaving city $1$, and the constraint $u_i-u_j+(n-1)x_{ij}\le n-2$ is written for every pair of cities different from $1$. What does it impose when arc $(i,j)$ is in the tour ($x_{ij}=1$)?
  1. $u_j\le u_i-1$: city $j$ comes before city $i$
  2. $u_j=n-1$: city $j$ is the last one
  3. Nothing: the constraint is always satisfied
  4. $u_j\ge u_i+1$: city $j$ comes after city $i$
Explanation. With $x_{ij}=1$ the constraint becomes $u_i-u_j\le -1$. A subtour not through city $1$ would need strictly increasing positions along a cycle, which is impossible.
oc-f-262 · level facile · notes
In the MTZ model of the TSP ($1\le u_i\le n-1$ positions of the cities $i\ne 1$), why does the constraint $u_i-u_j+(n-1)x_{ij}\le n-2$ exclude nothing when $x_{ij}=0$?
  1. It becomes $u_i=u_j$, true because unvisited cities share a position
  2. It becomes $(n-1)\le n-2$, true for every $n$
  3. It becomes $u_i-u_j\le n-2$, always true because positions lie in $[1,n-1]$
  4. It becomes $u_i-u_j\le 0$, true because positions are increasing
Explanation. The difference of two positions in $[1,n-1]$ is at most $n-2$: the coefficient $n-1$ is the smallest big-M that excludes no tour.
oc-f-263 · level teorema · notes
In the LP relaxation of the MTZ model of the TSP, setting $u_i=1$ for every city $i\ne1$, the MTZ constraints become $(n-1)x_{ij}\le n-2$. What follows?
  1. The relaxation coincides with that of the DFJ model
  2. Every $x$ satisfying the degree constraints with $x_{ij}\le\frac{n-2}{n-1}$ is feasible: the relaxation is weak
  3. Every feasible $x$ has $x_{ij}\in\{0,1\}$: the relaxation is exact
  4. The relaxation becomes infeasible for $n\ge 4$
Explanation. With all positions equal the MTZ constraints only bound the $x_{ij}$ by $\frac{n-2}{n-1}$, a value close to $1$: almost all the solutions of the assignment relaxation remain feasible.
oc-f-264 · level facile · notes
In the TSP ($x_{ij}=1$ if arc $(i,j)$ is in the tour), the assignment relaxation keeps only the degree constraints (one arc leaves and one enters every city) with $x_{ij}\ge 0$. What can an integer solution of it contain?
  1. Several disjoint subtours covering all the cities
  2. Only one tour through all the cities
  3. Cities with two outgoing arcs
  4. Arcs from a city to itself
Explanation. The degree constraints describe an assignment: every city has a successor, and the integer solutions are sets of disjoint cycles. The MTZ or DFJ constraints are needed to remove subtours.
oc-f-265 · level facile · notes
For the TSP the MTZ model has $O(n^2)$ constraints and the DFJ model an exponential number, with a stronger LP relaxation. How is the DFJ model used in practice?
  1. All the constraints are written at the start, since the solver handles them in polynomial time
  2. The constraints are replaced by the MTZ constraints, which have the same relaxation
  3. Only the constraints of the subsets with two cities are written
  4. Subtour elimination constraints are added only when the current relaxation solution violates them
Explanation. With $2^{n-1}-n$ constraints the model cannot be written in full: the relaxation is solved, violated subtours are searched and their constraints added.
oc-f-266 · level facile · notes
In the $\mathrm{ILP}_{\mathrm{ATSP}}$ model of the asymmetric TSP ($x_{ij}=1$ if arc $(i,j)$ is in the tour, distances $d_{ij}$ and $d_{ji}$ different) with $n=4$ cities, two subtours $1\to2\to1$ and $3\to4\to3$ satisfy the degree constraints. Which constraint excludes them?
  1. $x_{34}+x_{43}=2$
  2. $u_3-u_4+3x_{34}\le 2$ without position variables
  3. $x_{34}+x_{43}\le 1$, the elimination constraint of $S=\{3,4\}$
  4. $x_{12}+x_{21}\ge 1$
Explanation. The subtour $3\to4\to3$ uses $2=|S|$ arcs inside $S=\{3,4\}$, while the constraint allows at most $|S|-1=1$. The ATSP model is the DFJ model with the arc distances.
oc-f-267 · level facile · notes
The metric TSP has distances satisfying the triangle inequality $d_{ij}\le d_{ik}+d_{kj}$. How does the integer linear programming model change with respect to the general TSP?
  1. Continuous variables are used, because the relaxation is exact
  2. It does not: the triangle inequality is a property of the data, not a constraint
  3. A constraint $x_{ij}\le x_{ik}+x_{kj}$ is added for every triple of cities
  4. The subtour elimination constraints are dropped, useless on metric instances
Explanation. The metric TSP has the same MTZ and DFJ models: the metric concerns the distances $d_{ij}$ of the objective, and helps approximation algorithms, not the formulation.
oc-f-287 · level facile · notes
In the MTZ and DFJ models of the TSP there is a binary variable $x_{ij}$ for every arc $(i,j)$, i.e., for every ordered pair of distinct cities, and two degree constraints per city (one arc leaves, one enters). With $n=6$ cities, how many variables $x_{ij}$ and degree constraints are there?
  1. $30$ variables and $6$ constraints
  2. $30$ variables and $12$ constraints
  3. $15$ variables and $6$ constraints
  4. $36$ variables and $12$ constraints
Explanation. The ordered pairs of distinct cities are $n(n-1)=30$; the degree constraints are $2n=12$. The unordered pairs are $15$, but the models use directed arcs.

14. Formulations of scheduling problems (12)

oc-f-048 · level teorema · notes
Scheduling $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ ($n$ jobs of duration $t_j$, $m$ identical machines): the model $\mathrm{ILP}_{P\|C_{\max}}$ has variables $x_{ij}$ (job $j$ on machine $i$) and $C_{\max}\ge\sum_j t_jx_{ij}$ for every machine $i$. Which statement on its LP relaxation is correct?
  1. $z(\mathrm{LP}_{P\|C_{\max}})=\max_j t_j$, and the ratio to $z(\mathrm{ILP}_{P\|C_{\max}})$ is at least $\frac1m$, reached asymptotically
  2. $z(\mathrm{LP}_{P\|C_{\max}})=\sum_j t_j/m$, and it equals $z(\mathrm{ILP}_{P\|C_{\max}})$ for every instance with $m=2$
  3. $z(\mathrm{LP}_{P\|C_{\max}})=\sum_j t_j/m$, and the ratio to $z(\mathrm{ILP}_{P\|C_{\max}})$ is at least $\frac1m$, reached asymptotically
  4. $z(\mathrm{LP}_{P\|C_{\max}})=\sum_j t_j/m$, and the ratio to $z(\mathrm{ILP}_{P\|C_{\max}})$ is at least $\frac12$ for every $m$
Explanation. Propositions 14.1–14.3: assigning every job with value $\frac1m$ to every machine gives the average load; the bound ignores the longest job, and this is why $C_{\max}\ge t_j$ is added.
oc-f-049 · level ipotesi · notes
Scheduling $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ ($n$ jobs of duration $t_j$, $m$ identical machines), model $\mathrm{ILP}_{P\|C_{\max}}$ with assignment variables and LP relaxation $\mathrm{LP}_{P\|C_{\max}}$. Which facts suffice to prove $z(\mathrm{LP}_{P\|C_{\max}})/z(\mathrm{ILP}_{P\|C_{\max}})\ge\frac1m$?
  1. $z(\mathrm{LP}_{P\|C_{\max}})=\sum_j t_j/m$; $LPT$ is a $\left(\frac43-\frac1{3m}\right)$-approximation with respect to the relaxation
  2. $z(\mathrm{LP}_{P\|C_{\max}})=\sum_j t_j/m$; all jobs on one machine is feasible, hence $z(\mathrm{ILP}_{P\|C_{\max}})\le\sum_j t_j$
  3. $z(\mathrm{LP}_{P\|C_{\max}})=\sum_j t_j/m$; $z(\mathrm{ILP}_{P\|C_{\max}})\ge\max_j t_j$ and the makespan is integer
  4. $z(\mathrm{LP}_{P\|C_{\max}})=\max_j t_j$; all jobs on one machine is feasible, hence $z(\mathrm{ILP}_{P\|C_{\max}})\le\sum_j t_j$
Explanation. The ratio is at least $\frac{\sum_j t_j/m}{\sum_j t_j}=\frac1m$: only the value of the relaxation and a trivial upper bound on the integer optimum are needed.
oc-f-050 · level facile · notes
Scheduling $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ with $m=2$ identical machines and durations $t=(10,1,1,1)$. What are the LP relaxation value $z(\mathrm{LP}_{P\|C_{\max}})$ of the model with assignment variables $x_{ij}$ and makespan $C_{\max}$, and the optimal makespan?
  1. $\frac{13}{2}$ and $10$
  2. $\frac{13}{2}$ and $7$
  3. $10$ and $10$
  4. $13$ and $10$
Explanation. The relaxation is the average load $13/2$; the job of length $10$ forces $z(I)\ge 10$, which is attained. This is why the valid inequality $C_{\max}\ge t_j$ is added.
oc-f-268 · level facile · notes
The $\mathrm{ILP}_{P\|C_{\max}}$ model of scheduling on $m$ identical machines has binary variables $x_{jh}$ (job $j$ on machine $h$) and the continuous variable $C_{\max}$, with one assignment constraint per job and one load constraint per machine. With $n=6$ jobs and $m=3$, how many variables and constraints does it have?
  1. $19$ variables and $18$ constraints
  2. $9$ variables and $19$ constraints
  3. $19$ variables and $9$ constraints
  4. $18$ variables and $9$ constraints
Explanation. The variables are $nm+1=19$; the constraints are $n=6$ assignment and $m=3$ load constraints, i.e., $9$.
oc-f-269 · level facile · notes
In scheduling $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ the largest load $\max_h L_h$ is minimized, which is not a linear function. How does the $\mathrm{ILP}_{P\|C_{\max}}$ model (with $x_{jh}=1$ if job $j$ goes on machine $h$) linearize it?
  1. It minimizes $\sum_j t_jx_{j1}$, the load of the first machine
  2. It minimizes a variable $C_{\max}$ with $C_{\max}\ge\sum_j t_jx_{jh}$ for every machine $h$
  3. It minimizes $\sum_h\sum_j t_jx_{jh}$, the sum of the loads
  4. It minimizes a variable $C_{\max}$ with $C_{\max}\le\sum_j t_jx_{jh}$ for every machine $h$
Explanation. With $C_{\max}$ at least every load, the minimum of $C_{\max}$ is the largest load. The sum of the loads is constant, $\sum_j t_j$.
oc-f-270 · level teorema · notes
In the LP relaxation of the $\mathrm{ILP}_{P\|C_{\max}}$ model (jobs divisible among the $m$ identical machines, $x_{jh}\ge 0$), which solution is optimal and what is its value?
  1. $x_{jh}=\frac1m$ for all $j,h$, with value $\frac1m\sum_j t_j$
  2. $x_{j1}=1$ for all $j$, with value $\sum_j t_j$
  3. $x_{jh}=\frac1m$ for all $j,h$, with value $\max_j t_j$
  4. $x_{jh}=\frac1n$ for all $j,h$, with value $\frac1n\sum_j t_j$
Explanation. Splitting every job equally all machines have load $\frac1m\sum_j t_j$; no solution does better, because the loads sum to $\sum_j t_j$ and the maximum is at least the average.
oc-f-271 · level facile · notes
Scheduling $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ with $m=3$ identical machines and processing times $t=(9,2,2,2)$. The LP relaxation of the model with assignment variables equals $\frac13\sum_j t_j$. What are the relaxation and the optimal makespan?
  1. $5$ and $5$
  2. $9$ and $9$
  3. $15$ and $9$
  4. $5$ and $9$
Explanation. $\frac{15}{3}=5$; but the job of length $9$ cannot be split, so the makespan is at least $9$, and $\{9\},\{2,2\},\{2\}$ attains it.
oc-f-272 · level facile · notes
In scheduling $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ with $m$ machines, the LP relaxation of the model with assignment variables equals $\frac1m\sum_j t_j$. On which kind of instance is the ratio between the relaxation and the optimal makespan close to $\frac1m$?
  1. Exactly $m$ jobs of equal length
  2. Jobs of length $1$, in a number multiple of $m$
  3. One very long job and a few short ones: the optimum is dominated by the long job
  4. Many short equal jobs: the loads balance almost perfectly
Explanation. If a job of length $T$ dominates, the optimum is about $T$ and the relaxation about $\frac Tm$. With short equal jobs the relaxation is almost exact.
oc-f-273 · level teorema · notes
In the $\mathrm{ILP}_{P\|C_{\max}}$ scheduling model the inequalities $C_{\max}\ge t_j$ for every job $j$ and $C_{\max}\ge\lceil\sum_j t_j/m\rceil$ are added. Which statement is correct?
  1. They make every vertex of the LP relaxation integer
  2. They are valid for every integer schedule and strengthen the LP relaxation
  3. They are valid only for the optimal schedule, so they may cut feasible schedules
  4. They are already implied by the LP relaxation, so the bound does not change
Explanation. In an integer schedule job $j$ sits on one machine, whose load is at least $t_j$, and the maximum of integer loads is at least the rounded-up average. The LP relaxation instead can split jobs, and is worth only $\frac1m\sum_j t_j$.
oc-f-274 · level facile · notes
In the $\mathrm{ILP}_{P\|C_{\max}}$ model of scheduling on $m$ identical machines ($x_{jh}=1$ if job $j$ goes on machine $h$), how many integer solutions correspond to the same schedule, if the schedule uses all the $m$ machines?
  1. $m!$, one per permutation of the machines
  2. $1$, because the machines are numbered
  3. $2^m$, one per subset of machines
  4. $n!$, one per order of the jobs
Explanation. The machines are identical: swapping their names gives another model solution with the same makespan. This symmetry slows down branch-and-bound, as in bin packing.
oc-f-275 · level facile · notes
In scheduling $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ with $m=4$ machines, the ratio between the LP relaxation of the model with assignment variables and the optimal makespan is always at least $\frac1m$. If the relaxation equals $12$, what do we know about the optimal makespan $z(I)$?
  1. $z(I)=12$
  2. $3\le z(I)\le 12$
  3. $z(I)\ge 48$
  4. $12\le z(I)\le 48$
Explanation. The relaxation is a lower bound, so $z(I)\ge 12$; from the ratio at least $\frac14$ we get $z(I)\le 4\cdot 12=48$, i.e., the makespan of all jobs on one machine.
oc-f-276 · level facile · notes
The $\mathrm{ILP}_{P\|C_{\max}}$ scheduling model and the $\mathrm{ILP}_{\mathrm{BPP}}$ bin packing model are both assignment models. What distinguishes them?
  1. In bin packing the variables are continuous; in scheduling they are binary
  2. In bin packing the largest load is minimized; in scheduling the number of machines
  3. In bin packing the capacity is fixed and bins are counted; in scheduling the machines are fixed and the largest load is minimized
  4. In bin packing items can be split; in scheduling jobs cannot
Explanation. The same assignment constraints appear in both; bin packing minimizes $\sum_b y_b$ with capacity $c$, scheduling minimizes $C_{\max}$ with $m$ machines.

15. Formulations of lot sizing problems (12)

oc-f-051 · level facile · notes
Capacitated lot sizing (CLS) over $m$ periods: $x_t$ is the production, $y_t=1$ if the setup is paid in period $t$, $cap_t$ the capacity and $D_{t,m}=\sum_{u=t}^m d_u$ the residual demand. In the big-M formulation the link is $x_t\le M_t\,y_t$. Which value of $M_t$ is the strongest?
  1. $M_t=\min\{cap_t,\ D_{t,m}\}$
  2. $M_t=\max\{cap_t,\ D_{t,m}\}$
  3. $M_t=\min\{cap_t,\ D_{1,m}\}$
  4. $M_t=\min\{cap_t,\ d_t\}$
Explanation. The production of period $t$ exceeds neither the capacity nor the residual demand $D_{t,m}$: the smallest valid value gives the strongest relaxation. With $d_t$ feasible plans producing in advance would be cut off; $D_{1,m}\ge D_{t,m}$ is valid but weaker.
oc-f-052 · level teorema · notes
In the big-M formulation of capacitated lot sizing (CLS), the production $x_t$ and the setup variable $y_t\in\{0,1\}$ are linked by $x_t\le M_t\,y_t$. Which statement on the choice of $M_t$ is correct?
  1. Every valid value gives a correct model, and the larger $M_t$, the stronger the LP relaxation
  2. The value of $M_t$ changes the optimal value of the integer model, even among valid values
  3. A value smaller than the production of a feasible plan strengthens the model without excluding solutions
  4. Every valid value gives a correct model, and the smaller $M_t$, the stronger the LP relaxation
Explanation. In the relaxation $\tilde y_t=\tilde x_t/M_t$: a small $M_t$ makes the setups more expensive. A value that is too small, however, cuts off feasible plans, and one that is too large weakens the bound.
oc-f-053 · level facile · notes
For capacitated lot sizing (CLS) there are the big-M formulation (constraints $x_t\le M_t\,y_t$) and the shortest path formulation. What distinguishes the latter from the former?
  1. It describes the plans as paths in a network, without setup variables; the integer optimal value is lower
  2. It has fewer variables than the big-M one and the same LP relaxation; the integer optimal value is the same
  3. It describes the plans as paths in a network, without big-M; its relaxation is stronger, the integer optimal value is the same
  4. It describes the plans as paths in a network, without big-M; its relaxation is weaker, the integer optimal value is the same
Explanation. The two formulations have the same optimal value; they differ in the lower bound of the relaxation and in the size of the model.
oc-f-277 · level facile · notes
Capacitated lot sizing (CLS) over $4$ periods: demands $d=(20,10,30,15)$, capacity $cap_t=50$ in every period. In the big-M formulation, $x_t\le M_t\,y_t$ links production and setup, and the strong value is $M_t=\min\{cap_t,D_{t,4}\}$ with $D_{t,4}$ the demand of periods $t$ to $4$. What is $M_3$?
  1. $30$
  2. $75$
  3. $45$
  4. $50$
Explanation. $D_{3,4}=30+15=45<50$, hence $M_3=45$: an optimal plan does not produce more than the residual demand. $75=D_{1,4}$ is the total demand, valid but weaker.
oc-f-278 · level facile · notes
In the LP relaxation of the big-M lot sizing formulation ($x_t\le M_t\,y_t$, $0\le y_t\le 1$, setup cost $sc_t$ for $y_t$), a solution produces $x_t=10$ with $M_t=40$ and $sc_t=80$. How much setup does it pay in period $t$, at least?
  1. $320$
  2. $20$
  3. $80$
  4. $0$
Explanation. The constraint gives $y_t\ge x_t/M_t=\frac14$, and the relaxation chooses the minimum: $80\cdot\frac14=20$. This is why a large $M_t$ weakens the relaxation.
oc-f-279 · level facile · notes
In lot sizing models, with production $x_t$, demand $d_t$ and stock $s_t$ at the end of period $t$ ($s_0=0$), which constraint expresses the stock balance?
  1. $s_{t-1}+x_t=d_t+s_t$
  2. $s_t+x_t=d_t+s_{t-1}$
  3. $x_t=d_t$
  4. $s_{t-1}+d_t=x_t+s_t$
Explanation. Incoming stock plus production covers the demand of the period and the outgoing stock. With $s_t\ge 0$ production cannot serve earlier periods.
oc-f-280 · level facile · notes
In the shortest-path lot sizing formulation, the variable $zv_{tk}\ge 0$ is the fraction of the demand $D_{t,k}$ of periods $t,\dots,k$ produced in period $t$, and it is an arc from $t-1$ to $k$ in a network with nodes $0,\dots,m$. What does a path from node $0$ to node $m$ represent?
  1. A lot-for-lot plan, with a setup in every period
  2. Any plan, even with late production
  3. The sequence of periods without a setup
  4. A plan producing only with zero incoming stock, one run per arc
Explanation. Every arc $(t-1,k)$ produces in $t$ the demand of periods $t$ to $k$, and the path covers every period once. Plans that are not paths are combinations of paths with fractional $zv_{tk}$.
oc-f-281 · level facile · notes
In the shortest-path formulation of capacitated lot sizing, the link with the setup is $\sum_{k\ge t}zv_{tk}\le y_t$. What makes it stronger than the big-M link $x_t\le M_t\,y_t$?
  1. It uses continuous $y_t$ variables instead of binary ones
  2. It drops the capacities $cap_t$, which weaken the relaxation
  3. It has coefficient $1$ instead of a big-M: a fractional run pays a fraction of setup equal to the fraction of the run
  4. It imposes $y_t=1$ in every period with positive demand
Explanation. The sum of the $zv_{tk}$ leaving node $t-1$ is the fraction of runs produced in $t$, and the setup covers it fully; in the big-M link a small production pays only $x_t/M_t$.
oc-f-282 · level facile · notes
In uncapacitated lot sizing (ULS), with $D_{t,m}$ the demand of periods $t$ to the last one $m$, which value of $M_t$ is the strongest in the constraint $x_t\le M_t\,y_t$?
  1. $M_t=+\infty$
  2. $M_t=D_{t,m}$
  3. $M_t=D_{1,m}$
  4. $M_t=d_t$
Explanation. Without capacities the limit comes only from the residual demand: producing more than $D_{t,m}$ in period $t$ is useless. $M_t=d_t$ would exclude runs covering several periods.
oc-f-283 · level facile · notes
In lot sizing with several products and setup times (MLS), product $p$ takes $vt_{pt}$ units of time per unit produced and $st_{pt}$ units of time for the setup ($y_{pt}=1$). Which capacity constraint is correct in period $t$?
  1. $\sum_p(st_{pt}\,y_{pt}+vt_{pt}\,x_{pt})\le cap_t$
  2. $\sum_p vt_{pt}\,x_{pt}\le cap_t$
  3. $\sum_p x_{pt}\le cap_t\,y_{pt}$
  4. $\sum_p st_{pt}\,y_{pt}\le cap_t$
Explanation. The time of the period is consumed by production and setups. Forgetting $st_{pt}\,y_{pt}$ is a typical mistake: setups would look free in terms of time.
oc-f-284 · level facile · notes
In lot sizing with several products and setup times, the strong big-M value for product $p$ in period $t$ is $M_{pt}=\min\{\lfloor(cap_t-st_{pt})/vt_{pt}\rfloor,\ sd_{ptm}\}$, with $sd_{ptm}$ the residual demand of $p$. With $cap_t=10$, $st_{pt}=2$, $vt_{pt}=2$ and $sd_{ptm}=7$, what is it?
  1. $7$
  2. $10$
  3. $5$
  4. $4$
Explanation. After the setup $10-2=8$ time units remain, i.e., $\lfloor 8/2\rfloor=4$ units of product, less than the residual demand $7$. Forgetting the setup time would give $\lfloor 10/2\rfloor=5$.
oc-f-285 · level facile · notes
In the lot sizing model with overtime (LS-OT), $\mu_t$ is the normal time used (at most $cap^{N}_t$, cost $cw_t$) and $\nu_t$ the overtime (at most $cap^{O}_t$, cost $co_t$). Which constraint links working time to production and setups?
  1. $\mu_t+\nu_t\le\sum_p x_{pt}$
  2. $\sum_p(st_{pt}\,y_{pt}+vt_{pt}\,x_{pt})\le\mu_t\,\nu_t$
  3. $\sum_p(st_{pt}\,y_{pt}+vt_{pt}\,x_{pt})\le\mu_t+\nu_t$
  4. $\sum_p vt_{pt}\,x_{pt}\le cap^{N}_t$
Explanation. The capacity becomes a decision: the time used $\mu_t+\nu_t$ must cover production and setups, and its cost $cw_t\mu_t+co_t\nu_t$ enters the objective.

Part: Dynamic programming (156)

16. Principles of dynamic programming (12)

oc-f-054 · level facile · notes
Dynamic programming (DP) solves a problem by combining the optimal values of subproblems stored in a table. Which two properties must the problem have for DP to be correct and useful?
  1. Optimal substructure and independent subproblems
  2. Linear objective function and integer constraints
  3. Totally unimodular matrix and integer data
  4. Optimal substructure and overlapping subproblems
Explanation. Optimal substructure makes the recurrence correct; overlapping subproblems make the lookup table, which stores the values already computed, useful.
oc-f-055 · level facile · notes
In dynamic programming (DP), what happens if a problem has optimal substructure but every subproblem appears only once in the recursion?
  1. The recurrence becomes wrong and needs more base cases
  2. The table must be filled top-down, not bottom-up
  3. The table gives no advantage: the algorithm is a plain recursion
  4. The table makes the algorithm polynomial in the input size
Explanation. Without overlapping subproblems there is nothing to reuse. Without the optimal substructure, instead, the recurrence would be wrong.
oc-f-056 · level facile · notes
A dynamic programming (DP) algorithm fills a table with the optimal values of the subproblems. How is an optimal solution obtained, besides the optimal value?
  1. Re-running the recursion top-down, without any memorization
  2. Storing the decisions in another table and reading them backward from the final state
  3. Storing the decisions in another table and reading them forward from the initial state
  4. Reading only the table of values forward, from the initial to the final state
Explanation. The design has two steps: the optimal value (recurrence, base cases, bottom-up table) and an optimal solution (decisions stored and read backwards).
oc-f-288 · level facile · notes
A dynamic programming (DP) algorithm follows the «divide, conquer and combine» paradigm. What does the combining principle say?
  1. The best subproblem is chosen and the others are discarded
  2. An optimal solution of the problem is built from optimal solutions of the subproblems
  3. The problem is decomposed into smaller subproblems of the same type
  4. Small subproblems are solved directly, without recursion
Explanation. Dividing generates the subproblems, conquering solves them (recursively, or directly if small), combining merges their optimal solutions into an optimal solution of the problem.
oc-f-289 · level facile · notes
For the knapsack problem with $n$ items brute force lists the $2^n$ subsets. How does dynamic programming (DP) avoid this enumeration?
  1. It stores the optimal value of every subproblem in a table and reuses it when the subproblem appears again
  2. It discards a priori the subsets with low profit, as a greedy heuristic
  3. It solves the LP relaxation and rounds the solution
  4. It examines the subsets in random order and stops at the first feasible one
Explanation. The distinct subproblems are far fewer than the solutions (for the KP $(n+1)(c+1)$ against $2^n$): each is solved once and its value is read from the table (lookup table).
oc-f-290 · level facile · notes
In dynamic programming (DP) the subproblems generated by the decomposition are «independent». What does this mean?
  1. Each appears only once in the recursion
  2. They all have the same size
  3. Their optimal solutions have no element in common
  4. Each can be solved without information on the solution of the others
Explanation. Independent means that a subproblem is solved on its own; it may however appear several times in the recursion (overlapping subproblems), and this is exactly what makes the table useful.
oc-f-291 · level facile · notes
In dynamic programming (DP), what does the optimal substructure property of a recursive decomposition state?
  1. Every optimal solution of a subproblem is also optimal for the problem
  2. The subproblems repeat many times in the recursion
  3. An optimal solution of the problem is obtained by combining optimal solutions of its subproblems
  4. Every subproblem has a unique optimal solution
Explanation. Optimal substructure guarantees the correctness of the recurrence; the repetition of subproblems is the other property, overlapping subproblems, which makes DP efficient.
oc-f-292 · level facile · notes
What is the first step in the design of a dynamic programming (DP) algorithm?
  1. Compute an upper bound with a heuristic
  2. Identify the recursive decomposition into subproblems and check optimal substructure and overlapping subproblems
  3. Write the integer linear programming model and its relaxation
  4. Sort the data by non-increasing efficiency
Explanation. First the decomposition is defined and the two properties are checked; then the optimal value is computed with the recurrence and the base cases, and finally an optimal solution is rebuilt.
oc-f-293 · level facile · notes
In dynamic programming (DP) the optimal value can be computed top-down (recursion with a table) or bottom-up. What does the bottom-up approach require?
  1. An order of the subproblems in which each comes after those it depends on
  2. That the subproblems never repeat
  3. That the table is filled in random order
  4. That the recurrence has a single base case
Explanation. Bottom-up fills the table from the base cases, in an order (for example increasing capacities) that makes the cells read by the recurrence already available. Top-down follows the recursion instead and stores the computed values.
oc-f-294 · level facile · notes
Among the dynamic programming algorithms of the module, which one has a number of steps polynomial in the input size (and not only pseudo-polynomial)?
  1. The DP for the 0-1 knapsack, $\Theta(nc)$
  2. The DP for the bounded knapsack, $\Theta(nc\,b_{\max})$
  3. The DP for capacitated lot sizing, $O(mDC)$
  4. Wagner and Whitin for uncapacitated lot sizing, $\Theta(m^2)$ with $m$ periods
Explanation. $m$ is the number of periods, and the input contains $m$ demands: $\Theta(m^2)$ is polynomial. The other times contain numbers of the input (capacity $c$, bound $b_{\max}$, demand $D$), exponential in the number of digits.
oc-f-295 · level facile · notes
In dynamic programming for the multidimensional knapsack (MDKP) and the multiple knapsack (MKP), the state contains a vector of $m$ residual capacities. When is the number of steps pseudo-polynomial?
  1. Never, since the vector makes the problem strongly polynomial
  2. Only when all the capacities are equal
  3. Only for a fixed number $m$ of dimensions or knapsacks
  4. Always, since every capacity is a number of the input
Explanation. The table has one dimension per capacity, hence $\prod_d(c_d+1)$ states: with fixed $m$ it is pseudo-polynomial, with $m$ part of the input it grows exponentially in $m$.
oc-f-296 · level facile · notes
In a dynamic programming (DP) algorithm, what does the lookup table contain?
  1. The subproblems still to be solved, in priority order
  2. The optimal value of every subproblem already solved, read when the subproblem appears again
  3. The list of all feasible solutions of the problem
  4. The LP relaxation solutions of every subproblem
Explanation. The table avoids recomputing overlapping subproblems: every value is computed once. To rebuild a solution the decisions are also stored, in another table.

17. DP for the 0/1 knapsack problem (12)

oc-f-057 · level facile · notes
In dynamic programming for the knapsack problem (KP: weights $w_j$, capacity $c$), with a single vector $v[0..c]$ updated item by item, why are the capacities $k$ scanned from $c$ down to $0$ for every item $j$?
  1. This way the number of steps drops from $\Theta(nc)$ to $\Theta(c)$, since half the vector is skipped
  2. This way the cell $v[c]$, which contains the optimal value, is filled first
  3. Otherwise $v[k-w_j]$ would already contain item $j$, which could be chosen several times
  4. Otherwise $v[k-w_j]$ would not be computed yet, and the recurrence would read empty cells
Explanation. Scanning in increasing order would read a value already updated with item $j$, and would solve the unbounded knapsack. The number of steps remains $\Theta(nc)$.
oc-f-058 · level facile · notes
In the knapsack problem (KP, capacity $c$, $P=\sum_j p_j$), the second DP algorithm computes $u(j,q)$, the minimum weight of a subset of $\{1,\dots,j\}$ with total profit exactly $q$. Where is the optimal value read?
  1. $u(n,c)$
  2. $\max\{q\in\{0,\dots,P\}: u(n,q)\le c\}$
  3. $\min\{q\in\{0,\dots,P\}: u(n,q)\ge c\}$
  4. $\max\{u(n,q): q\in\{0,\dots,P\}\}$
Explanation. $u(n,q)$ is not a profit but a minimum weight: profit $q$ is attainable if the minimum weight does not exceed the capacity. This algorithm executes $\Theta(nP)$ steps.
oc-f-059 · level facile · notes
In the DP for the knapsack problem, $v(j,k)$ is the optimal value with items $1,\dots,j$ and capacity $k$. In the reconstruction of an optimal solution, starting from $(n,c)$, how are the indices updated when item $j$ is chosen?
  1. $j$ decreases by $1$ and $k$ decreases by $w_j$
  2. $j$ decreases by $1$ and $k$ stays the same
  3. $j$ stays the same and $k$ decreases by $w_j$
  4. $j$ decreases by $1$ and $k$ decreases by $p_j$
Explanation. At every step the item index decreases by one; the capacity decreases by $w_j$ only if item $j$ is selected (i.e., if $v(j,k)\ne v(j-1,k)$).
oc-f-297 · level facile · notes
In the first DP for the 0-1 knapsack (KP), $v(j,k)$ is the largest profit with items $1,\dots,j$ and capacity $k$, for $j=0,\dots,n$ and $k=0,\dots,c$. How many cells does the table have with $n=4$ items and capacity $c=9$?
  1. $40$
  2. $16$
  3. $50$
  4. $36$
Explanation. $(n+1)(c+1)=5\cdot 10=50$: rows start at $j=0$ and columns at $k=0$. $16=2^4$ counts the subsets of the items.
oc-f-298 · level facile · notes
In the list-based DP for the 0-1 knapsack, a state is a pair (weight, profit) of a subset, and $L_j$ keeps the non-dominated states with items $1,\dots,j$; $(w,p)$ dominates $(w',p')$ if $w\le w'$ and $p\ge p'$. With items $(w_1,p_1)=(2,6)$, $(w_2,p_2)=(3,5)$ and $L_0=\{(0,0)\}$, how many states does $L_2$ have?
  1. $5$
  2. $3$
  3. $4$
  4. $2$
Explanation. The subsets give $(0,0)$, $(2,6)$, $(3,5)$, $(5,11)$; $(3,5)$ is dominated by $(2,6)$, which weighs less and is worth more. $3$ states remain.
oc-f-299 · level facile · notes
In the list-based DP for the 0-1 knapsack (integer capacity $c$), the list $L_j$ contains only non-dominated (weight, profit) states of weight at most $c$. Why does it have at most $c+1$ states?
  1. Non-dominated states have pairwise different weights, and the possible integer weights are $0,1,\dots,c$
  2. Every item adds at most one state to the list
  3. Non-dominated states all have the same profit
  4. The list is emptied when it exceeds $c$ elements
Explanation. Two states with the same weight are comparable, and one dominates the other; hence in $L_j$ the weights are distinct and, sorted by increasing weight, the profits are increasing too. The number of states is therefore at most $\min\{2^j,c+1\}$.
oc-f-300 · level facile · notes
In the list-based DP for the 0-1 knapsack, why can a state $(w',p')$ dominated by $(w,p)$, i.e., with $w\le w'$ and $p\ge p'$, be discarded?
  1. Because $(w',p')$ is infeasible
  2. Because dominated states always weigh more than the capacity
  3. Because the list must have at most $n$ elements
  4. Every set of remaining items that can be added to $(w',p')$ can be added to $(w,p)$ too, with no smaller profit
Explanation. If $S'$ fits with $(w',p')$, it fits with $(w,p)$ too since $w\le w'$, and gives profit $p+p(S')\ge p'+p(S')$: no optimal solution is lost.
oc-f-301 · level facile · notes
In the second DP for the 0-1 knapsack, $u(j,q)$ is the minimum weight of a subset of items $1,\dots,j$ with profit exactly $q$, for $q=0,\dots,P$ with $P=\sum_j p_j$. How many cells does the table have with profits $p=(6,5,8,9)$?
  1. $50$
  2. $140$
  3. $145$
  4. $116$
Explanation. $P=28$, hence $(n+1)(P+1)=5\cdot 29=145$. The number of steps is $\Theta(nP)$: it pays off when the profits are small compared with the capacity.
oc-f-302 · level facile · notes
In the second DP for the 0-1 knapsack, $u(j,q)$ is the minimum weight of a subset of items $1,\dots,j$ with profit exactly $q$ ($+\infty$ if none exists). Items: $p=(3,2,4)$, $w=(4,3,5)$. What is $u(3,5)$?
  1. $8$
  2. $7$
  3. $+\infty$
  4. $5$
Explanation. The only subset with profit $5$ is $\{1,2\}$, of weight $4+3=7$. $\{1,3\}$ has profit $7$, not $5$; $5$ mixes up weight and profit.
oc-f-303 · level facile · notes
In the second DP for the 0-1 knapsack, $u(n,q)$ is the minimum weight to get profit exactly $q$. With capacity $c=8$ the last row, for $q=0,\dots,8$, is $(0,+\infty,3,4,+\infty,7,6,9,10)$. What is the optimal knapsack value?
  1. $6$
  2. $8$
  3. $5$
  4. $7$
Explanation. The optimal value is the largest $q$ with $u(n,q)\le c$: $u(n,8)=10$ and $u(n,7)=9$ exceed $8$, $u(n,6)=6\le 8$. Hence $6$.
oc-f-304 · level facile · notes
In the list implementation of the second DP for the 0-1 knapsack ((weight, profit) states, lists $L_j$ of non-dominated states), when is the capacity $c$ used?
  1. At every step: states heavier than $c$ are discarded at once
  2. At the start: items heavier than $c/2$ are discarded
  3. Never: the optimal value is the largest profit in $L_n$
  4. Only at the end: among the states of $L_n$ those of weight at most $c$ are taken
Explanation. As in the recurrence on $u(j,q)$, the capacity does not appear in the lists: $L_j$ collects the states of all subsets, even too heavy ones, and the check $w\le c$ is done on $L_n$.
oc-f-305 · level facile · notes
In the list-based DP for the 0-1 knapsack, the last list of non-dominated (weight, profit) states is $L_n=\{(0,0),(2,6),(5,11),(8,14),(9,15),(11,19)\}$ and the capacity is $c=9$. What is the optimal value?
  1. $14$
  2. $11$
  3. $15$
  4. $19$
Explanation. Among the states of weight at most $9$ the largest profit is $15$, of state $(9,15)$. $(11,19)$ exceeds the capacity.

18. DP for the unbounded knapsack problem (12)

oc-f-060 · level facile · notes
In the unbounded knapsack problem (UKP: $n$ item types, profit $p_j$, weight $w_j$, unlimited copies), the DP with table $v(j,k)$ (types $1,\dots,j$, capacity $k$) uses a recurrence with two terms. Which term allows using several copies of type $j$?
  1. $v(j-1,k)$, which excludes type $j$
  2. $p_j+v(j,k-w_j)$, which stays in row $j$
  3. $p_j+v(j-1,k-w_j)$, which moves to row $j-1$
  4. $p_j+v(j-1,k)$, which does not reduce the capacity
Explanation. Staying in row $j$ after inserting a copy allows inserting more; with row $j-1$ one would get the 0/1 KP.
oc-f-061 · level facile · notes
In the unbounded knapsack problem (UKP), $v(k)$ is the maximum profit with capacity $k$ and unlimited copies of every type. What is $v(k)$ if no item type fits into capacity $k\ge 1$?
  1. $0$: part of the capacity may stay unused
  2. $-\infty$: the capacity must be filled exactly
  3. $+\infty$: the subproblem is infeasible
  4. $v(k-1)$: the previous capacity is used
Explanation. The capacity need not be filled: if no type fits, the empty solution is feasible and is worth $0$. The value $+\infty$ is used in coin change, where the amount must be returned exactly.
oc-f-062 · level facile · notes
In the unbounded knapsack problem (UKP: $n$ item types, capacity $c$), the first DP algorithm fills a vector $v$ indexed by the residual capacity. How many cells does it have and how many steps does it execute?
  1. $c$ cells, $\Theta(c)$ steps
  2. $(n+1)(c+1)$ cells, $\Theta(nc)$ steps
  3. $c+1$ cells, $\Theta(n^2c)$ steps
  4. $c+1$ cells, $\Theta(nc)$ steps
Explanation. The optimal value depends only on the residual capacity, so a vector of $c+1$ cells (from $0$ to $c$) is enough; for each cell the $n$ types are tried: $\Theta(nc)$, pseudo-polynomial.
oc-f-306 · level facile · notes
In the unbounded knapsack (UKP) type $j$ can be used at most $\lfloor c/w_j\rfloor$ times. With weights $w=(2,3,4)$ and capacity $c=8$, how many vectors $(x_1,x_2,x_3)$ with $0\le x_j\le\lfloor c/w_j\rfloor$ are there, before checking the capacity?
  1. $27$
  2. $8$
  3. $60$
  4. $45$
Explanation. $\lfloor 8/2\rfloor+1=5$, $\lfloor 8/3\rfloor+1=3$, $\lfloor 8/4\rfloor+1=3$: $5\cdot3\cdot3=45$ candidate vectors, of which only some respect the capacity.
oc-f-307 · level facile · notes
Unbounded knapsack (UKP) with two types, $p=(3,5)$ and $w=(2,3)$, capacity $c=7$. With $v(0)=0$ and $v(k)=\max_{j:\,w_j\le k}\{p_j+v(k-w_j)\}$ ($0$ if no type fits), what is $v(7)$?
  1. $8$
  2. $12$
  3. $11$
  4. $10$
Explanation. $v=(0,0,3,5,6,8,10,11)$ for $k=0,\dots,7$: for example two copies of type $1$ and one of type $2$. The greedy by efficiency (two copies of type $2$) gives $10$, the 0-1 knapsack gives $8$.
oc-f-308 · level facile · notes
In the first DP for the unbounded knapsack (UKP), $v(k)$ is the largest profit with capacity $k$ and $s(k)$ the type attaining the maximum in the recurrence. How is an optimal solution rebuilt starting from $k=c$?
  1. Read $s(c)$: all the copies are of that type
  2. Take one copy of type $s(k)$ and set $k\leftarrow k-w_{s(k)}$, until no type fits
  3. Take type $s(k)$ and move to the next type, as in the KP
  4. Take $\lfloor k/w_{s(k)}\rfloor$ copies of $s(k)$ and stop
Explanation. Every step of the recurrence adds one copy of a type and reduces the capacity by its weight; the type may repeat in later steps.
oc-f-309 · level facile · notes
In the second DP for the unbounded knapsack (UKP), $v(j,k)=\max\{v(j-1,k),\ p_j+v(j,k-w_j)\}$ (the second term if $w_j\le k$). In which order are the cells computed bottom-up?
  1. Row by row (increasing $j$) and, in every row, by increasing $k$
  2. Row by row and, in every row, by decreasing $k$
  3. Column by column, by decreasing $j$
  4. In any order, because the cells are independent
Explanation. $v(j,k)$ reads $v(j-1,k)$ (previous row) and $v(j,k-w_j)$ (same row, smaller capacity): the latter must already be computed, so $k$ increases. This is what allows several copies of type $j$.
oc-f-310 · level facile · notes
Unbounded knapsack (UKP) with types $p=(3,5)$, $w=(2,3)$. In the second DP, $v(j,k)$ is the largest profit with types $1,\dots,j$ only and capacity $k$. What is $v(1,7)$?
  1. $11$
  2. $3$
  3. $10$
  4. $9$
Explanation. With type $1$ only, $\lfloor 7/2\rfloor=3$ copies fit: $v(1,7)=9$. $11=v(2,7)$ also uses type $2$; $3$ is the value of a single copy.
oc-f-311 · level facile · notes
For the unbounded knapsack (UKP) with $n$ types and capacity $c$, the first DP uses a vector $v(k)$ and the second a table $v(j,k)$. Which comparison is correct?
  1. The first takes $\Theta(nc)$ steps, the second $\Theta(n^2c)$
  2. They have the same number of cells, but the second is exponential
  3. The first has $c+1$ cells, the second $(n+1)(c+1)$; both take $\Theta(nc)$ steps
  4. The first takes $\Theta(c)$ steps, the second $\Theta(nc)$
Explanation. The first computes every $v(k)$ with a maximum over $n$ types; the second computes every cell with two terms. The total work is $\Theta(nc)$ in both cases.
oc-f-312 · level facile · notes
In the second DP for the unbounded knapsack (UKP), $v(j,k)$ uses types $1,\dots,j$ and capacity $k$. Why is the base case $v(0,k)=0$ for every $k$, and not $-\infty$ for $k>0$?
  1. Because $-\infty$ cannot be stored in a table
  2. The knapsack need not be filled: with no type available the empty solution is feasible and worth $0$
  3. Because profits are positive, so no value is negative
  4. Because the capacity $k$ is always zero when $j=0$
Explanation. In the UKP it is enough not to exceed the capacity. In rod cutting, where the whole rod must be divided, the same recurrence uses $v(0,\ell)=-\infty$ for $\ell>0$ instead.
oc-f-313 · level facile · notes
In the first DP for the unbounded knapsack (UKP), to compute $v(k)=\max_{j:\,w_j\le k}\{p_j+v(k-w_j)\}$, which already computed cells are read?
  1. The cells $v(k-w_j)$, one for every type $j$ with $w_j\le k$
  2. Only the cell $v(k-1)$
  3. All the cells $v(0),\dots,v(k-1)$
  4. The cells $v(k-w_j)$ of the previous row, one per type
Explanation. Every term corresponds to a first copy of type $j$ and reads the value of the residual capacity $k-w_j$; there are at most $n$ terms, hence $\Theta(nc)$ steps.
oc-f-314 · level facile · notes
In the unbounded knapsack (UKP) a type $i$ dominates a type $j$ if $w_i\le w_j$ and $p_i\ge p_j$. What can be done with the dominated type $j$?
  1. Nothing: with unlimited copies no type is dominated
  2. Use it only to fill the residual capacity
  3. Remove type $i$ instead, which has smaller weight
  4. Remove it: each of its copies can be replaced by a copy of $i$ without exceeding the capacity or losing profit
Explanation. Replacing a copy of $j$ by one of $i$ does not increase the weight and does not decrease the profit, so there is an optimal solution without type $j$.

19. DP for the bounded knapsack problem (12)

oc-f-063 · level facile · notes
In the bounded knapsack problem (BKP) type $j$ has $b_j$ available copies of weight $w_j$. In the recurrence, for type $j$ with $b_j=3$, $w_j=4$ and residual capacity $k=10$, how many values of the number of copies $d$ are tried?
  1. $3$ ($d=0,1,2$)
  2. $4$ ($d=0,1,2,3$)
  3. $2$ ($d=1,2$)
  4. $11$ ($d=0,\dots,10$)
Explanation. $d\in\{0,1,\dots,\min\{b_j,\lfloor k/w_j\rfloor\}\}=\{0,1,2\}$: both the bound on the copies and the capacity matter.
oc-f-064 · level facile · notes
In the bounded knapsack problem (BKP) type $j$ has $b_j$ available copies, $b_{\max}=\max_j b_j$, and the capacity is $c$. Which statement on the BKP and its DP is correct?
  1. It generalizes the KP ($b_j=1$) but not the UKP; the DP executes $\Theta(nc\,b_{\max})$ steps
  2. It generalizes the KP ($b_j=1$) and the UKP ($b_j=\lfloor c/w_j\rfloor$); the DP executes $\Theta(nc)$ steps
  3. It is a special case of the KP; the DP is polynomial because $b_{\max}\le n$
  4. It generalizes the KP ($b_j=1$) and the UKP ($b_j=\lfloor c/w_j\rfloor$); the DP executes $\Theta(nc\,b_{\max})$ steps
Explanation. With one copy per type we get the KP, with $\lfloor c/w_j\rfloor$ copies the UKP. The innermost loop tries up to $b_{\max}+1$ values, hence $\Theta(nc\,b_{\max})$, pseudo-polynomial.
oc-f-065 · level facile · notes
In the DP of the bounded knapsack problem (BKP), $s[j][k]$ is the number of copies of type $j$ (weight $w_j$) chosen in the state with types $1,\dots,j$ and capacity $k$. In the reconstruction, after reading $s[j][k]$, how are the indices updated?
  1. $k\leftarrow k-w_j$, and $j$ stays the same
  2. $k$ stays the same, and $j$ decreases by $s[j][k]$
  3. $k\leftarrow k-s[j][k]\,w_j$, and $j$ decreases by $1$
  4. $k\leftarrow k-s[j][k]$, and $j$ decreases by $1$
Explanation. The $s[j][k]$ chosen copies are removed, each of weight $w_j$; the type index always decreases by one.
oc-f-315 · level facile · notes
In the bounded knapsack (BKP) type $j$ has $b_j$ available copies. With $b=(2,2,1,1,1,1)$, how many vectors $(x_1,\dots,x_6)$ with $0\le x_j\le b_j$ are there, before checking the capacity?
  1. $144$
  2. $64$
  3. $8$
  4. $256$
Explanation. Every type has $b_j+1$ choices: $3\cdot3\cdot2^4=144$. $2^6=64$ are the choices with at most one copy per type.
oc-f-316 · level facile · notes
In the bounded knapsack (BKP: profits $p_j$, weights $w_j$, available copies $b_j$, capacity $c$), how is a feasible solution encoded?
  1. By a subset of the items of total weight at most $c$
  2. By an integer vector $x\ge 0$ with $\sum_j w_jx_j=c$
  3. By a binary vector $x$ with $\sum_j b_jw_jx_j\le c$
  4. By an integer vector $x$, $0\le x_j\le b_j$, with $\sum_j w_jx_j\le c$
Explanation. $x_j$ counts the copies of type $j$, bounded by $b_j$, and the total weight does not exceed the capacity. The subset is the KP encoding ($b_j=1$).
oc-f-317 · level facile · notes
Bounded knapsack (BKP) with two types: $p=(4,7)$, $w=(2,3)$, copies $b=(3,1)$, capacity $c=7$. With $v(j,k)=\max_{0\le d\le\min\{b_j,\lfloor k/w_j\rfloor\}}\{d\,p_j+v(j-1,k-d\,w_j)\}$ and $v(0,k)=0$, what is $v(2,7)$?
  1. $11$
  2. $16$
  3. $15$
  4. $12$
Explanation. $d=0$: $v(1,7)=3\cdot4=12$; $d=1$: $7+v(1,4)=7+8=15$. The maximum is $15$. With one copy per type (KP) one would get $11$.
oc-f-318 · level facile · notes
In the recursion tree of the DP for the bounded knapsack (BKP), every node is a call $v(j,k)$ and its children are the calls generated by the recurrence. How many children does the node $v(j,k)$ have at most?
  1. $n$, one for every item type
  2. $\min\{b_j,\lfloor k/w_j\rfloor\}+1$, one for every number of copies of type $j$
  3. $2$, as in the KP
  4. $b_j$, one for every available copy
Explanation. The values $d=0,1,\dots,\min\{b_j,\lfloor k/w_j\rfloor\}$ are tried, and each generates the call $v(j-1,k-d\,w_j)$. With $b_j=1$ we get the two children of the KP.
oc-f-319 · level facile · notes
In the DP for the bounded knapsack (BKP), $v(j,k)$ is the largest profit with types $1,\dots,j$ and capacity $k$. Which property does every row $v(j,\cdot)$ have?
  1. It is non-decreasing in $k$: with more capacity there are more feasible solutions
  2. It is strictly increasing in $k$
  3. It is constant: the profit does not depend on the capacity
  4. It decreases when $k$ exceeds $\sum_j b_jw_j$
Explanation. A feasible solution with capacity $k$ is feasible with capacity $k+1$ too, because the knapsack need not be filled; the value may however stay the same for several consecutive capacities.
oc-f-320 · level facile · notes
In the DP for the bounded knapsack (BKP), $v(j,k)$ is the largest profit with types $1,\dots,j$ and capacity $k$. What is the base case $v(0,k)$, for $k=0,\dots,c$?
  1. $-\infty$ for $k\ge 1$
  2. $k$ for every $k$
  3. $+\infty$ for $k\ge 1$
  4. $0$ for every $k$
Explanation. With no type available the only solution is the empty knapsack, feasible for every capacity and with profit $0$.
oc-f-321 · level facile · notes
A bounded knapsack (BKP) can be transformed into a 0-1 knapsack with $\sum_j b_j$ items, one per copy. How many steps does the KP DP take on the transformed instance, with capacity $c$?
  1. $\Theta(2^{\sum_j b_j})$, exponential
  2. $\Theta(c\,b_{\max})$, independent of $n$
  3. $\Theta(c\sum_j b_j)$, at most $\Theta(nc\,b_{\max})$ as the direct DP
  4. $\Theta(nc)$, independent of the copies
Explanation. The KP DP takes $\Theta(n'c)$ steps with $n'=\sum_j b_j\le n\,b_{\max}$ items: the same order, in the worst case, as the direct BKP DP.
oc-f-322 · level facile · notes
In the bounded knapsack (BKP: type $j$ with weight $w_j$ and $b_j$ copies, capacity $c$), when does the bound $b_j$ never constrain the solution?
  1. When $b_jw_j<c$
  2. When $b_j\ge\lfloor c/w_j\rfloor$: more copies would not fit anyway
  3. When $b_j=1$
  4. When $b_j\le\lfloor c/w_j\rfloor$
Explanation. With $b_j\ge\lfloor c/w_j\rfloor$ the capacity already imposes $x_j\le\lfloor c/w_j\rfloor$: if this holds for every type, the BKP is an unbounded knapsack.
oc-f-323 · level facile · notes
In the DP for the bounded knapsack (BKP) the recurrence tries $d=0,\dots,\min\{b_j,\lfloor k/w_j\rfloor\}$ copies of type $j$. What does it reduce to if $b_j=1$ for every type?
  1. To the 0-1 knapsack DP: $v(j,k)=\max\{v(j-1,k),\ p_j+v(j-1,k-w_j)\}$
  2. To the unbounded knapsack DP: $v(j,k)=\max\{v(j-1,k),\ p_j+v(j,k-w_j)\}$
  3. To the subset sum DP, with boolean values
  4. To a recurrence with a single term, $v(j-1,k)$
Explanation. With $b_j=1$ only the choices $d=0$ and $d=1$ remain, i.e., exclude or take item $j$, and the next row starts from $j-1$ in both cases.

20. DP for the subset sum problem (12)

oc-f-066 · level facile · notes
In subset sum (integers $w_1,\dots,w_n$, target $c$), the DP computes $r(j,k)$, true if some subset of $\{w_1,\dots,w_j\}$ sums to exactly $k$. What are the base cases?
  1. $r(j,0)=\textit{false}$ for every $j$, and $r(0,k)=\textit{true}$ for $k\ge 1$
  2. $r(0,k)=\textit{true}$ for every $k$
  3. $r(j,0)=\textit{false}$ and $r(0,k)=\textit{false}$
  4. $r(j,0)=\textit{true}$ for every $j$, and $r(0,k)=\textit{false}$ for $k\ge 1$
Explanation. The value $0$ is always obtained with the empty subset; without items no positive value is reachable. With $r(j,0)=\textit{false}$ no cell would ever become true.
oc-f-067 · level facile · notes
In subset sum (integers $w_1,\dots,w_n$, target $c$), $r(j,k)$ is true if some subset of $\{w_1,\dots,w_j\}$ sums to exactly $k$. Once the table is computed, where is the answer read?
  1. In any true cell of column $c$
  2. In the last true cell of row $n$
  3. In cell $r(n,c)$
  4. In cell $r(c,n)$
Explanation. The answer is $r(n,c)$; a true cell in another column only says that another value is reachable. If $r(n,c)$ is false there is nothing to reconstruct.
oc-f-068 · level facile · notes
In subset sum (integers $w_1,\dots,w_n$, target $c$: does some subset sum to exactly $c$?), how many steps does the DP with table $r(j,k)$ execute?
  1. $\Theta(n\log c)$
  2. $\Theta(nc)$
  3. $\Theta(c^2)$
  4. $\Theta(2^n)$
Explanation. The table has $(n+1)(c+1)$ cells, each computed in constant time: $\Theta(nc)$, a pseudo-polynomial time.
oc-f-324 · level facile · notes
Subset sum: weights $w=(3,5,7)$, target $c=10$. $r(j,k)$ is true if a subset of the first $j$ weights sums to exactly $k$. What is $r(3,10)$?
  1. False, because $10$ is not a weight
  2. True, for example $3+7$
  3. False: no subset sums to $10$
  4. True, because $10\le 3+5+7$
Explanation. The reachable sums are $0,3,5,7,8,10,12,15$: $10=3+7$. That $c$ is below the total sum is not enough: an exact sum is needed.
oc-f-325 · level facile · notes
Subset sum with weights $w=(2,2,2)$ and target $c=5$: $r(j,k)$ is true if a subset of the first $j$ weights sums to exactly $k$. What is $r(3,5)$?
  1. False: the reachable sums are $0,2,4,6$
  2. True: $5\le 6$
  3. True: $2+2+1$
  4. False: the table has no column $5$
Explanation. With all weights even every sum is even, so $5$ is not reachable. Column $5$ exists ($k=0,\dots,c$) and contains only false values.
