In a randomized construction for a minimization criterion, the restricted candidate list is $RCL=\{q: g(q)\le g^{\min}+\alpha(g^{\max}-g^{\min})\}$. The candidates have values $g=(10,12,15,18,30)$ and $\alpha=0.25$. How many candidates does the $RCL$ contain?
  1. $2$
  2. $1$
  3. $4$
  4. $3$
Explanation. The threshold is $10+0.25\cdot 20=15$, so the values $10$, $12$ and $15$ enter. The candidate is then drawn at random from the $RCL$.
oc-f-429 · level facile · notes
In the randomized construction with restricted candidate list $RCL=\{q: g(q)\le g^{\min}+\alpha(g^{\max}-g^{\min})\}$, what is obtained with $\alpha=0$ and with $\alpha=1$?
  1. In both cases the greedy, since the $RCL$ always contains the best
  2. With $\alpha=0$ the $RCL$ is empty; with $\alpha=1$ it contains only the best
  3. With $\alpha=0$ the greedy (with random ties); with $\alpha=1$ a fully random choice
  4. With $\alpha=0$ a random choice; with $\alpha=1$ the greedy
Explanation. With $\alpha=0$ only the candidates of value $g^{\min}$ remain; with $\alpha=1$ the threshold is $g^{\max}$ and all candidates are admitted. $\alpha$ balances quality and variety of the solutions.
oc-f-430 · level facile · notes
In regret insertion $REG_2$ for the TSP, $\Delta^1_q\le\Delta^2_q$ are the two smallest insertion costs of city $q$ into the edges of the subtour, and the regret is $reg_q=\Delta^2_q-\Delta^1_q$. City $a$ has $\Delta^1=3$, $\Delta^2=10$; city $b$ has $\Delta^1=2$, $\Delta^2=4$. Which city does $REG_2$ insert?
  1. City $a$, with insertion cost $3$
  2. City $a$, with regret $7$
  3. City $b$, with insertion cost $2$
  4. City $b$, with regret $2$
Explanation. $REG_2$ inserts the city of largest regret: $reg_a=7>reg_b=2$. Postponing $a$ risks losing its only convenient edge; cheapest insertion would choose $b$ instead.
oc-f-431 · level facile · notes
The pilot method turns a constructive heuristic $\mathscr{H}$ (the pilot) into a stronger one: at each step it tries every candidate, completes the partial solution with $\mathscr{H}$ and chooses the candidate with the best completion. Which statement is correct?
  1. It is never worse than the pilot, but runs the pilot once per candidate at each step
  2. It always finds the optimal solution, because it tries all candidates
  3. It may be worse than the pilot, because it changes the first choice
  4. It costs as much as the pilot, because it runs it only once
Explanation. At the first step the candidate chosen by the pilot is among those tried, and its completion is the pilot solution: the method finds at least as much, at a much larger cost.
oc-f-432 · level facile · notes
A constructive heuristic returns a feasible solution. What is needed, besides its value, to say how far the solution is from the optimum?
  1. A second run of the same heuristic
  2. The order in which the elements were chosen
  3. Nothing: the value of a constructive heuristic is always close to the optimum
  4. A dual bound, to compute the gap
Explanation. The value is a primal bound and gives only one side of the interval containing the optimum; with a dual bound (for example a relaxation) the gap follows.
oc-f-433 · level facile · notes
Constructive heuristics stop as soon as the solution is complete, and every choice made is final. How are they usually used in a larger heuristic algorithm?
  1. As a dual bound for branch-and-bound
  2. They are never combined with other methods
  3. As the starting point of a local search or a metaheuristic, which improve the complete solution
  4. As the final algorithm, after a local search
Explanation. They are fast but do not revise choices: a local search starts from their solution and corrects wrong decisions with moves in the neighbourhood.

31. Local search (22)

oc-f-102 · level teorema · notes
A local search $LS$ for a minimization problem with integer-valued objective $f$ and optimal value $z(I)$ starts from $x^0$ and always moves to a strictly better neighbor. Which statement is correct?
  1. It stops at a local optimum after exactly $f(x^0)-z(I)$ moves
  2. It may never stop, if the neighborhood used is not exact
  3. It stops at a local optimum after at most $f(x^0)-z(I)$ moves
  4. It stops at a global optimum after at most $f(x^0)-z(I)$ moves
Explanation. Every move improves $f$ by at least $1$ and $f$ does not go below $z(I)$; when no neighbour is better, the solution is a local optimum, not necessarily a global one.
oc-f-103 · level ipotesi · notes
A local search $LS$ for a minimization problem (integer objective $f$, optimal value $z(I)$) starts from $x^0$ and moves to strictly better neighbors. Which facts are needed to prove that it stops at a local optimum after at most $f(x^0)-z(I)$ moves?
  1. The rule is best improvement; solutions are finite; $f$ is convex and $f\ge z(I)$
  2. Every move strictly improves $f$; solutions are finite; with integer $f$ every move improves by at least $1$ and $f\ge z(I)$
  3. The neighborhood is exact; solutions are finite; with integer $f$ every move improves by at least $1$ and $f\ge z(I)$
  4. The problem has a unique optimal solution; every move strictly improves $f$; $f$ is integer
Explanation. Termination follows from the strict improvement over a finite set; the number of moves from the decrease of at least $1$ per move down to at most $z(I)$. The neighbourhood need not be exact.
oc-f-104 · level facile · notes
In the symmetric TSP, a 2-opt move removes two non-adjacent edges of the tour and reconnects the two paths in the only other way that gives a tour. How many moves does the 2-opt neighborhood of a tour with $n=10$ cities have?
  1. $35$
  2. $45$
  3. $90$
  4. $100$
Explanation. The 2-opt neighbourhood has $\frac{n(n-3)}{2}=\frac{10\cdot 7}{2}=35$ moves: the pairs of non-adjacent edges of the tour. $45=\binom{10}{2}$ counts all the pairs of cities.
oc-f-105 · level facile · notes
In scheduling $P\,||\,C_{\max}$, the move neighborhood moves one job to another machine and the swap neighborhood exchanges two jobs on different machines. An $LPT$ solution is a local optimum for the move neighborhood. What can be concluded?
  1. It is a global optimum, because move examines all the jobs
  2. It is a local optimum for the swap neighborhood too
  3. It is a local optimum for every neighborhood of the problem
  4. Nothing about global optimality: a swap move may still improve it
Explanation. A local optimum depends on the neighborhood: in the notes an $LPT$ solution is a local optimum for move but not for swap, which improves it.
oc-f-106 · level facile · notes
In local search for bin packing (or for $P\,||\,C_{\max}$) one minimizes $SQ=\sum_b L_b^2$, where $L_b$ is the load (total weight) of bin $b$. A move shifts an item of weight $\delta=2$ from bin $a$, with $L_a=9$, to bin $b$, with $L_b=5$. By how much does $SQ$ change?
  1. $-16$
  2. $0$
  3. $-8$
  4. $8$
Explanation. Only the two loads change: $(L_a-\delta)^2+(L_b+\delta)^2-L_a^2-L_b^2=2\delta(L_b-L_a+\delta)=2\cdot 2\cdot(5-9+2)=-8$; indeed $49+49-81-25=-8$.
oc-f-434 · level facile · notes
In the 2-opt local search for the symmetric TSP, the tour $(1,3,2,4,5)$ has length $25$. The move removing edges $\{3,2\}$ and $\{4,5\}$ and adding $\{3,4\}$ and $\{2,5\}$ has $d_{32}=4$, $d_{45}=6$, $d_{34}=3$, $d_{25}=5$. With incremental evaluation, how long is the new tour?
  1. $25$
  2. $17$
  3. $23$
  4. $27$
Explanation. $\Delta=d_{34}+d_{25}-d_{32}-d_{45}=3+5-4-6=-2$, hence $25-2=23$. Looking at the four edges that change is enough, instead of recomputing the whole tour.
oc-f-435 · level facile · notes
In the 2-opt local search for the TSP with $n$ cities, the neighbourhood has $\frac{n(n-3)}{2}$ moves. What does a best-improvement iteration cost with incremental evaluation ($O(1)$ per move), and recomputing every tour from scratch ($O(n)$ per move)?
  1. $O(1)$ and $O(n)$
  2. $O(n^2)$ and $O(n^3)$
  3. $O(n)$ and $O(n^2)$
  4. $O(n^2)$ and $O(n^2)$
Explanation. The cost of an iteration is the number of neighbours times the cost of evaluating one. Incremental evaluation changes neither the local optima nor the search path, only the time.
oc-f-436 · level facile · notes
In a local search for a minimization problem, the neighbours of the current solution are examined in a fixed order and have objective changes $\Delta=(+2,-1,-5,-3)$. Which neighbour do the first-improvement and the best-improvement rules choose?
  1. First improvement the second ($-1$), best improvement the third ($-5$)
  2. Both the third ($-5$)
  3. First improvement the first ($+2$), best improvement the third ($-5$)
  4. First improvement the second ($-1$), best improvement the fourth ($-3$)
Explanation. First improvement stops at the first improving neighbour, best improvement examines the whole neighbourhood and takes the most negative change. With first improvement the result depends on the order of examination.
oc-f-437 · level facile · notes
In local search the first-improvement and best-improvement rules are compared. Which statement is correct?
  1. Best improvement always finds a better local optimum than first improvement
  2. First improvement does not terminate if the neighbourhood is large
  3. The two rules always follow the same path, with different times
  4. First improvement makes cheaper iterations but usually more moves; the two rules may stop in different local optima
Explanation. In the local optimum both examine the whole neighbourhood. Before getting there they follow different paths, and neither dominates the other on all instances.
oc-f-438 · level facile · notes
In a local search for bin packing minimizing the number of bins, almost all moves (moving an item to another bin) leave the objective unchanged. What happens to a search that accepts only strictly improving moves?
  1. It always finds the optimum, because the objective is integer
  2. It becomes a random search, because all moves are equivalent
  3. It stops on a plateau, even if a sequence of zero-value moves would lead to a better solution
  4. It never terminates, because zero-value moves are infinite
Explanation. On a plateau the objective does not distinguish the solutions and the search is blind. The remedy is an auxiliary objective, for example the sum of the squared loads.
oc-f-439 · level facile · notes
In the local search for the 0-1 knapsack with penalties, the search space is $\{0,1\}^n$ and the profit minus a penalty proportional to the excess weight over the capacity is maximized. What must the search remember?
  1. Nothing: with the penalty every local optimum is feasible
  2. The best feasible solution visited, because the final local optimum may be infeasible
  3. Only the last vector visited, which is always feasible
  4. The vector with the highest penalty, to be avoided
Explanation. With penalties the search may cross and stop in the infeasible region; the returned solution is the best feasible one met along the way.
oc-f-440 · level facile · notes
In the local search for the 0-1 knapsack with a penalty on the excess weight, how does the penalty per unit of excess matter?
  1. Large: local optima are feasible but the search never leaves the feasible region; small: it crosses it but may stop outside
  2. Large: the search becomes random; small: the search becomes exact
  3. It does not matter: the penalty changes only the value, not the chosen moves
  4. Small: local optima are always feasible; large: never
Explanation. A high penalty makes every infeasible vector unattractive, a low one makes them attractive: the value balances freedom of movement and feasibility of the result.
oc-f-441 · level facile · notes
In a multi-start local search every run, from a random starting solution, reaches the optimum with probability $q=0.1$, independently of the others. With $M=10$ runs, what is approximately the probability that none finds it?
  1. $0\%$
  2. $10\%$
  3. $90\%$
  4. $35\%$
Explanation. All runs miss the optimum with probability $(1-q)^M=0.9^{10}\approx 0.35$. The runs have no memory, and for large $n$ the probability $q$ shrinks quickly.
oc-f-442 · level facile · notes
In multi-start local search $M$ local searches are run from different starting solutions and the best local optimum is kept. Which statement is correct?
  1. The result depends on the order of the runs
  2. With $M\ge n$ runs the optimum is guaranteed
  3. The runs are independent and parallelizable, but none uses what the others found
  4. Every run restarts from the local optimum of the previous one
Explanation. Multi-start is the simplest form of diversification: without memory, every run starts from scratch and spends most of its moves on the descent.
oc-f-443 · level facile · notes
Local search for $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ with $3$ machines of loads $(10,10,6)$. A job of time $3$ is moved from machine $1$ to machine $3$. How does the makespan change, and why?
  1. It rises to $13$, because machine $3$ receives the job
  2. It stays $10$: machine $2$ still has load $10$, and a move improves only if it leaves the unique critical machine
  3. It drops to $9$, because machine $3$ reaches $9$
  4. It drops to $7$, the new load of machine $1$
Explanation. The new loads are $(7,10,9)$: the maximum stays $10$. With two critical machines no single move improves the makespan, which is why an auxiliary objective is needed.
oc-f-444 · level facile · notes
For $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ the move neighbourhood moves a job to another machine and the swap neighbourhood exchanges two jobs on different machines. What structural difference is there between the two moves?
  1. A swap keeps the number of jobs on every machine, a move changes it: the two neighbourhoods complement each other
  2. A move keeps the number of jobs on every machine, a swap changes it
  3. A swap changes the loads of three machines, a move of two
  4. No difference: a swap is always a move
Explanation. A swap equals two moves, but as a single move it cannot fix a machine with too many jobs; a local optimum for move may not be one for swap, and vice versa.
oc-f-445 · level facile · notes
A local search needs a starting solution. Which choice is typical, and what limit does it have on bin packing if the First Fit Decreasing (FFD) solution is already optimal?
  1. An infeasible solution, which the search makes feasible
  2. The LP relaxation solution, always integer
  3. The empty solution, because local search adds elements
  4. The solution of a constructive heuristic; if it is already optimal no move improves it, and starting from worse solutions is better to study the search
Explanation. Local search improves a complete feasible solution, usually a constructive one. From an optimal solution there is no improving move: for bin packing the notes start from Next Fit and First Fit.
oc-f-446 · level facile · notes
To study a local search on many starting solutions, random solutions are generated with a pseudo-random number generator (Park and Miller) from a seed. Why is the seed fixed?
  1. Because without a seed the generator produces only infeasible solutions
  2. Because the seed is the number of iterations of the search
  3. The same seed gives the same starting solutions in every language and computer: experiments are reproducible
  4. Because a fixed seed gives better starting solutions
Explanation. The sequence is determined by the seed: notes, exercises and code can obtain exactly the same results, and comparisons between methods use the same starts.
oc-f-447 · level facile · notes
In the TSP with Euclidean distances (cities in the plane), what can be said about a tour that is a local optimum for the 2-opt neighbourhood?
  1. It contains the shortest edge of the instance
  2. It has no crossing edges
  3. It is always the optimal tour
  4. All its edges have the same length
Explanation. If two edges cross, the triangle inequality shows that the 2-opt move uncrossing them shortens the tour; so a local optimum has no crossings, but it may not be optimal.
oc-f-448 · level facile · notes
In the TSP, the Or-opt move relocates a short segment of consecutive cities elsewhere in the tour, without reversing it. Which statement on the two neighbourhoods 2-opt and Or-opt is correct?
  1. A local optimum for 2-opt may not be one for Or-opt, and vice versa
  2. Every local optimum for 2-opt is also one for Or-opt
  3. Or-opt contains all the 2-opt moves
  4. The two neighbourhoods have the same number of moves
Explanation. The two moves change different edges (two for 2-opt, three for Or-opt) and contain different moves: combining them, for example in a variable neighbourhood descent, improves the results.
oc-f-449 · level facile · notes
Variable neighbourhood descent ($VND$) uses the neighbourhoods $N_1,\dots,N_K$, ordered from the smallest to the largest. How does it proceed?
  1. It runs a complete local search in every $N_k$, once, in order
  2. It chooses a random neighbourhood at every iteration
  3. It uses only $N_K$, the largest, since it contains the others
  4. It looks for a better neighbour in $N_1$, then in $N_2$ and so on; after every improvement it returns to $N_1$; it stops when no $N_k$ improves
Explanation. The expensive neighbourhoods are explored only when the cheap ones are exhausted; $VND$ is deterministic and terminates by the same argument as local search.
oc-f-450 · level facile · notes
When variable neighbourhood descent ($VND$) with neighbourhoods $N_1,\dots,N_K$ stops, what is known about the returned solution?
  1. It is a local optimum only with respect to the last neighbourhood $N_K$
  2. It is the best solution of $N_1$ from the starting solution
  3. It is a local optimum with respect to each of the neighbourhoods $N_1,\dots,N_K$
  4. It is a global optimum, because it tried all the neighbourhoods
Explanation. The last exploration of every neighbourhood found no improvement and the solution did not change any more: it is a local optimum for all of them. A global optimum would be one for every neighbourhood, but not conversely.

32. Metaheuristics (17)

oc-f-107 · level facile · notes
In simulated annealing for a minimization problem the Metropolis rule is used. At temperature $T=3$ a neighbor that worsens the objective by $\delta=6$ is proposed. With which probability is it accepted?
  1. $e^{2}$
  2. $1$
  3. $e^{-1/2}$
  4. $e^{-2}$
Explanation. Metropolis rule: a worsening $\delta>0$ is accepted with probability $e^{-\delta/T}=e^{-6/3}=e^{-2}\approx 0.14$; an improvement is always accepted.
oc-f-108 · level facile · notes
Tabu search is a local search metaheuristic with a tabu list of moves forbidden for a number of iterations (the tabu tenure). Which statement is correct?
  1. It moves to a random neighbor, accepted with probability $e^{-\delta/T}$; the tabu list forbids visited solutions forever
  2. It moves to the overall best neighbor; the tabu list only serves to stop the algorithm when full
  3. It moves to the best non-tabu neighbor even if worse; a tabu move is allowed if it beats the best solution found
  4. It moves to the best non-tabu neighbor only if better; a tabu move is never allowed, even if it beats the best
Explanation. The tabu list is a short-term memory: the moves that undo the recent moves are forbidden for a number of iterations (the tenure). With tenure $0$ tabu search reduces to a local search.
oc-f-109 · level facile · notes
Metaheuristics (simulated annealing, tabu search, ILS, VNS, GRASP, LNS, genetic algorithms) explore the space of feasible solutions. Which kind of bounds do they produce?
  1. The optimal value, if run long enough
  2. Only primal bounds: they do not know how far they are from the optimum
  3. Only dual bounds: every solution found bounds the optimum
  4. Primal and dual bounds, whose gap closes over time
Explanation. Metaheuristics build feasible solutions; to measure their quality a dual bound from another source (relaxation, exact algorithm) is needed.
oc-f-110 · level facile · notes
GRASP (Greedy Randomized Adaptive Search Procedure) is a multi-start metaheuristic. What does it do at every iteration?
  1. It builds a solution with a randomized greedy on the restricted candidate list and improves it by local search
  2. It perturbs the current local optimum with a random kick and reapplies local search to the perturbed solution
  3. It destroys part of the current solution, repairs it with a heuristic and accepts the result if it is better
  4. It selects two parents from the population, recombines them and mutates the child, which replaces the worst individual
Explanation. GRASP is a multi-start method: randomized construction plus local search, and at the end the best solution found. The kick belongs to ILS, destroy and repair to LNS, recombination to genetic algorithms.
oc-f-451 · level facile · notes
In the metaheuristics for bin packing and $P\,||\,C_{\max}$, solutions with the same objective are compared by the sum of the squared loads. In which direction is it optimized in the two problems?
  1. Minimized in both, because balanced loads are always better
  2. Maximized in both, because the total is fixed
  3. Bin packing: minimized; $P\,||\,C_{\max}$: maximized
  4. Bin packing: maximized (items in few full bins); $P\,||\,C_{\max}$: minimized (balanced loads)
Explanation. In bin packing we want to empty a bin, and the sum of squares grows when weight concentrates; in $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ we want to lower the largest load, and the sum decreases when loads balance.
oc-f-452 · level facile · notes
The metaheuristics of the module use pseudo-random numbers generated by the Park and Miller method from a seed. Why are the results reported over several seeds?
  1. Because with a single seed the metaheuristic does not terminate
  2. Because different seeds change the instance solved
  3. The result of a metaheuristic depends on the random numbers: a single seed is not enough to evaluate it
  4. Because every seed gives a different global optimum
Explanation. The instance is the same, but the random choices change the path: minimum, average and maximum over several runs are reported, as recommended for every randomized heuristic.
oc-f-453 · level facile · notes
In simulated annealing the temperature is lowered with the rule $T\leftarrow\alpha T$, with $0<\alpha<1$. What effect does the temperature have on accepting a neighbour worse by $\delta>0$, accepted with probability $e^{-\delta/T}$?
  1. At low temperature all neighbours are accepted
  2. At high temperature worsenings are often accepted (diversification); at low temperature almost never (intensification)
  3. At high temperature worsenings are never accepted
  4. The temperature changes only the number of iterations, not the acceptance
Explanation. $e^{-\delta/T}$ tends to $1$ for large $T$ and to $0$ for small $T$: cooling moves gradually from exploration to descent, as in the annealing of metals.
oc-f-454 · level facile · notes
In tabu search the tabu list does not store the visited solutions but attributes of the recent moves (for example «job $j$ on machine $h$»), for a fixed tabu tenure. Which problem does this create, and how is it repaired?
  1. It also forbids solutions never visited; the aspiration criterion admits a tabu move that beats the best solution found
  2. It does not prevent going back to the solution just left; it is repaired with infinite tenure
  3. It makes the search random; it is repaired with the Metropolis criterion
  4. It forbids only the current solution; it is repaired by storing the whole path
Explanation. An attribute forbids all solutions containing it, not only the one left. Aspiration restores the tabu moves that would lead to a new record.
oc-f-455 · level facile · notes
Iterated local search ($ILS$) repeats: perturbation of the current local optimum, local search, acceptance criterion. What role does the strength of the perturbation play?
  1. The stronger, the better the result always is
  2. It does not matter: the local search undoes any perturbation
  3. Too weak: the search does not terminate; too strong: the search becomes exact
  4. Too weak: the local search returns to the same optimum; too strong: it nearly restarts from a random solution
Explanation. The perturbation must leave the current valley without losing the structure of the good solution: with too strong a perturbation $ILS$ degenerates into multi-start.
oc-f-456 · level facile · notes
In iterated local search ($ILS$) for the TSP with the 2-opt local search, the double bridge is used as perturbation: it cuts the tour into four segments $A,B,C,D$ and reconnects them as $A\,C\,B\,D$. Why is it suitable?
  1. It always returns a shorter tour
  2. It changes a single edge, so it does not lose the tour structure
  3. It changes three edges without reversing segments, and a single 2-opt move does not easily undo it
  4. It is a 2-opt move, so the local search always accepts it
Explanation. The double bridge is a move that 2-opt does not contain and cannot undo in one step: it moves the search to another valley while keeping most of the tour.
oc-f-457 · level facile · notes
In variable neighbourhood search ($VNS$) the neighbourhood $N_k$ contains the solutions obtained with $k$ moves. At every iteration a random solution of $N_k$ is chosen (shaking) and improved by a local search. How is $k$ updated?
  1. $k$ is chosen at random at every iteration
  2. If the local optimum found is better it is accepted and $k=1$ again; otherwise $k+1$
  3. $k$ always increases by $1$, whatever the result
  4. If the local optimum is better $k+1$; otherwise back to $k=1$
Explanation. The search explores close to the current optimum first and goes farther only when the neighbourhood gives no improvement: shaking diversifies, local search intensifies.
oc-f-458 · level facile · notes
What is the difference between variable neighbourhood descent ($VND$) and variable neighbourhood search ($VNS$)?
  1. $VND$ explores the neighbourhoods completely and deterministically; $VNS$ draws random solutions from them (shaking) and then applies a local search
  2. $VND$ is randomized, $VNS$ is deterministic
  3. $VND$ uses one neighbourhood, $VNS$ several
  4. They are the same algorithm with two names
Explanation. $VND$ stops at a local optimum for all the neighbourhoods; $VNS$ uses the growing neighbourhoods to jump out of local optima, and is a metaheuristic.
oc-f-459 · level facile · notes
In $GRASP$ every iteration builds a solution with a randomized greedy (restricted candidate list with parameter $\alpha$) and improves it with a local search. What happens with $\alpha=0$?
  1. The construction becomes completely random
  2. The local search is no longer run
  3. $GRASP$ always finds the global optimum
  4. Every iteration builds the same greedy solution: $GRASP$ becomes a single local search
Explanation. With $\alpha=0$ the list contains only the best candidates, and up to ties the construction is always the same; a little randomness is enough to get different solutions.
oc-f-460 · level facile · notes
In large neighbourhood search ($LNS$) a destroy method removes part of the solution and a repair method rebuilds it. What roles do the two methods play?
  1. Both diversify, and local search intensifies
  2. Both intensify: $LNS$ does not diversify
  3. Destroy (usually random) diversifies, repair intensifies
  4. Destroy intensifies, repair diversifies
Explanation. Removing different parts moves the search, and the number $q$ of removed elements controls how far; repair rebuilds the removed part as well as possible, with a heuristic or even exactly.
oc-f-461 · level facile · notes
In $LNS$ for bin packing, some bins of a First Fit Decreasing (FFD) solution are emptied and the removed items are repacked by FFD itself. What happens?
  1. The optimum is always obtained after one iteration
  2. The solution never improves: FFD repacks the removed items the same way
  3. The solution always improves by at least one bin
  4. The solution becomes infeasible
Explanation. Repairing with the same algorithm that built the solution repeats the same choices; with an exact repair of the subproblem, instead, $LNS$ can empty a bin.
oc-f-462 · level facile · notes
In a genetic algorithm for the 0-1 knapsack with binary encoding, one-point crossover with cut $cut=2$ combines parents $(1,1,0,0,1)$ and $(0,1,1,1,0)$. Which child does it produce, taking the first $cut$ genes from the first parent?
  1. $(1,1,1,1,0)$
  2. $(0,1,0,0,1)$
  3. $(1,1,0,1,0)$
  4. $(1,1,0,0,0)$
Explanation. Genes $1,2$ from the first parent, $(1,1)$, and genes $3,4,5$ from the second, $(1,1,0)$. The child may exceed the capacity and must be repaired, for example by removing the items of minimum efficiency.
oc-f-463 · level facile · notes
What distinguishes a memetic algorithm from a genetic algorithm?
  1. It uses no crossover, only mutation
  2. It uses a single solution instead of a population
  3. It accepts worsenings with the Metropolis rule
  4. Every new individual is improved by a local search before entering the population
Explanation. The memetic algorithm combines recombination of the population (diversification) with local search on every child (intensification).

33. MIP heuristics for the classical problems (22)

oc-f-111 · level facile · notes
In scheduling $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ an assignment is a binary vector $\vet x$ with $x_{ij}=1$ if job $j$ is on machine $i$; the distance $\Delta(\vet x,\bar{\vet x})$ counts the differing components. Two assignments differ in the machine of $3$ jobs. What is $\Delta(\vet x,\bar{\vet x})$?
  1. $3m$
  2. $6$
  3. $3$
  4. $9$
Explanation. Every moved job changes two components (one from $1$ to $0$, the other from $0$ to $1$): $\Delta=2r=6$.
oc-f-112 · level facile · notes
In the models of bin packing and $P\,||\,C_{\max}$, the binary variables $x_{jb}$ assign every item (job) $j$ to a bin (machine) $b$. Why does rounding the LP relaxation solution to the nearest integer almost always fail?
  1. The constraints $\sum_b x_{jb}=1$ are equalities: fractional values rounded one by one almost never sum to $1$
  2. The capacity constraints are inequalities: fractional values rounded one by one always satisfy them
  3. The LP relaxation of these models is unbounded, so there is no solution to round
  4. The LP relaxation of these models always has an integer solution, so rounding changes nothing
Explanation. With $\sum_b x_{jb}=1$ and fractional values, rounding each variable separately leaves items unassigned or assigned twice.
oc-f-113 · level facile · notes
In integer programming heuristics, diving fixes variables in sequence solving the LP relaxation, and RINS solves an integer subproblem close to the incumbent (best known) solution. Which statement is correct?
  1. A dive is a single path in the tree; RINS fixes the variables on which relaxation and incumbent differ
  2. A dive explores the whole tree; RINS fixes the variables on which relaxation and incumbent agree
  3. A dive explores the whole tree; RINS guarantees a solution better than the incumbent
  4. A dive is a single path in the tree; RINS fixes the variables on which relaxation and incumbent agree
Explanation. The first wrong fixing of a dive is never revised; RINS solves a sub-MIP with the agreeing variables fixed, so it cannot find solutions that change them.
oc-f-464 · level facile · notes
In heuristics based on integer linear programming (MIP), $\tilde{\vet x}$ denotes an optimal solution of the LP relaxation and $\bar{\vet x}$ the incumbent. What is the incumbent?
  1. The best feasible integer solution found so far
  2. The rounded LP relaxation solution
  3. The current dual bound of branch-and-bound
  4. The first fractional solution found by the simplex
Explanation. The incumbent is a primal bound: improvement heuristics (RINS, local branching, proximity search) start from $\bar{\vet x}$ and look for a better nearby solution.
oc-f-465 · level facile · notes
Which advantage do heuristics based on integer linear programming (MIP) have over problem-specific heuristics?
  1. They need no model of the problem
  2. They are always faster than constructive heuristics
  3. They always guarantee the optimum in polynomial time
  4. They use only the model, so they apply to every formulated problem, and the relaxation also gives a dual bound
Explanation. They work on the variables and constraints of the model, not on the structure of the problem; the LP relaxation (and the solver branch-and-bound) gives the other side of the gap.
oc-f-466 · level facile · notes
On the reference instances of knapsack, bin packing, TSP and $P\,||\,C_{\max}$, the LP relaxation value rounded in the direction of the bound (down for maximization, up for minimization) equals the optimal value. What does this mean for MIP heuristics?
  1. The relaxation is useless, because it is always weak
  2. The instances are wrong, because a relaxation is never exact
  3. The relaxations are strong: finding a good solution is the hard part, and the rounded bound can prove its optimality
  4. Heuristics are useless, because the relaxation is already integer
Explanation. When the bound is already tight, the practical problem is to find an integer solution of that value: it is the typical situation in which MIP heuristics are useful.
oc-f-467 · level facile · notes
In the rounding heuristic the LP relaxation solution is rounded to the nearest integer. Why is rounding down always feasible for the 0-1 knapsack ($\max\sum p_jx_j$, a single constraint $\sum w_jx_j\le c$, non-negative weights)?
  1. Because the split item always has $x_s=0$
  2. Lowering the $x_j$ never increases the weight, so the constraint stays satisfied
  3. Because the relaxation always has an integer solution
  4. Because rounding down always gives the optimal solution
Explanation. With non-negative coefficients in a $\le$ constraint, decreasing the variables keeps feasibility; in bin packing and $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ the assignment constraints are equalities and rounding fails.
oc-f-468 · level facile · notes
The 0-1 knapsack with $c=9$ has LP relaxation $\tilde x=(1,1,0.25,0)$ (items by non-increasing efficiency, weights $w=(5,4,4,3)$). What do rounding to the nearest integer and rounding down give?
  1. Both $(1,1,0,0)$, of weight $9$: feasible
  2. $(1,1,1,0)$, of weight $13$: infeasible
  3. $(1,1,0,1)$, of weight $12$: infeasible
  4. $(0,0,0,0)$: everything is lost
Explanation. $0.25$ rounds to $0$ in both cases, and the integer variables stay: the point $(1,1,0,0)$ weighs $5+4=9\le c$.
oc-f-469 · level facile · notes
In diving one fractional variable at a time is fixed and the LP relaxation is solved again. What is the difference between fractional diving and guided diving?
  1. Fractional fixes the most fractional variable to $1$; guided fixes a random variable
  2. Fractional explores the whole tree; guided a single path
  3. Fractional uses the incumbent; guided uses only the relaxation
  4. Fractional fixes the least fractional variable to its nearest integer; guided fixes the one closest to its incumbent value, at that value
Explanation. Both follow a single path in the branch-and-bound tree; guided diving uses a known solution to choose the direction.
oc-f-470 · level facile · notes
In diving on $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ the LP relaxation value stays the same for many successive fixings. Why?
  1. Because the makespan is an integer variable
  2. Because every fixing makes the relaxation infeasible
  3. The relaxation has a huge set of optimal solutions: fixing a variable moves the point to another one of equal value
  4. Because diving no longer solves the relaxation after the first fixing
Explanation. The relaxation splits the jobs among the machines and is worth $\sum_j t_j/m$ in many ways: its value says nothing about the quality of the path until the fixings bind the loads.
oc-f-471 · level facile · notes
The feasibility pump alternates the projection of an integer point onto the LP relaxation region (an LP minimizing the distance $\Delta$) and the rounding of the projection. What is its goal?
  1. Improving an already known incumbent
  2. Finding a first feasible solution; quality is the job of improvement methods
  3. Finding the optimal solution with a single projection
  4. Computing a dual bound better than the relaxation
Explanation. The pump almost ignores the objective: it looks for an integer feasible point. Heuristics such as RINS or proximity search then start from that solution.
oc-f-472 · level facile · notes
In the feasibility pump, the distance between a rounded binary point $\hat{\vet x}$ and a point $\vet x$ of the relaxation is $\Delta(\vet x,\hat{\vet x})=\sum_{j:\hat x_j=0}x_j+\sum_{j:\hat x_j=1}(1-x_j)$. What is it for $\hat{\vet x}=(1,0,1)$ and $\vet x=(0.8,\ 0.3,\ 0.5)$?
  1. $1$
  2. $1.6$
  3. $0.4$
  4. $0.7$
Explanation. $(1-0.8)+0.3+(1-0.5)=0.2+0.3+0.5=1$. If $\Delta=0$ the relaxation point is integer and the pump has found a feasible solution.
oc-f-473 · level facile · notes
In local branching for the 0-1 knapsack the sub-MIP is solved with the constraint $\Delta(\vet x,\bar{\vet x})\le k$, where $\Delta$ counts the binary components different from the incumbent $\bar{\vet x}$. Which local search neighbourhood does it correspond to with $k=1$?
  1. The swap neighbourhood, exchanging an item inside with one outside
  2. The whole space $\{0,1\}^n$
  3. No neighbourhood: the sub-MIP is empty
  4. The flip neighbourhood, adding or removing one item
Explanation. A single different component means changing one item; with $k=2$ swaps are included too. The solver explores the neighbourhood exactly.
oc-f-474 · level teorema · notes
In proximity search, for a minimization problem with integer objective values, a sub-MIP is solved with the constraint $f(\vet x)\le f(\bar{\vet x})-\theta$, with $\theta=1$, minimizing the distance from the incumbent $\bar{\vet x}$. What follows if the sub-MIP is infeasible?
  1. $\theta$ must be increased to find a solution
  2. The LP relaxation is unbounded
  3. The incumbent is optimal: no solution has value at least $1$ lower
  4. The incumbent is infeasible
Explanation. With integer values, a better solution would have $f(\vet x)\le f(\bar{\vet x})-1$; if none exists, $\bar{\vet x}$ is optimal. The proof comes from the solver, which proves infeasibility with its bounds.
oc-f-475 · level facile · notes
In relax-and-fix for lot sizing, the setup variables $y_t$ are divided into blocks of consecutive periods. How does the method proceed?
  1. It solves every block as an independent problem, without the stocks
  2. It solves a MIP with the first block binary and the others relaxed, fixes the first block, then moves to the next block
  3. It solves the LP relaxation once and rounds all the blocks together
  4. It fixes all setups to $1$ and optimizes the quantities
Explanation. Every sub-MIP has few binaries; the decisions of the fixed blocks stay, and the future ones are only relaxed. Fix-and-optimize then improves the solution by freeing one block at a time.
oc-f-476 · level facile · notes
In capacitated lot sizing, why are the only hard decisions in MIP heuristics the $m$ setup variables $y_t$?
  1. With the setups fixed, the rest is an LP with a network matrix and integer data, which has an integer optimal solution
  2. Because the produced quantities always equal the demands
  3. Because stocks are always zero in an optimal plan
  4. Because the LP relaxation is exact even with fractional setups
Explanation. With $y$ fixed the balance and capacity constraints remain, a flow structure: the simplex gives integer quantities. The relaxation with fractional $y$ is weak instead.
oc-f-477 · level facile · notes
Modern MIP solvers, such as Gurobi, run many heuristics during branch-and-bound and accept an initial solution (MIP start). What is a MIP start for?
  1. It replaces the LP relaxation at the root
  2. It guarantees that the solver returns exactly that solution
  3. It switches off the internal heuristics of the solver
  4. It gives an incumbent at once: the solver prunes nodes with worse bounds and starts with a finite gap
Explanation. A good initial incumbent allows pruning by bound from the start; the solver may still find better ones.
oc-f-478 · level facile · notes
The internal heuristics of a MIP solver are compared on a single instance, and one parameter setting finds the optimal solution earlier. What can be concluded?
  1. That the other settings are wrong
  2. That the solver is exact only with that setting
  3. Little: results depend on instance, solver version and machine, and a single instance proves nothing
  4. That this setting is the best for every instance
Explanation. As for every heuristic, comparisons need many instances and equal conditions; the time to find the optimum varies a lot from one instance to another.
oc-f-479 · level facile · notes
Constructive heuristics, local search and metaheuristics give only a primal bound. What do MIP heuristics run inside a solver add?
  1. A guaranteed polynomial running time
  2. A dual bound from the relaxation and branch-and-bound, hence a gap and sometimes a proof of optimality
  3. A constant approximation guarantee
  4. Solutions always better than those of metaheuristics
Explanation. With the model comes its relaxation: for the reference knapsack $\lfloor 17.75\rfloor=17$, so a solution of value $17$ is optimal.
oc-f-480 · level facile · notes
For a 0-1 knapsack with integer profits, the LP relaxation equals $17.75$ and a MIP heuristic finds a solution of value $17$. What can be concluded?
  1. The solution is optimal, because $\lfloor 17.75\rfloor=17$ is an upper bound
  2. The solution is $0.75$ away from the optimum
  3. Another heuristic is needed to know whether it is optimal
  4. The optimum is $18$, the round-up of the relaxation
Explanation. With integer profits every solution has integer value at most $17.75$, hence at most $17$: the solution found attains the bound.
oc-f-481 · level facile · notes
In the bin packing model used by MIP heuristics there are $m$ available bins, with variables $x_{jb}$ and $y_b$. Which value of $m$ is always valid, and which is used on the reference instance with $n=6$ items?
  1. $m=\lceil\sum_j w_j/c\rceil$, always valid; on the instance $m=3$
  2. $m=1$, because bins are added during the search
  3. $m=2n$, for some margin; on the instance $m=12$
  4. $m=n$, always valid: one bin per item; on the instance $m=6$
Explanation. With $m=n$ there is always a solution (one item per bin). The weight bound is a lower bound: if it were below the optimum, the model would have no solution.
oc-f-565 · level teorema · notes
In the $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ model with binary variables $x_{jh}$ (job $j$ on machine $h$), the distance between two feasible assignments is $\Delta(\vet x,\bar{\vet x})=\sum_{(j,h):\bar x_{jh}=1}(1-x_{jh})+\sum_{(j,h):\bar x_{jh}=0}x_{jh}$. If $r$ jobs change machine, what is $\Delta$, and why?
  1. $r$: every moved job changes one component
  2. $rm$: every moved job changes all its $m$ components
  3. $r^2$: every pair of moved jobs counts once
  4. $2r$: every moved job leaves a $0$ where $\bar x$ has $1$ and puts a $1$ where $\bar x$ has $0$
Explanation. A job on the same machine contributes $0$; one moved from machine $h$ to $h'$ contributes $1$ for $h$ and $1$ for $h'$. This is why $\Delta\le 2$ is the move neighbourhood of local search.

Part: Approximation algorithms (89)

34. Principles of approximation algorithms (12)

oc-f-114 · level facile · notes
An algorithm $\mathscr{A}$ for a maximization problem (optimal value $z(I)$) has relative error $\frac{z(I)-\mathscr{A}(I)}{z(I)}$ at most $\varepsilon=\frac14$. What is its relative guarantee $\alpha$, meant as a bound on the ratio $z(I)/\mathscr{A}(I)$?
  1. $\alpha=\frac54$
  2. $\alpha=\frac34$
  3. $\alpha=4$
  4. $\alpha=\frac43$
Explanation. For a maximization problem $z-\mathscr{A}\le\varepsilon z$ gives $z/\mathscr{A}\le\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon}=\frac43$; for a minimization problem one would get $1+\varepsilon=\frac54$.
oc-f-115 · level facile · notes
For a minimization problem, a heuristic finds $\mathscr{A}(I)=12$ and the optimal value is $z(I)=10$. What are the performance ratio $\mathscr{A}(I)/z(I)$ and the relative error $\frac{\mathscr{A}(I)-z(I)}{z(I)}$?
  1. $\frac65$ and $2$
  2. $\frac56$ and $16.7\%$
  3. $\frac65$ and $20\%$
  4. $\frac56$ and $20\%$
Explanation. For minimization the ratio is $\mathscr{A}(I)/z(I)=\frac{12}{10}$, always at least $1$; the relative error is $\frac{12-10}{10}=20\%$; the additive error is $2$.
oc-f-116 · level teorema · notes
An approximation scheme receives an instance and an $\varepsilon>0$ and returns a solution with relative error at most $\varepsilon$. Which statement on PTAS and FPTAS is correct?
  1. A time $O(n^{1/\varepsilon})$ is polynomial both in the size and in $\frac1\varepsilon$, hence it gives an FPTAS
  2. In a PTAS the time is polynomial for every fixed $\varepsilon$; in an FPTAS it is polynomial in $\frac1\varepsilon$ too
  3. In a PTAS the time is polynomial in $\frac1\varepsilon$ too; in an FPTAS only for every fixed $\varepsilon$
  4. In both a PTAS and an FPTAS the time is polynomial for every fixed $\varepsilon$, and in neither in $\frac1\varepsilon$
Explanation. A running time such as $O(n^{1/\varepsilon})$ is polynomial for fixed $\varepsilon$ (PTAS) but not in $\frac1\varepsilon$; an FPTAS, such as $O(n^2/\varepsilon)$, is polynomial in both.
oc-f-117 · level facile · notes
For a minimization problem, the relative guarantee of an algorithm $\mathscr{A}$ is a constant $\alpha$ with $\mathscr{A}(I)\le\alpha\,z(I)$ for every instance $I$. On some instance $\mathscr{A}$ has ratio $\mathscr{A}(I)/z(I)=\frac32$. What does this instance prove?
  1. That no constant smaller than $\frac32$ is a relative guarantee of $\mathscr{A}$
  2. That $\frac32$ is a relative guarantee of $\mathscr{A}$, valid for every instance
  3. That the worst-case ratio of $\mathscr{A}$ is exactly $\frac32$
  4. That every constant larger than $\frac32$ is a relative guarantee of $\mathscr{A}$
Explanation. An instance only gives a lower bound on $r(\mathscr{A})$: proving that $\frac32$ is a guarantee requires a proof valid for every instance.
oc-f-482 · level facile · notes
For a maximization problem, the performance ratio of an algorithm $\mathscr{A}$ on instance $I$ is $z(I)/\mathscr{A}(I)$, with $z(I)$ the optimal value. A knapsack heuristic finds $\mathscr{A}(I)=16$ on an instance with $z(I)=20$. What is the ratio?
  1. $\frac45$
  2. $4$
  3. $\frac{9}{5}$
  4. $\frac54$
Explanation. $20/16=\frac54\ge 1$. In maximization problems the optimum goes in the numerator, so the ratio is always at least $1$ as in minimization problems; $\frac45$ is the inverted ratio.
oc-f-483 · level teorema · notes
The worst-case performance ratio $r(\mathscr{A})$ is the supremum of the performance ratios of $\mathscr{A}$ over all instances, and $\mathscr{A}$ is an $\alpha$-approximation if the ratio is at most $\alpha$ on every instance. Which statement is correct?
  1. $\mathscr{A}$ is an $\alpha$-approximation if and only if $\alpha\le r(\mathscr{A})$
  2. $r(\mathscr{A})$ is always attained by some instance
  3. $\mathscr{A}$ is an $\alpha$-approximation if and only if $\alpha\ge r(\mathscr{A})$
  4. $\mathscr{A}$ is an $\alpha$-approximation if and only if $\alpha=r(\mathscr{A})$
Explanation. Every ratio is at most the supremum, so $\alpha=r(\mathscr{A})$ and every larger $\alpha$ are guarantees; a smaller constant is not. The supremum may only be approached, as for $GR^e$ in the knapsack.
oc-f-484 · level facile · notes
For a minimization problem, $\mathscr{A}$ has additive guarantee $\beta$ if $\mathscr{A}(I)-z(I)\le\beta$ for every instance. An algorithm for coloring planar graphs uses at most $5$ colors, and the graph contains a triangle. Which additive guarantee follows on these graphs?
  1. $\beta=1$, because $\chi(G)\le 4$
  2. $\beta=2$, because $\chi(G)\ge 3$
  3. $\beta=5$, the number of colors used
  4. $\beta=\frac53$, the ratio of colors used to chromatic number
Explanation. With a triangle $\chi(G)\ge 3$, hence $5-\chi(G)\le 2$. The additive guarantee measures the difference, not the ratio.
oc-f-485 · level facile · notes
For a minimization problem, an algorithm with additive guarantee $\beta$ satisfies $\mathscr{A}(I)\le z(I)+\beta$. What does it imply for the performance ratio $\mathscr{A}(I)/z(I)$?
  1. $\mathscr{A}(I)/z(I)\le 1+\beta/z(I)$, which tends to $1$ as $z(I)$ grows
  2. $\mathscr{A}(I)/z(I)\le\beta$ for every instance
  3. $\mathscr{A}(I)/z(I)\ge 1+\beta$ for every instance
  4. Nothing: the two guarantees are independent
Explanation. Dividing by $z(I)>0$ gives $1+\beta/z(I)$: on instances with large optimal value an additive guarantee is stronger than any constant relative guarantee.
oc-f-486 · level facile · notes
For a minimization problem, an $\alpha$-approximation satisfies $\mathscr{A}(I)\le\alpha\,z(I)$ for every instance. A $2$-approximation returns a solution of value $30$. What is known about the optimal value $z(I)$?
  1. $z(I)=15$
  2. $30\le z(I)\le 60$
  3. $z(I)\le 15$
  4. $15\le z(I)\le 30$
Explanation. The solution is feasible, so $z(I)\le 30$; the guarantee gives $30\le 2z(I)$, i.e., $z(I)\ge 15$. The guarantee provides a dual bound even without a relaxation.
oc-f-487 · level facile · notes
An approximation scheme for a maximization problem is a family of algorithms $\{\mathscr{A}_\varepsilon\}_{\varepsilon\in(0,1)}$ which, for every $\varepsilon$ and every instance, have relative error $\frac{z(I)-\mathscr{A}_\varepsilon(I)}{z(I)}$ at most $\varepsilon$. What is expected of the running time as $\varepsilon$ decreases?
  1. That it stays constant: $\varepsilon$ changes only the guarantee
  2. That it becomes exponential for every $\varepsilon<\frac12$
  3. That it grows: a smaller allowed error needs more time
  4. That it decreases: with small $\varepsilon$ there are fewer solutions to examine
Explanation. It is natural that time grows when the allowed error decreases; PTAS and FPTAS differ exactly in how it grows with respect to $\frac1\varepsilon$.
oc-f-488 · level facile · notes
An approximation scheme with $n$ input elements and error parameter $\varepsilon$ is an FPTAS if the time is polynomial both in the instance size and in $\frac1\varepsilon$. Which of the following times gives an FPTAS?
  1. $O(n^{1/\varepsilon^2})$
  2. $O(n^2/\varepsilon)$
  3. $O(n^{1/\varepsilon})$
  4. $O(2^{1/\varepsilon}\,n)$
Explanation. $n^2/\varepsilon$ is a polynomial in $n$ and $\frac1\varepsilon$: it is the time of the Basic-FPTAS for the knapsack. The others are polynomial in $n$ for fixed $\varepsilon$ (PTAS) but not in $\frac1\varepsilon$.
oc-f-489 · level facile · notes
A PTAS for a problem has time $O(n^{1/\varepsilon})$ on instances with $n$ elements. How does the time change going from $\varepsilon=\frac12$ to $\varepsilon=\frac1{10}$?
  1. From $O(n^2)$ to $O(n^{10})$: polynomial for every fixed $\varepsilon$, but with growing exponent
  2. From $O(n^2)$ to $O(n^{2/10})$: it decreases
  3. It stays $O(n^2)$: the exponent does not depend on $\varepsilon$
  4. It becomes exponential in $n$
Explanation. For every fixed $\varepsilon$ the time is a polynomial in $n$, but its degree grows as $\frac1\varepsilon$: this is why it is not an FPTAS.

35. Approximation algorithms for the knapsack problem (17)

oc-f-118 · level teorema · notes
For the knapsack problem (KP): $GR$ is the greedy by efficiency, $GR^e$ takes the best of $GR$ and the best single item, $H^2$ tries all subsets of at most two items completing them with the greedy; $r(\cdot)$ is the worst-case ratio. Which statement is correct?
  1. $GR$ has no constant guarantee, $GR^e$ has $r(GR^e)=2$, $H^2$ has $r(H^2)=\frac43$
  2. $GR$ has $r(GR)=2$ and $GR^e$ has $r(GR^e)=\frac43$
  3. $GR^e$ has $r(GR^e)=\frac32$ and $H^2$ has $r(H^2)=2$
  4. $GR$, $GR^e$ and $H^2$ all have ratio $2$
Explanation. The greedy by efficiency can be arbitrarily bad; taking the best of $GR$ and the best single item gives $2$; guessing two items ($H^2$) gives $\frac43$; all the ratios are tight.
oc-f-119 · level ipotesi · notes
In the knapsack problem, $GR$ is the greedy by efficiency, $p_{\max}$ the maximum profit of an item and $GR^e(I)=\max\{GR(I),p_{\max}\}$; with the items sorted by efficiency, $s$ is the split item. Which facts are used to prove $z(I)/GR^e(I)\le 2$?
  1. $z(I)\le\sum_{i<s}p_i+p_s$; $GR(I)\ge\sum_{i<s}p_i$; $p_{\max}\le p_s$
  2. $z(I)\le\sum_{i<s}p_i$; $GR(I)\ge\sum_{i\le s}p_i$; $p_{\max}\ge p_s$
  3. $z(I)=GR(I)$ with sorted items; $GR(I)\ge p_s$; $p_{\max}\ge p_s$
  4. $z(I)\le\sum_{i<s}p_i+p_s$; $GR(I)\ge\sum_{i<s}p_i$; $p_{\max}\ge p_s$
Explanation. $z(I)\le z(\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{KP}})\le\sum_{i<s}p_i+p_s\le GR(I)+p_{\max}\le 2\,GR^e(I)$: the greedy takes at least the items before $s$, and the best single item is worth at least $p_s$.
oc-f-120 · level facile · notes
For the knapsack problem (KP), algorithm $H^{\ell}$ tries all subsets of at most $\ell$ items and completes each with the greedy by efficiency. Which worst-case performance ratio does it guarantee with $\ell=3$?
  1. $\frac32$
  2. $\frac65$
  3. $\frac54$
  4. $\frac43$
Explanation. $r(H^{\ell})=\frac{\ell+2}{\ell+1}$: with $\ell=3$ we get $\frac54$. The running time $O(n^{\ell+1})$ grows with $\ell$: the family is a PTAS, not an FPTAS.
oc-f-121 · level facile · notes
For the knapsack problem (KP, $n$ items, capacity $c$, integer profits), an algorithm has absolute guarantee $k$ if $z(I)-\mathscr{A}(I)\le k$ for every instance. What does the theorem on the absolute guarantee for the KP state?
  1. There is a polynomial algorithm with absolute guarantee $1$, obtained by rounding the Dantzig bound
  2. An algorithm with absolute guarantee $k$ and time $O(f(n,c))$ would solve every instance optimally in the same time
  3. An algorithm with absolute guarantee $k$ and time $O(f(n,c))$ would give an FPTAS with time $O(f(n,c)/k)$
  4. There is a polynomial algorithm with absolute guarantee $p_{\max}$, the maximum profit of an item
Explanation. Multiplying the profits by $k+1$ turns an absolute error $\le k$ into zero error: an algorithm with a fixed absolute guarantee would solve the KP exactly in the same time.
oc-f-490 · level facile · notes
0-1 knapsack with capacity $c=9$ and items already sorted by non-increasing efficiency $p_j/w_j$: $p=(10,7,6,4)$, $w=(5,4,3,3)$. The split item $s$ is the first one not fitting entirely, and $\mathit{rc}=c-\sum_{j<s}w_j$ is the residual capacity. What are $s$ and $\mathit{rc}$?
  1. $s=3$, $\mathit{rc}=0$
  2. $s=2$, $\mathit{rc}=0$
  3. $s=3$, $\mathit{rc}=3$
  4. $s=4$, $\mathit{rc}=0$
Explanation. The first two items weigh $5+4=9\le c$; adding the third gives $12>9$, hence $s=3$ and $\mathit{rc}=9-9=0$. The second item is the last that fits, not the split item.
oc-f-491 · level facile · notes
In the 0-1 knapsack (weights $w_j\le c$, items sorted by non-increasing efficiency) the split item is the first one not fitting entirely. Why is $\sum_j w_j>c$ assumed?
  1. Because otherwise the LP relaxation is unbounded
  2. Because otherwise the split item would be the first one
  3. Because otherwise the greedy does not terminate
  4. Otherwise all items fit, there is no split item and taking them all is optimal
Explanation. With $\sum_j w_j\le c$ the instance is trivial: the greedy takes everything and is optimal. The assumption guarantees that the split item exists.
oc-f-492 · level facile · notes
0-1 knapsack with capacity $c=8$ and items sorted by non-increasing efficiency: $p=(12,9,5,2)$, $w=(4,3,2,1)$. The greedy $GR$ examines the items in this order and takes every item that fits, even after the split item. What is $GR(I)$?
  1. $24$
  2. $28$
  3. $23$
  4. $21$
Explanation. Items $1$ and $2$ fit (weight $7$); item $3$ does not (it would be $9$); item $4$ does (weight $8$): $12+9+2=23$. Stopping at the split item would give $21$.
oc-f-493 · level teorema · notes
0-1 knapsack with large capacity $c=M$ and two items: $(p_1,w_1)=(2,1)$ and $(p_2,w_2)=(M,M)$. The greedy by efficiency $GR$ takes item $1$ first. What is the ratio $z(I)/GR(I)$?
  1. $\frac{M+2}{2}$, but bounded by $2$
  2. $\frac M2$, which grows without bound with $M$
  3. $2$, for every $M$
  4. $1$: $GR$ is optimal
Explanation. Item $1$ has efficiency $2>1$; after it item $2$ does not fit, so $GR(I)=2$ while $z(I)=M$. This is why $GR$ has no constant guarantee, and $GR^e$ adds the comparison with the best single item.
oc-f-494 · level facile · notes
The Ext-Greedy algorithm for the knapsack is $GR^e(I)=\max\{GR(I),\,p_{\max}\}$, where $GR$ is the greedy by efficiency and $p_{\max}$ the largest profit of an item (fitting alone). On the instance with $c=M$, items $(2,1)$ and $(M,M)$, what is $GR^e(I)$?
  1. $M$, the optimal value
  2. $2$, the value of $GR$
  3. $M+2$, the sum of the profits
  4. $\frac M2$
Explanation. $GR(I)=2$ and $p_{\max}=M$, hence $GR^e(I)=M=z(I)$: the comparison with the single item repairs exactly the case where $GR$ fails.
oc-f-495 · level facile · notes
For the 0-1 knapsack, the Ext-Greedy algorithm $GR^e(I)=\max\{GR(I),p_{\max}\}$ has ratio $z(I)/GR^e(I)\le 2$ on every instance. Which instance shows that $2$ cannot be improved?
  1. $c=M$ and two items $(2,1)$, $(M,M)$: $GR^e$ is optimal
  2. All items equal: $GR^e$ is always optimal
  3. A single item: the ratio is $1$
  4. $c=2M$ and three items $(2,1)$, $(M,M)$, $(M,M)$: $GR^e$ is about $M$, the optimum $2M$
Explanation. The greedy takes $(2,1)$ and one of the items $(M,M)$, then the other does not fit: $GR=M+2$; $p_{\max}=M$; the optimum takes the two large items, $2M$. The ratio $\frac{2M}{M+2}$ tends to $2$.
oc-f-496 · level facile · notes
The $H^2$ algorithm for the knapsack tries every feasible pair of items $(i,k)$, $i<k$. After inserting $i$ and $k$, on which items does it run $GR^e$ to complete the solution?
  1. Only on the items more efficient than $i$ and $k$
  2. On the items with profit larger than $\max\{p_i,p_k\}$
  3. On the other items with profit at most $\min\{p_i,p_k\}$ and weight at most the residual capacity
  4. On all the other items, whatever their profit
Explanation. $H^2$ guesses the two items of largest profit of an optimal solution: the residual items must therefore have no larger profit. The result is the maximum over all completions and $p_{\max}$.
oc-f-497 · level facile · notes
The $H^2$ algorithm for the knapsack with $n$ items tries $O(n^2)$ pairs of items and for each runs $GR^e$ on the residual items. How much time does it take?
  1. $O(n^4)$, for every implementation
  2. $O(n^3)$, and $O(n^2)$ with a more careful implementation
  3. $O(n\log n)$, as the greedy
  4. $O(2^n)$, because it tries subsets
Explanation. With the items already sorted by efficiency, every completion costs $O(n)$ and the pairs are $O(n^2)$; a more careful implementation goes down to $O(n^2)$. In exchange the guarantee drops from $2$ to $\frac43$.
oc-f-498 · level facile · notes
The $H^{\ell}$ algorithm for the knapsack tries all sets of $\ell$ items and completes them with the greedy; it has worst-case ratio $\frac{\ell+2}{\ell+1}$ and time $O(n^{\ell+1})$. What are the guarantee and the time with $\ell=4$?
  1. $\frac65$ and $O(n^5)$
  2. $\frac54$ and $O(n^4)$
  3. $\frac65$ and $O(n^4)$
  4. $\frac43$ and $O(n^5)$
Explanation. $\frac{4+2}{4+1}=\frac65$ and $n^{4+1}=n^5$. Every extra item in the «guess» improves the guarantee and multiplies the time by $n$.
oc-f-499 · level facile · notes
For the knapsack, $H^{\varepsilon}$ is $H^{\ell}$ with $\ell=\lceil 1/\varepsilon\rceil-2$ (at least $2$ and at most $n$), and $H^{\ell}$ has ratio $\frac{\ell+2}{\ell+1}$. With $\varepsilon=\frac1{10}$, which $\ell$ is used and which ratio is guaranteed?
  1. $\ell=10$ and ratio $\frac{12}{11}$
  2. $\ell=8$ and ratio $\frac98$
  3. $\ell=9$ and ratio $\frac{11}{10}$
  4. $\ell=8$ and ratio $\frac{10}{9}$
Explanation. $\ell=10-2=8$ and $\frac{8+2}{8+1}=\frac{10}{9}$, i.e., relative error at most $\frac1{10}$. The time $O(n^{\ell+1})$ is polynomial for fixed $\varepsilon$: the family is a PTAS.
oc-f-500 · level facile · notes
The Basic-FPTAS $FP^{\varepsilon}$ for the knapsack guarantees $z(I)/FP^{\varepsilon}(I)\le\frac1{1-\varepsilon}$. With $\varepsilon=0.2$, which guarantee follows, and which relative error?
  1. Ratio at most $\frac54$, relative error at most $25\%$
  2. Ratio at most $5$, relative error at most $80\%$
  3. Ratio at most $\frac54$, relative error at most $20\%$
  4. Ratio at most $\frac65$, relative error at most $20\%$
Explanation. $\frac1{1-0.2}=\frac54$; in terms of error $\frac{z-FP}{z}\le\varepsilon=20\%$. $\frac65=1+\varepsilon$ would be the guarantee of a minimization problem.
oc-f-501 · level facile · notes
The Basic-FPTAS for the knapsack replaces the profits $p_j$ with scaled profits $\lfloor p_j/K\rfloor$ and solves the scaled instance to optimality with dynamic programming by profits. Why does the time become polynomial in $n$ and $\frac1\varepsilon$?
  1. Because rounding the profits makes the greedy optimal
  2. The scaled profits are small (of order $n/\varepsilon$), and the DP by profits costs in proportion to the total profit
  3. Because the DP on the capacity does not depend on the profits
  4. Because the scaled instance has fewer items
Explanation. With $K$ proportional to $\varepsilon$ and to a bound on the optimum divided by $n$, the scaled profits are bounded by a polynomial in $n$ and $\frac1\varepsilon$; the DP by profits then takes $O(n^2/\varepsilon)$ steps.
oc-f-502 · level ipotesi · notes
The theorem on the absolute guarantee for the knapsack (integer profits) states that an algorithm with $z(I)-\mathscr{A}(I)\le k$ on every instance would solve every instance to optimality in the same time. Which construction is used in the proof?
  1. Multiply all profits by $k+1$: two different values differ by at least $k+1$, so an error at most $k$ is zero
  2. Divide all profits by $k$ and round down
  3. Add $k$ dummy items of zero profit
  4. Reduce the capacity by $k$ units
Explanation. On instance $I'$ with profits $(k+1)p_j$ the solutions are the same, $z(I')=(k+1)z(I)$, and the values are multiples of $k+1$: an error at most $k$ must be $0$, and the solution is optimal for $I$ too.

36. Approximation algorithms for bin packing (12)

oc-f-122 · level teorema · notes
In bin packing (BPP), Next Fit (NF), Worst Fit (WF), First Fit (FF) and Best Fit (BF) pack the items one at a time in the given order. What are their worst-case performance ratios $r(\cdot)$?
  1. $r(NF)=2$ and $r(WF)=r(FF)=r(BF)=\frac{17}{10}$
  2. $r(NF)=r(WF)=r(FF)=r(BF)=2$
  3. $r(NF)=\frac{17}{10}$ and $r(FF)=r(BF)=\frac32$
  4. $r(NF)=r(WF)=2$ and $r(FF)=r(BF)=\frac{17}{10}$
Explanation. All Any Fit algorithms have the half-full property, hence they are $2$-approximation algorithms; WF asymptotically attains $2$ as NF does, while FF and BF have $\frac{17}{10}$.
oc-f-123 · level ipotesi · notes
In bin packing (capacity $c$, weights $w_i$, optimum $z(I)$), a solution with $k$ bins has the half-full property if at most one bin has total weight at most $c/2$. Which facts are needed to prove that an algorithm with this property is a $2$-approximation?
  1. Every item weighs more than $c/2$; every solution with $z(I)$ bins has $\sum_i w_i\le z(I)\,c$; $k$ is an integer
  2. The items are sorted by non-increasing weight; $z(I)\ge\lceil\sum_i w_i/c\rceil$; $k$ is an integer
  3. The property gives $\sum_i w_i>(k-1)\frac c2$; every solution with $z(I)$ bins has $\sum_i w_i\le z(I)\,c$; $k$ is an integer
  4. The property gives $\sum_i w_i>(k-1)\frac c2$; the LP relaxation equals $\sum_i w_i/c$ and its dual has an integer solution
Explanation. From the two inequalities $(k-1)\frac c2<z(I)\,c$, i.e., $k<2z(I)+1$, and by integrality $k\le 2z(I)$.
oc-f-124 · level ipotesi · notes
In bin packing, an algorithm is Any Fit if it opens a new bin only when the current item fits in none of the bins already open. Which fact is used to prove that its solutions have the half-full property (at most one bin with weight at most $c/2$)?
  1. The number of bins is at most $\frac{17}{10}z(I)$, hence few bins are empty
  2. The content of two bins never fits into one, whatever the bin selection rule
  3. The rule always chooses the bin with minimum residual capacity, which fills up first
  4. The items are sorted by non-increasing weight, hence the first bins are full
Explanation. If two bins both had weight at most $c/2$ they could be merged, but the Any Fit condition excludes it. NF is not Any Fit, but it still has guarantee $2$.
oc-f-125 · level facile · notes
In bin packing, Next Fit (NF) keeps a single bin open and, if the item does not fit, closes it and opens a new one. An algorithm is Any Fit if it opens a new bin only when the item fits in none of the previous ones. Why is NF not Any Fit?
  1. It may open a bin for an item that would fit into a closed bin
  2. It sorts the items by non-increasing weight before packing them
  3. It always chooses the bin with maximum residual capacity
  4. It may exceed the capacity of the open bin
Explanation. An Any Fit algorithm opens a new bin only if the item fits into none of the bins opened so far; NF keeps a single open bin.
oc-f-503 · level facile · notes
Bin packing with capacity $c=10$ and items of weight $6,5,4,3,2$ in this order. Next Fit (NF) keeps a single bin open and opens a new one when the item does not fit; First Fit (FF) puts each item into the first open bin where it fits. How many bins do they use?
  1. NF $3$, FF $3$
  2. NF $4$, FF $2$
  3. NF $3$, FF $2$
  4. NF $2$, FF $2$
Explanation. NF: $\{6\}$, $\{5,4\}$, $\{3,2\}$. FF: $\{6,4\}$, $\{5,3,2\}$: the item of weight $4$ goes back to the first bin, which NF has already closed.
oc-f-504 · level facile · notes
In bin packing with $n$ items, Next Fit (NF) keeps a single bin open at a time. How much time does it take, and why?
  1. $O(2^n)$: all partitions are tried
  2. $O(n)$: every item is compared only with the open bin
  3. $O(n^2)$: every item is compared with all bins
  4. $O(n\log n)$: the items must be sorted
Explanation. One check per item: if it fits it goes into the open bin, otherwise a new bin is opened. The speed is paid with poorly filled bins.
oc-f-505 · level facile · notes
Bin packing with capacity $c=10$: three open bins have residual capacities $2$, $5$ and $3$, and an item of weight $3$ arrives. Where do First Fit (first that fits), Best Fit (smallest sufficient residual) and Worst Fit (largest residual) put it?
  1. FF in the $2$nd, BF in the $3$rd, WF in the $2$nd
  2. FF in the $1$st, BF in the $1$st, WF in the $2$nd
  3. FF in the $3$rd, BF in the $3$rd, WF in the $2$nd
  4. FF in the $2$nd, BF in the $2$nd, WF in the $3$rd
Explanation. The first bin (residual $2$) is not enough. FF takes the second, BF the smallest sufficient residual ($3$, the third), WF the largest ($5$, the second). All three are Any Fit algorithms.
oc-f-506 · level facile · notes
In bin packing a solution has the half-full property if at most one bin has total weight at most $c/2$. With $c=10$, a solution has loads $(4,4,7)$. Can it have been produced by an Any Fit algorithm (which opens a new bin only if the item fits in none of the previous ones)?
  1. Yes: every feasible solution is obtained with some order of the items
  2. Yes, but only with Worst Fit
  3. No, because the total load $15$ is not a multiple of $c$
  4. No: two bins have load at most $5$, and the content of the second would have fitted into the first
Explanation. Any Fit solutions have the half-full property: when the second lightly loaded bin was opened, its first item fitted into the other one. The loads $4$ and $4$ violate the property.
oc-f-507 · level ipotesi · notes
Next Fit (NF) is not an Any Fit algorithm, yet it has $NF(I)\le 2\,z(I)$ in bin packing. Which fact is used in the proof?
  1. NF sorts the items by non-increasing weight
  2. The content of any two bins does not fit into one
  3. Two consecutive NF bins together weigh more than $c$
  4. Every NF bin weighs more than $c/2$
Explanation. NF opens bin $b+1$ only because its first item did not fit into bin $b$: consecutive pairs exceed $c$, and adding up gives the guarantee $2$.
oc-f-508 · level facile · notes
In bin packing, a solution with $k$ bins and the half-full property (at most one bin with weight at most $c/2$) satisfies $\sum_j w_j>(k-1)\frac c2$. With $\sum_j w_j=23$ and $c=10$, how many bins can an Any Fit algorithm use at most?
  1. $3$
  2. $5$
  3. $6$
  4. $4$
Explanation. $23>(k-1)\cdot 5$ gives $k-1\le 4$, i.e., $k\le 5$. The weight bound gives $z(I)\ge\lceil 2.3\rceil=3$, consistent with the guarantee $k\le 2z(I)$.
oc-f-509 · level facile · notes
First Fit, Best Fit and Worst Fit are all Any Fit algorithms for bin packing, so they have the half-full property and ratio at most $2$. What does the fact that $r(WF)=2$ while $r(FF)=r(BF)=\frac{17}{10}$ teach?
  1. The Any Fit condition alone gives only the guarantee $2$: the bin selection rule matters
  2. Worst Fit is not an Any Fit algorithm
  3. First Fit and Best Fit violate the half-full property
  4. The guarantees depend only on the order of the items
Explanation. The half-full property holds for every Any Fit rule and gives $2$; going down to $\frac{17}{10}$ requires knowing which bin is chosen, the first or the fullest.
oc-f-510 · level facile · notes
Next Fit (NF) for bin packing has worst-case ratio $r(NF)=2$. Which kind of instance approaches it?
  1. Items all of weight $c$: every algorithm uses one bin per item
  2. Items sorted by non-increasing weight
  3. Items all of weight $1$, with large $c$
  4. Items alternating weight about $c/2$ and very small weight: NF closes every bin half empty
Explanation. With weights $\frac c2,\epsilon,\frac c2,\epsilon,\dots$ NF puts one large and one small item into every bin, while the optimum pairs the large items and gathers the small ones in few bins.

37. Vertex coloring: limits of approximation (12)

oc-f-126 · level teorema · notes
In the vertex coloring problem (VCP) on a graph $G$ with $n$ vertices the number of colors is minimized. If $P\neq NP$, which statement on the approximation of the VCP is correct?
  1. It has no polynomial $\alpha$-approximation for any constant $\alpha\ge 1$
  2. It has a polynomial $\frac43$-approximation, but none below $\frac43$
  3. It has a polynomial $2$-approximation, the greedy coloring
  4. It has a $(\Delta(G)+1)$-approximation for every graph, $DSATUR$
Explanation. The $\frac43$ theorem excludes the ratios below $\frac43$; Zuckerman's result also excludes $n^{1-\varepsilon}$, hence any constant. The bound $GC(I)\le\Delta(G)+1$ does not compare with $\chi(G)$.
oc-f-127 · level ipotesi · notes
In vertex coloring, let $\chi(G)$ be the chromatic number. The proof that no polynomial algorithm $\mathscr{A}$ has ratio $\mathscr{A}(I)/\chi(G)$ smaller than $\frac43$ on every graph (if $P\neq NP$) uses:
  1. that deciding whether $\chi(G)\le 2$ is NP-complete, and that the number of colors is an integer
  2. that deciding whether $\chi(G)\le 4$ is NP-complete, and that planar graphs have $\chi(G)\le 4$
  3. that the greedy coloring uses at most $\Delta(G)+1$ colors, and that the number of colors is an integer
  4. that deciding whether $\chi(G)\le 3$ is NP-complete, and that the number of colors is an integer
Explanation. With ratio $<\frac43$, a graph with $\chi\le 3$ would get at most $3$ colors and one with $\chi\ge 4$ at least $4$: 3-colorability would be decided in polynomial time. With $2$ colors the argument says nothing, because that test is easy.
oc-f-128 · level facile · notes
In an interval graph every vertex $v$ is an interval $[a_v,b_v]$ of the line, and two vertices are adjacent if the intervals intersect. With which vertex order is the greedy coloring (smallest free color) optimal?
  1. By non-increasing length $b_v-a_v$
  2. With any vertex order
  3. By non-decreasing left endpoint $a_v$
  4. By non-increasing right endpoint $b_v$
Explanation. With the intervals sorted by left endpoint, when an interval receives color $q$ there are $q$ intervals intersecting at $a_v$: the coloring uses $\omega(G)$ colors, hence it is optimal.
oc-f-129 · level facile · notes
A graph $G$ with $n$ vertices and $m$ edges is bipartite if and only if its chromatic number is $\chi(G)\le 2$. How much does it cost to decide whether $G$ is bipartite and, if so, to find a coloring with $2$ colors?
  1. $O(nm)$, with a shortest path from every vertex
  2. $O(n+m)$, with a breadth-first search
  3. $O(n^3)$, with linear programming
  4. $O(2^n)$: the problem is NP-complete
Explanation. A breadth-first search colors alternating two colors and finds an odd cycle if the graph is not bipartite. Already with $3$ colors the problem is NP-complete.
oc-f-511 · level ipotesi · notes
The proof that no polynomial algorithm for coloring has ratio $\mathscr{A}(I)/\chi(G)<\frac43$ on every graph (if $P\neq NP$) uses a hypothetical algorithm with ratio $<\frac43$. What would it do on a graph with $\chi(G)=3$?
  1. It would use exactly $4$ colors, which says nothing
  2. It would use $2$ colors, contradicting $\chi(G)=3$
  3. It would not terminate, because the problem is NP-hard
  4. It would use fewer than $4$ colors, i.e., $3$: it would decide in polynomial time whether $\chi(G)\le 3$
Explanation. With ratio $<\frac43$ and $\chi(G)=3$ the number of colors is $<4$, hence $3$; if $\chi(G)\ge 4$ it is at least $4$. 3-colorability, NP-complete, would be decided: only the integrality of $\chi$ is used.
oc-f-512 · level facile · notes
The same argument excluding ratios below $\frac43$ for coloring (distinguishing $3$ colors from $4$) does not exclude ratios below $\frac32$ by distinguishing $2$ colors from $3$. Why?
  1. Because $\frac32>\frac43$
  2. Because with $2$ colors the greedy coloring is always optimal
  3. Deciding whether $\chi(G)\le 2$ is polynomial (bipartite graphs), so no contradiction arises
  4. Because no graph has $\chi(G)=2$
Explanation. The argument needs an NP-complete decision problem; 2-colorability is decided by breadth-first search in $O(n+m)$.
oc-f-513 · level facile · notes
For vertex coloring, giving every vertex a different color is always feasible. Which ratio does this trivial algorithm guarantee, and what does Zuckerman's theorem say (if $P\neq NP$)?
  1. Ratio at most $\log n$; an algorithm with constant ratio exists
  2. Ratio at most $n$; no polynomial algorithm guarantees $n^{1-\varepsilon}$ for any $\varepsilon>0$
  3. Ratio at most $2$; no polynomial algorithm guarantees less than $2$
  4. Ratio at most $\Delta+1$; no polynomial algorithm guarantees less than $\frac43$
Explanation. $n$ colors against $\chi(G)\ge 1$ give ratio at most $n$. The theorem says one cannot do substantially better: in particular the problem has no constant ratio.
oc-f-514 · level facile · notes
Every planar graph with $n\ge 3$ vertices has at most $3n-6$ edges and hence a vertex of degree at most $5$. How many edges does a planar graph with $n=10$ vertices have at most?
  1. $24$
  2. $30$
  3. $45$
  4. $27$
Explanation. $3\cdot 10-6=24$. The sum of the degrees is at most $48<6n$, so some vertex has degree at most $5$: it is the key step of the five color theorem.
oc-f-515 · level facile · notes
On planar graphs, the algorithm $PL$ colors with at most $5$ colors in polynomial time (Heawood's theorem). Which guarantee does it have with respect to the chromatic number $\chi(G)$, if the graph has at least one edge?
  1. Relative: $PL(I)/\chi(G)\le\frac43$
  2. None: planar graphs are as hard as all others
  3. Additive: $PL(I)-\chi(G)\le 0$, i.e., $PL$ is exact
  4. Additive: $PL(I)-\chi(G)\le 2$, recognizing separately the graphs with $\chi(G)\le 2$
Explanation. With at least one edge $\chi(G)\ge 2$; bipartite graphs are recognized and colored in $O(n+m)$, and on the others $\chi(G)\ge 3$, hence $5-\chi(G)\le 2$. With the four color theorem the error drops to $1$.
oc-f-516 · level facile · notes
The greedy coloring uses at most $\Delta(G)+1$ colors, with $\Delta(G)$ the maximum degree. On graphs of maximum degree at most $3$ with at least one edge, which ratio with respect to $\chi(G)$ does it guarantee?
  1. At most $\frac43$, because $\chi(G)\ge 3$
  2. Exactly $1$: on these graphs it is optimal
  3. At most $2$, because it uses at most $4$ colors and $\chi(G)\ge 2$
  4. At most $4$, because it uses at most $4$ colors and $\chi(G)\ge 1$
Explanation. $\frac{\Delta+1}{\chi(G)}\le\frac42=2$: on bounded-degree graphs the greedy coloring has a constant ratio, while on general graphs no polynomial algorithm has one.
oc-f-517 · level facile · notes
Interval graph with vertices $[1,4]$, $[2,5]$, $[3,6]$, $[7,8]$ (two vertices adjacent if the intervals intersect). The greedy coloring with the vertices by non-decreasing left endpoint uses $\omega(G)$ colors. How many colors are needed?
  1. $1$
  2. $3$
  3. $4$
  4. $2$
Explanation. The first three intervals overlap at the point $3.5$ and form a clique, so $\omega=3$; $[7,8]$ intersects none and reuses color $1$. The greedy gives $3=\chi(G)$.
oc-f-518 · level facile · notes
Zuckerman's theorem excludes, if $P\neq NP$, the ratios $n^{1-\varepsilon}$ for coloring. Why does it also exclude every constant ratio $\alpha$?
  1. On graphs with large enough $n$, $\alpha<n^{1-\varepsilon}$: an $\alpha$-approximation would have ratio below the forbidden threshold
  2. Because every constant is below $\frac43$
  3. Because constant ratios hold only on planar graphs
  4. It does not: $\alpha$-approximations with large $\alpha$ exist
Explanation. For fixed $\varepsilon$, when $n>\alpha^{1/(1-\varepsilon)}$ we have $\alpha<n^{1-\varepsilon}$; the small graphs, finitely many, are solved optimally in constant time.

38. Maximum clique: limits of approximation (12)

oc-f-130 · level teorema · notes
In the strong product $G\boxtimes H$ the vertices are the pairs $(u,x)$, and two distinct pairs are adjacent if each component is equal or adjacent in its own graph. With $\omega$ the cardinality of a maximum clique, which statement is correct?
  1. $\omega(G\boxtimes H)=\max\{\omega(G),\omega(H)\}$
  2. $\omega(G\boxtimes H)=\omega(G)^{\omega(H)}$
  3. $\omega(G\boxtimes H)=\omega(G)\,\omega(H)$
  4. $\omega(G\boxtimes H)=\omega(G)+\omega(H)$
Explanation. The product of two cliques is a clique of the strong product, and a clique of the product has at most $|p(K)|\cdot\max_u|K_u|$ vertices: the clique number multiplies.
oc-f-131 · level ipotesi · notes
In the maximum clique problem (MCP), with $\omega(G)$ the cardinality of a maximum clique and $G\boxtimes G$ the strong product of $G$ with itself, the theorem «a constant $\alpha$-approximation for the MCP would give a $\beta$-approximation for every $\beta>1$» is proved using:
  1. $\omega(G)=\alpha(\overline{G})$; from a stable set of $\overline{G}$ one extracts a clique of $G$; the half-full property
  2. $\omega(G\boxtimes G)=\omega(G)^2$; from a clique $K$ of the product one extracts a clique of $G$ with at least $\sqrt{|K|}$ vertices; a constant number of products
  3. $\omega(G\boxtimes G)=2\,\omega(G)$; from a clique $K$ of the product one extracts a clique of $G$ with at least $|K|/2$ vertices; a constant number of products
  4. $\omega(G\boxtimes G)=\omega(G)^2$; from a clique $K$ of the product one extracts a clique of $G$ with at least $\sqrt{|K|}$ vertices; $\log n$ products
Explanation. Applying the algorithm to $G\boxtimes G$ gives ratio $\sqrt\alpha$ on $G$; repeating a constant number of times brings the ratio below any $\beta>1$. A number of products growing with $n$ would not be polynomial.
oc-f-132 · level facile · notes
