For the maximum clique problem (MCP) and the maximum stable set problem (MSSP) on a graph $G$ with $n$ vertices, with $\omega(G)$ the cardinality of a maximum clique, which statement on planar graphs is correct?
  1. $\omega(G)\le 4$ and the MCP is solved in $O(n^4)$, while the MSSP stays NP-hard
  2. $\omega(G)\le 4$ and the MCP is solved in $O(n^4)$; so is the MSSP, through the complement
  3. $\omega(G)\le 4$, but the MCP stays NP-hard like the MSSP
  4. $\omega(G)\le 5$ and the MCP is solved in $O(n^5)$, while the MSSP stays NP-hard
Explanation. A clique of a planar graph has at most $4$ vertices, so it is enough to enumerate the subsets of at most $4$ vertices. The complement of a planar graph is not planar: the easy case does not transfer to the MSSP.
oc-f-133 · level facile · notes
A graph has $n=24$ vertices and maximum degree $\Delta(G)=5$. A stable set is maximal if no vertex can be added to it. At least how many vertices does every maximal stable set have?
  1. $5$
  2. $6$
  3. $20$
  4. $4$
Explanation. A maximal stable set $S$ has $|S|\ge\frac{n}{\Delta(G)+1}=\frac{24}{6}=4$: every vertex is in $S$ or adjacent to a vertex of $S$, and every vertex of $S$ covers at most $\Delta+1$ vertices.
oc-f-519 · level facile · notes
In the strong product $G\boxtimes H$ the vertices are the pairs $(u,x)$ with $u$ a vertex of $G$ and $x$ of $H$. How many vertices does $G\boxtimes G$ have if $G$ has $5$ vertices, and what is its maximum clique if $\omega(G)=2$?
  1. $25$ vertices, $\omega(G\boxtimes G)=8$
  2. $25$ vertices, $\omega(G\boxtimes G)=4$
  3. $10$ vertices, $\omega(G\boxtimes G)=4$
  4. $25$ vertices, $\omega(G\boxtimes G)=2$
Explanation. The vertices are $5\cdot 5=25$, and $\omega(G\boxtimes H)=\omega(G)\,\omega(H)$: $2\cdot 2=4$. The product is built in polynomial time.
oc-f-520 · level facile · notes
From a clique $K$ of the strong product $G\boxtimes G$ a clique of $G$ is obtained: either the projection $p(K)$ (the first components) or the largest fibre (the second components with the same first one). If $|K|=16$, how many vertices does the obtained clique of $G$ have at least?
  1. $4$
  2. $8$
  3. $16$
  4. $2$
Explanation. $|K|\le|p(K)|\cdot\max_u|K_u|$, so the larger of the two factors is at least $\sqrt{|K|}=4$. It is the constructive step of the amplification.
oc-f-521 · level facile · notes
In the maximum clique problem, an algorithm with ratio $\omega(G)/\mathscr{A}(I)\le\alpha$ applied to $G\boxtimes G$ gives a clique $K$ of the product, from which a clique of $G$ with at least $\sqrt{|K|}$ vertices is obtained. Which ratio follows on $G$ with $\alpha=4$?
  1. $4$
  2. $16$
  3. $\frac43$
  4. $2$
Explanation. $|K|\ge\omega(G)^2/\alpha$, so the obtained clique has at least $\omega(G)/\sqrt\alpha$ vertices: ratio $\sqrt 4=2$. Repeating the products the ratio drops below every constant larger than $1$.
oc-f-522 · level facile · notes
In the maximum clique problem, a single vertex is always a clique. Which ratio $\omega(G)/\mathscr{A}(I)$ does the algorithm returning one vertex guarantee, and what does the Håstad–Zuckerman theorem say (if $P\neq NP$)?
  1. At most $\Delta+1$; a polynomial algorithm with ratio $\frac43$ exists
  2. At most $\sqrt n$; a PTAS exists
  3. At most $n$; no polynomial algorithm guarantees $n^{1-\varepsilon}$ for any $\varepsilon>0$
  4. At most $2$; no polynomial algorithm guarantees less than $2$
Explanation. $\omega(G)\le n$, so one vertex gives ratio at most $n$; the theorem says one cannot do substantially better, and in particular excludes every constant ratio.
oc-f-523 · level facile · notes
In the maximum clique problem on graphs of maximum degree $\Delta$, a maximum clique is found exactly in $O(n\,2^{\Delta}\Delta^2)$ time, examining the subsets of the neighbours of every vertex. When is this time polynomial in $n$?
  1. Only on planar graphs
  2. For every fixed $\Delta$, and even when $\Delta\le\log_2 n$
  3. Only for $\Delta\le 3$
  4. Never: $2^{\Delta}$ is always exponential
Explanation. With $\Delta\le\log_2 n$ we have $2^{\Delta}\le n$, and the time is $O(n^2\log^2 n)$. A clique contains a vertex and some of its neighbours, so examining the $2^{\Delta}$ subsets of the neighbourhood is enough.
oc-f-524 · level facile · notes
For the maximum stable set on a graph with maximum degree $\Delta(G)$, the greedy adding vertices while possible finds a maximal stable set $S$ with $|S|\ge\frac{n}{\Delta(G)+1}$. Which ratio $\alpha(G)/|S|$ does it guarantee with $\Delta(G)=3$?
  1. At most $4$
  2. At most $3$
  3. At most $\frac43$
  4. No constant ratio
Explanation. $\alpha(G)\le n$ and $|S|\ge\frac n4$ give $\alpha(G)/|S|\le 4=\Delta+1$. Every added vertex excludes at most $\Delta$ neighbours.
oc-f-525 · level facile · notes
The maximum clique on $G$ is the maximum stable set on the complement $\overline G$, so the two problems have the same approximation limits on general graphs. Why are the easy special cases of the clique (planar graphs, bounded degree) not easy for the stable set?
  1. Because the maximum stable set is always polynomial
  2. Because the complement of a planar graph is still planar
  3. Because on planar graphs $\alpha(G)=\omega(G)$
  4. The complement of a planar or bounded-degree graph is dense: on the original class the stable set stays NP-hard
Explanation. Passing to the complement changes the class of graphs. On planar graphs the maximum stable set stays NP-hard, but it is approximable (it even has a PTAS).
oc-f-526 · level facile · notes
In the maximum clique problem on a planar graph with $n$ vertices, why is it enough to examine the subsets of at most $4$ vertices?
  1. Because every planar graph is bipartite
  2. Because planar graphs have at most $4n$ edges
  3. Every planar graph has $\omega(G)\le 4$ ($K_5$ is not planar): $O(n^4)$ subsets are checked
  4. Because every planar graph has maximum degree at most $4$
Explanation. A clique of $5$ vertices would be a $K_5$, which is not planar; the subsets of at most $4$ vertices are $O(n^4)$ and each is checked in constant time.

39. The TSP: the general case and the metric case (12)

oc-f-134 · level teorema · notes
For the TSP (shortest tour visiting all cities), $DT$ is the double tree algorithm and $CH$ Christofides' algorithm; the TSP is metric if the triangle inequality holds. If $P\neq NP$, which statement is correct?
  1. The general TSP has no constant approximation; on the metric TSP $DT$ has ratio $\frac32$ and $CH$ ratio $2$
  2. The general TSP has a $2$-approximation, $DT$; on the metric TSP $CH$ has ratio $\frac32$
  3. The general TSP has no constant approximation; on the metric TSP the nearest neighbour has ratio $\frac32$
  4. The general TSP has no constant approximation; on the metric TSP $DT$ has ratio $2$ and $CH$ ratio $\frac32$
Explanation. A constant guarantee on the general TSP would decide the Hamiltonian cycle problem; with the triangle inequality $DT$ and $CH$ have ratios $2$ and $\frac32$, both tight.
oc-f-135 · level ipotesi · notes
In the metric TSP, the double tree algorithm $DT$ doubles a minimum spanning tree $T$ (of length $d(T)$), follows an Eulerian circuit of it and skips the cities already visited. With $z(I)$ the length of the optimal tour, which facts are needed to prove $DT(I)\le 2\,z(I)$?
  1. $d(T)\le z(I)/2$; the Eulerian circuit of the doubled tree has length $2d(T)$; distances are symmetric
  2. The nearest neighbour tour has length at most $2z(I)$; every city has even degree; distances are symmetric
  3. $d(T)\le z(I)$; the Eulerian circuit of the doubled tree has length $2d(T)$; shortcuts do not lengthen the walk
  4. $d(T)\le z(I)$; the minimum perfect matching on the odd-degree cities costs at most $z(I)/2$; shortcuts do not lengthen the walk
Explanation. Removing an edge from the optimal tour gives a spanning tree, hence $d(T)\le z(I)$; shortcutting uses the triangle inequality. The matching is needed for $CH$.
oc-f-136 · level ipotesi · notes
In the metric TSP, Christofides' algorithm $CH$ adds to a minimum spanning tree $T$ a minimum perfect matching $M$ on the odd-degree cities of $T$, follows an Eulerian circuit of $T\cup M$ and skips the cities already visited. Which facts are needed to prove $CH(I)\le\frac32\,z(I)$?
  1. $d(T)\le z(I)$; the doubled tree circuit has length $2d(T)$; distances are symmetric
  2. $d(T)\le z(I)$; $d(M)\le z(I)/2$; shortcuts do not lengthen the Eulerian circuit of $T\cup M$
  3. $d(T)\le z(I)/2$; $d(M)\le z(I)$; shortcuts do not lengthen the Eulerian circuit of $T\cup M$
  4. $d(T)\le z(I)$; $d(M)\le d(T)$; the odd-degree cities of $T$ are odd in number
Explanation. The Eulerian circuit of $T\cup M$ has length $d(T)+d(M)\le z(I)+\frac{z(I)}2$; the odd-degree cities of a tree are even in number, so the perfect matching exists.
oc-f-527 · level ipotesi · notes
The proof that the general TSP has no polynomial $\alpha$-approximation (if $P\neq NP$) starts from a graph $G$ asking whether a Hamiltonian cycle exists. Which TSP instance is built?
  1. Euclidean distances between the vertices of $G$ drawn in the plane
  2. Distance $2$ on the edges and $1$ on non-adjacent pairs
  3. Distance $1$ on the edges of $G$ and $\alpha n+1$ on non-adjacent pairs: an $\alpha$-approximation would separate tours of length $n$ from the others
  4. Distance $1$ on all pairs: every tour is optimal
Explanation. If $G$ is Hamiltonian the optimum is $n$ and the algorithm returns at most $\alpha n$, hence only edges of $G$; otherwise every tour uses a non-adjacent pair and exceeds $\alpha n$. An NP-complete problem would be decided.
oc-f-528 · level facile · notes
A TSP instance is metric if $d_{ij}\le d_{ik}+d_{kj}$ for all triples. Which of the following families of instances is always metric?
  1. Instances with distances drawn at random in $[1,100]$
  2. Cities in the plane with Euclidean distances
  3. Instances with arbitrary integer distances
  4. Instances with $d_{ij}\in\{1,2\}$ and $d_{ij}\ne d_{ji}$
Explanation. The Euclidean distance satisfies the triangle inequality. Symmetric distances $1$ and $2$ are metric, but here they are asymmetric; random distances can violate it.
oc-f-529 · level facile · notes
The general TSP has no constant approximation (if $P\neq NP$), the metric TSP does. What is known about the complexity of the metric TSP?
  1. It stays NP-hard, but admits algorithms with constant ratio such as $2$ or $\frac32$
  2. It is polynomial, because the triangle inequality allows shortcuts
  3. It is polynomial on Euclidean instances, NP-hard on the others
  4. It has no constant approximation either
Explanation. The metric does not make the problem easy, but it allows turning closed walks into tours without making them longer: it is the basis of the double tree and Christofides.
oc-f-530 · level facile · notes
In the metric TSP, the double tree algorithm $DT$ computes a minimum spanning tree $T$, doubles its edges, follows an Eulerian circuit and skips the cities already visited. If $d(T)=7$, what is known about the $DT$ tour and the optimal tour $z(I)$?
  1. $DT(I)=14$ and $z(I)=7$
  2. $DT(I)\le 7$ and $z(I)\ge 14$
  3. $DT(I)\le\frac{21}{2}$ and $z(I)\ge 7$
  4. $DT(I)\le 14$ and $z(I)\ge 7$
Explanation. The Eulerian circuit of the doubled tree has length $2d(T)=14$ and shortcuts do not lengthen it; removing an edge from the optimal tour gives a tree, so $d(T)\le z(I)$.
oc-f-531 · level facile · notes
In the Christofides algorithm for the metric TSP, a minimum perfect matching on the cities of odd degree in the minimum spanning tree $T$ is added to $T$. In a star with a centre and $3$ leaves (degree of the centre $3$, of the leaves $1$), how many such cities are there?
  1. $1$
  2. $0$
  3. $4$
  4. $3$
Explanation. The centre has degree $3$ and the three leaves degree $1$: all four are odd. In every graph the number of odd-degree vertices is even, so the perfect matching exists.
oc-f-532 · level ipotesi · notes
For every TSP instance, metric or not, the length $d(T)$ of a minimum spanning tree is at most the length $z(I)$ of the optimal tour. Which argument proves it?
  1. Doubling the tree gives the optimal tour
  2. Removing an edge from the optimal tour gives a path through all cities, i.e., a spanning tree, of length at most $z(I)$
  3. The triangle inequality allows shortening the tree
  4. The optimal tour always contains the minimum spanning tree
Explanation. The path is a spanning tree, and $T$ is the shortest spanning tree; distances are positive, so removing an edge does not lengthen. The metric is not needed.
oc-f-533 · level ipotesi · notes
In the metric TSP, let $O$ be an even set of cities and $M$ a minimum perfect matching of $O$. Why is $d(M)\le z(I)/2$?
  1. The optimal tour, shortcut to the cities of $O$ only, splits into two perfect matchings of $O$ with alternate edges; the shorter one has length at most $z(I)/2$
  2. Because $M$ has half the edges of the optimal tour
  3. Because every edge of $M$ is shorter than every edge of the tour
  4. Because $M$ is contained in the minimum spanning tree
Explanation. By the triangle inequality the tour restricted to $O$ is not longer than $z(I)$; its edges, taken alternately, form two perfect matchings, and the cheaper one is at most half.
oc-f-534 · level facile · notes
In the metric TSP, the Christofides algorithm uses a minimum spanning tree $T$ with $d(T)=10$ and a minimum perfect matching $M$ with $d(M)=4$, and returns a tour of length $CH(I)=14$. What is known about the optimal tour $z(I)$?
  1. $z(I)=14$
  2. $\frac{28}{3}\le z(I)\le 10$
  3. $z(I)\ge 14$
  4. $10\le z(I)\le 14$
Explanation. The $CH$ tour is feasible, so $z(I)\le 14$; $d(T)\le z(I)$ gives $z(I)\ge 10$ (stronger than $14/\frac32\approx 9.33$ and than $2d(M)=8$).
oc-f-535 · level facile · notes
In the metric TSP there are instances $I_n$, with $n\ge 5$ odd, on which the double tree $DT$ has ratio exactly $\frac{2n}{n+2}$. What is it with $n=7$, and what does it tend to?
  1. $\frac{7}{9}$, and it tends to $1$
  2. $2$ for every $n$
  3. $\frac{14}{9}$, and it tends to $2$
  4. $\frac{14}{9}$, and it tends to $\frac32$
Explanation. $\frac{2\cdot 7}{7+2}=\frac{14}{9}\approx 1.56$; as $n\to+\infty$ the ratio tends to $2$, so the guarantee $2$ of $DT$ cannot be improved.

40. Approximation for $P\|C_{\max}$ (12)

oc-f-137 · level ipotesi · notes
In scheduling $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ ($m$ machines, durations $t_j$, optimal makespan $z(I)$), $LIST$ assigns every job of the list to the least loaded machine. Let $\ell$ be a job finishing last, with start $S_\ell$. Which facts are needed to prove $LIST(I)\le\left(2-\frac1m\right)z(I)$?
  1. $\ell$ is the longest job; $z(I)=\frac1m\sum_j t_j$; $z(I)\ge t_\ell$
  2. The jobs are sorted by non-increasing duration; $z(I)\ge t_\ell$; $S_\ell\le z(I)$
  3. $S_\ell\le\frac1m\sum_{j\ne\ell}t_j$; $z(I)\ge\frac1m\sum_j t_j$; $z(I)\ge t_\ell$
  4. $S_\ell\le\frac1m\sum_{j\ne\ell}t_j$; $z(I)\ge\frac1m\sum_j t_j$; $z(I)\ge 2t_\ell$
Explanation. $LIST(I)=S_\ell+t_\ell\le\frac1m\sum_j t_j+\left(1-\frac1m\right)t_\ell\le\left(2-\frac1m\right)z(I)$. Sorting is needed only for the better guarantee of $LPT$.
oc-f-138 · level facile · notes
In scheduling $P\,||\,C_{\max}$, $LPT$ sorts the jobs by non-increasing duration and assigns each to the least loaded machine; its guarantee is $\frac43-\frac1{3m}$. What is it with $m=2$ machines?
  1. $\frac54$
  2. $\frac76$
  3. $\frac32$
  4. $\frac43$
Explanation. $\frac43-\frac1{3m}=\frac43-\frac16=\frac76$. With any list $LIST$ guarantees $2-\frac12=\frac32$.
oc-f-139 · level teorema · notes
In scheduling $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ with $m$ machines, $LIST$ (any list, least loaded machine) guarantees $2-\frac1m$ and $LPT$ (list by non-increasing duration) guarantees $\frac43-\frac1{3m}$. Which statement is correct?
  1. Both are tight: for every $m\ge 2$ some instances reach exactly the two ratios
  2. Both are tight only for $m=2$; for $m\ge 3$ the ratios are lower
  3. The guarantee of $LIST$ is tight, that of $LPT$ is not: its true ratio is lower
  4. Neither is reached: the ratios are only approached as $n$ grows
Explanation. Graham's instance families reach exactly the two ratios for every $m\ge 2$ (for $LPT$: $2m+1$ jobs, two of every duration from $2m-1$ down to $m$ plus one of duration $m$).
oc-f-536 · level facile · notes
Scheduling $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ with $m=3$ machines. $LIST$ assigns every job, in list order, to the least loaded machine (ties: smallest index); $LPT$ does the same with the list sorted by non-increasing time. With the list $t=(2,3,4,6,2,2)$, what are the makespans of $LIST$ and $LPT$?
  1. $7$ and $7$
  2. $8$ and $8$
  3. $9$ and $7$
  4. $8$ and $7$
Explanation. $LIST$: $2,3,4$ on the three machines, $6$ on the first ($8$), then $2$ on the second ($5$) and $2$ on the third ($6$): makespan $8$. $LPT$ with $(6,4,3,2,2,2)$ gives loads $(6,6,7)$, and $\lceil 19/3\rceil=7$ proves it optimal.
oc-f-537 · level facile · notes
In scheduling $P\,||\,C_{\max}$, $LIST$ assigns the jobs in the order of a given list and $LPT$ first sorts them by non-increasing time. Which practical difference is there between the two algorithms?
  1. $LPT$ uses the most loaded machine, $LIST$ the least loaded
  2. $LPT$ is exponential, $LIST$ polynomial
  3. $LIST$ can work online, assigning jobs as they arrive; $LPT$ must know all the jobs to sort them
  4. $LIST$ requires knowing all the jobs, $LPT$ does not
Explanation. Both use the least-loaded-machine rule; sorting costs $O(n\log n)$ and needs all the data, but lowers the guarantee from $2-\frac1m$ to $\frac43-\frac1{3m}$.
oc-f-538 · level teorema · notes
In scheduling $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ with $m$ machines, let $\ell$ be a job ending last in the $LIST$ schedule, with start $S_\ell$. Why is $S_\ell\le\frac1m\sum_{j\ne\ell}t_j$?
  1. Because $S_\ell$ is always zero
  2. $\ell$ goes on the least loaded machine, whose load is at most the average load, which contains only jobs other than $\ell$
  3. Because $\ell$ is the longest job
  4. Because the jobs are sorted by non-increasing time
Explanation. When $\ell$ is assigned, the chosen machine has the minimum load, and the minimum is at most the average of the loads at that time, which add up jobs other than $\ell$. No order of the list is needed.
oc-f-539 · level facile · notes
In scheduling $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ with $m=3$ machines, $LIST$ produces a schedule in which job $\ell$, of time $t_\ell=4$, ends last; the other jobs have total time $15$. Which upper bound on the makespan of $LIST$ follows?
  1. $9$
  2. $19$
  3. $5$
  4. $\frac{19}{3}$
Explanation. $S_\ell\le\frac{15}{3}=5$, hence $LIST(I)=S_\ell+t_\ell\le 9$. With $z(I)\ge\frac{19}{3}$ and $z(I)\ge 4$ the guarantee $2-\frac1m$ follows.
oc-f-540 · level facile · notes
In scheduling $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ with $m=3$ machines, the list contains $m(m-1)=6$ jobs of time $1$ followed by a job of time $m=3$. What are the makespan of $LIST$ and the optimal one?
  1. $3$ and $3$: $LIST$ is optimal
  2. $5$ and $4$: ratio $\frac54$
  3. $6$ and $3$: ratio $2$
  4. $5$ and $3$: ratio $\frac53=2-\frac13$
Explanation. $LIST$ spreads the short jobs, $2$ per machine, then the long job ends at $2+3=5$. The optimum puts the long job alone and $3$ short jobs on each of the others: $3$. The guarantee is attained.
oc-f-541 · level facile · notes
Scheduling $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ with $m=3$ machines and times $t=(5,5,4,4,3,3,3)$. $LPT$ assigns the jobs in this order to the least loaded machine (ties: smallest index). What are the makespan of $LPT$ and the optimal one?
  1. $11$ and $10$
  2. $12$ and $9$
  3. $11$ and $9$: ratio $\frac{11}{9}=\frac43-\frac19$
  4. $9$ and $9$: $LPT$ is optimal
Explanation. $LPT$: $5,5,4$ on the three machines, $4\to M_3$ ($8$), $3\to M_1$ ($8$), $3\to M_2$ ($8$), the last $3\to M_1$ ($11$). The optimum is $\{5,4\},\{5,4\},\{3,3,3\}$ with makespan $9$.
oc-f-542 · level facile · notes
In scheduling $P\,||\,C_{\max}$, $LIST$ guarantees $2-\frac1m$ and $LPT$ guarantees $\frac43-\frac1{3m}$. What are they with $m=4$ machines?
  1. $2$ and $\frac43$
  2. $\frac74$ and $\frac54$
  3. $\frac74$ and $\frac43$
  4. $\frac32$ and $\frac54$
Explanation. $2-\frac14=\frac74$ and $\frac43-\frac1{12}=\frac{15}{12}=\frac54$. As $m$ grows the guarantees tend to $2$ and $\frac43$.
oc-f-543 · level facile · notes
In scheduling $P\,||\,C_{\max}$, the lower bound $\mathrm{LB}=\max\{\max_jt_j,\ \frac1m\sum_jt_j\}$ holds for the optimal makespan. How is the makespan of $LIST$ related to $\mathrm{LB}$?
  1. $LIST(I)\le\frac1m\sum_jt_j+\max_jt_j\le 2\,\mathrm{LB}$
  2. $LIST(I)=\mathrm{LB}$ for every list
  3. $LIST(I)\le\mathrm{LB}+1$
  4. $LIST(I)\ge 2\,\mathrm{LB}$ for some instance
Explanation. The last job starts within the average of the others and lasts at most $\max_jt_j$; both terms are at most $\mathrm{LB}$. A finer estimate gives $2-\frac1m$.
oc-f-544 · level facile · notes
In scheduling $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ with $m=2$ machines and times $t=(1,1,2)$ in this order, what are the makespans of $LIST$ (given list, least loaded machine, ties smallest index) and $LPT$?
  1. $LIST$ $2$, $LPT$ $2$
  2. $LIST$ $3$, $LPT$ $3$
  3. $LIST$ $4$, $LPT$ $2$
  4. $LIST$ $3$, $LPT$ $2$
Explanation. $LIST$: $1\to M_1$, $1\to M_2$, $2\to M_1$: loads $(3,1)$. $LPT$: $2\to M_1$, $1\to M_2$, $1\to M_2$: loads $(2,2)$, optimal. Placing long jobs first avoids leaving them last.

Part: Ad hoc algorithms (25)

41. Knapsack: branch-and-bound on the critical item (12)

oc-f-140 · level facile · notes
In branch-and-bound for the knapsack problem (integer profits), the best known solution has value $\mathrm{LB}=15$; a node has LP relaxation $z(\mathrm{LP}^k_{\mathrm{KP}})=\frac{47}{3}$ with a fractional solution. What is done with the node?
  1. $\mathrm{LB}$ is updated to $\frac{47}{3}$ and the node is branched on
  2. It is pruned by bound, because $\lfloor 47/3\rfloor=15\le\mathrm{LB}$
  3. It is branched on, because $\frac{47}{3}>15=\mathrm{LB}$
  4. It is pruned by integrality, and $\mathrm{LB}$ becomes $15$
Explanation. The profits are integer, so the bound of the node is $\lfloor z(\mathrm{LP}^k_{\mathrm{KP}})\rfloor$; comparing $\frac{47}{3}$ with $15$ without rounding would explore a useless node.
oc-f-141 · level facile · notes
In branch-and-bound for the knapsack problem, at node $k$ some items are fixed to $1$ ($\mathscr{I}^k_{\text{in}}$), others to $0$ ($\mathscr{I}^k_{\text{out}}$) and the rest are free ($\mathscr{I}^k$); $c(k)$ is the residual capacity and $p(k)$ the profit of the items fixed to $1$. How are the split item and $z(\mathrm{LP}^k_{\mathrm{KP}})$ computed?
  1. On the free items $\mathscr{I}^k$ with capacity $c(k)$, adding the constant $p(k)$
  2. On the free items $\mathscr{I}^k$ with capacity $c$, adding the constant $p(k)$
  3. On the free items $\mathscr{I}^k$ with capacity $c(k)$, without adding constants
  4. On all the items of the instance with capacity $c$, without adding constants
Explanation. The items of $\mathscr{I}^k_{\text{in}}$ use capacity and bring profit: $c(k)$ is the residual capacity and $p(k)$ their profit. Forgetting either is a typical mistake.
oc-f-142 · level teorema · notes
In branch-and-bound for the knapsack problem, at every node $k$ some variables are fixed to $0$ or $1$ and $\mathrm{LP}^k_{\mathrm{KP}}$ is the LP relaxation of the subproblem. Which statement on $\mathrm{LP}^k_{\mathrm{KP}}$ is correct?
  1. It is solved with the simplex method, and the solution may have many fractional variables
  2. It is solved with DP on the residual capacity, and the solution is always integer
  3. It is solved with Dantzig's formulas, and the solution always has two fractional variables
  4. It is solved with Dantzig's formulas, and the solution has at most one fractional variable
Explanation. Every node is a smaller linear KP: the free items sorted by efficiency are taken up to the split item, taken partially. This is why branching is on the variable of the split item.
oc-f-554 · level facile · notes
In the branch-and-bound for the 0-1 knapsack, the LP relaxation of a node, solved by Dantzig's formulas, has at most one fractional variable. On which variable does it branch, and with which constraints?
  1. On the variable of largest profit: children with $x_j\le\frac12$ and $x_j\ge\frac12$
  2. On the first free variable: children with $x_j=0$, $x_j=\frac12$ and $x_j=1$
  3. On all free variables together
  4. On the variable of the split item $s$: children with $x_s\le 0$ (excluded) and $x_s\ge 1$ (taken)
Explanation. The split item is the only fractional one; the two children exclude or take it, and the parent node is not solved again.
oc-f-555 · level facile · notes
In the branch-and-bound for the 0-1 knapsack, $c(k)$ is the residual capacity of node $k$ after the items fixed to $1$. What is done with a node with $c(k)<0$?
  1. Its relaxation is solved with capacity $0$
  2. The lower bound is updated with the profit $p(k)$
  3. It is pruned by infeasibility: the items fixed to $1$ already exceed the capacity
  4. It branches on the heaviest item fixed to $1$
Explanation. With $c(k)<0$ no choice of the free items makes the node feasible: its relaxation is infeasible and the node is closed.
oc-f-556 · level facile · notes
In the branch-and-bound for the 0-1 knapsack, at node $k$ the free items $\mathscr{I}^k$ weigh in total at most the residual capacity $c(k)$. What is done with the node?
  1. It is solved with the simplex, because Dantzig's formulas do not hold
  2. The relaxation is integer (all free items taken): it is pruned by integrality and the lower bound is possibly updated
  3. It branches on the most efficient free item
  4. It is pruned by infeasibility
Explanation. Without a split item the solution $\tilde x_j=1$ for every free item is integer and optimal for the node: it is a feasible KP solution, and the node needs no children.
oc-f-557 · level facile · notes
Branch-and-bound for the 0-1 knapsack with capacity $c=9$ and items sorted by non-increasing efficiency, $p=(10,7,6,4)$, $w=(5,4,3,3)$. At the node with $x_1$ fixed to $0$ (item $1$ excluded), the relaxation is solved on items $2,3,4$ with capacity $9$. What is $z(\mathrm{LP}^k_{\mathrm{KP}})$?
  1. $\frac{47}{3}$
  2. $17$
  3. $\frac{71}{4}$
  4. $13$
Explanation. Items $2$ and $3$ weigh $7$; item $4$ is the split item with $\mathit{rc}=2$: $7+6+4\cdot\frac23=\frac{47}{3}\approx 15.67$. $13$ forgets the fractional part.
oc-f-558 · level facile · notes
Branch-and-bound for the 0-1 knapsack with capacity $c=9$ and items sorted by non-increasing efficiency, $p=(10,7,6,4)$, $w=(5,4,3,3)$. At the node with $x_3$ fixed to $1$, the residual capacity is $c(k)=6$ and the fixed profit is $p(k)=6$; the relaxation is solved on the free items $1,2,4$. What is $z(\mathrm{LP}^k_{\mathrm{KP}})$?
  1. $\frac{47}{3}$
  2. $\frac{47}{4}$
  3. $19$
  4. $\frac{71}{4}$
Explanation. Item $1$ fits (weight $5$, residual $1$); item $2$ is the split item: $6+10+7\cdot\frac14=\frac{71}{4}=17.75$. Without the constant $p(k)$ one would get $\frac{47}{4}$.
oc-f-559 · level facile · notes
In the branch-and-bound for the 0-1 knapsack the nodes are extracted with the FIFO or LIFO rule. In which order are the two children of a node solved?
  1. First the child with the higher bound
  2. In random order
  3. First the child with $x_s\le 0$ (split item excluded), then the one with $x_s\ge 1$
  4. First the child with $x_s\ge 1$, then the one with $x_s\le 0$
Explanation. The fixed rule makes the run reproducible: nodes are numbered in the order in which they are solved, with the «down» branch before the «up» branch.
oc-f-560 · level teorema · notes
In the branch-and-bound for the 0-1 knapsack, the relaxation of a node may have several optimal solutions when some items have the same efficiency $p_j/w_j$. Which statement is correct?
  1. With equal efficiencies the simplex must be used
  2. Dantzig's formulas give an optimal solution with at most one fractional variable, even if other optimal solutions have more
  3. Every optimal solution has exactly one fractional variable
  4. With equal efficiencies the relaxation becomes infeasible
Explanation. With equal efficiencies capacity can be moved between items without changing the value, giving more fractional variables; the algorithm uses Dantzig's solution and branches on the only fractional one.
oc-f-561 · level facile · notes
In the branch-and-bound for the 0-1 knapsack with integer profits, which bound is used to prune a node, and which operation of the general algorithm is not needed?
  1. $\lfloor z(\mathrm{LP}^k_{\mathrm{KP}})\rfloor$; no simplex is needed, since every node is solved by Dantzig's formulas
  2. $\lceil z(\mathrm{LP}^k_{\mathrm{KP}})\rceil$; no branching is needed
  3. $z(\mathrm{LP}^k_{\mathrm{KP}})$ without rounding; no lower bound is needed
  4. $p(k)$; no relaxation is needed
Explanation. With integer profits every solution in the subtree is worth at most $\lfloor z(\mathrm{LP}^k)\rfloor$, and the node is pruned if this does not exceed the lower bound; a node relaxation is a continuous knapsack, solved by a scan.
oc-f-562 · level facile · notes
In the branch-and-bound for the 0-1 knapsack (integer profits), what is the bound at the root, where no variable is fixed?
  1. The value of the greedy by efficiency
  2. The sum of all profits
  3. Martello and Toth's bound, always
  4. Dantzig's bound $\mathrm{UB}_{\mathrm{D}}=\lfloor z(\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{KP}})\rfloor$
Explanation. At the root the relaxation is $\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{KP}}$ and its round-down is Dantzig's bound. The greedy gives the initial lower bound instead.

42. Vertex coloring: the DSATUR branch-and-bound (13)

oc-f-143 · level facile · notes
In DSATUR branch-and-bound for vertex coloring, the best known coloring (incumbent) uses $\mathrm{UB}=4$ colors. Every node $k$ is a partial coloring using $q(k)$ colors. When is node $k$ pruned?
  1. When $q(k)\ge 4$
  2. When $q(k)>4$
  3. When $q(k)\ge 3$
  4. When $q(k)\ge 4$ and all vertices are colored
Explanation. The number of colors is an integer: a node that already uses $\mathrm{UB}$ colors cannot lead to a coloring with fewer colors. The rule $q(k)>\mathrm{UB}$ is a typical mistake.
oc-f-144 · level facile · notes
In DSATUR branch-and-bound for vertex coloring, every node $k$ is a partial coloring with $q(k)$ colors, and branching is on the uncolored vertex with the largest number of distinct colors among its neighbors (saturation degree). Which children are created?
  1. One for every already used color compatible with the vertex, without the new color $q(k)+1$
  2. One for every color $1,\dots,\mathrm{UB}-1$, even if not yet used at the node
  3. Only one, with the smallest color compatible with the vertex
  4. One for every already used color compatible with the vertex, plus one with the new color $q(k)+1$
Explanation. The colors not used yet are equivalent: the first new color is enough, otherwise the tree would contain symmetric copies of the same coloring. The child with the new color is always created.
oc-f-145 · level teorema · notes
In DSATUR branch-and-bound for vertex coloring, every node gives the chosen vertex an already used color or a single new color. Which statement on the complete tree, without pruning, is correct?
  1. The leaves are only the optimal colorings, because the others are discarded by the branching
  2. Some partitions of the vertices into stable sets appear in no leaf of the tree
  3. Every partition of the vertices into stable sets is the set of color classes of exactly one leaf
  4. Every partition of the vertices into stable sets appears in $q!$ leaves, one for every permutation of the colors
Explanation. This is the no-symmetry property: with a single child for the new color, every VCP solution is enumerated once, up to renaming the colors; this is why the algorithm is exact.
oc-f-146 · level facile · notes
In DSATUR branch-and-bound for vertex coloring (a minimization problem), where do the first upper bound $\mathrm{UB}$ and the lower bound $\mathrm{LB}$ on the chromatic number come from?
  1. $\mathrm{UB}$ from the number of vertices $n$, $\mathrm{LB}$ from the maximum degree $\Delta(G)$
  2. $\mathrm{UB}$ from the $DSATUR$ heuristic, $\mathrm{LB}$ from a greedily found clique
  3. $\mathrm{UB}$ from a greedily found clique, $\mathrm{LB}$ from the $DSATUR$ heuristic
  4. $\mathrm{UB}$ from the LP relaxation, $\mathrm{LB}$ from the $DSATUR$ heuristic
Explanation. The VCP is a minimization problem: a coloring gives the upper bound, a clique the lower bound ($\omega(G)\le\chi(G)$). The vertices of the clique get different colors from the start.
oc-f-545 · level facile · notes
In the DSATUR branch-and-bound for coloring, node $k$ uses $q(k)$ colors; for the uncolored vertex $v$, $\mathit{sat}_k(v)$ is the number of distinct colors among its neighbours and $F_k(v)$ the set of already used colors that $v$ can take. With $q(k)=4$ and $\mathit{sat}_k(v)=3$, how many children does the node have if it branches on $v$?
  1. $4$: one per used color
  2. $5$: one per color from $1$ to $5$
  3. $2$: the only allowed used color and the new color $5$
  4. $1$: only the only allowed used color
Explanation. $|F_k(v)|=q(k)-\mathit{sat}_k(v)=1$, and the child with the new color $q(k)+1$ is added: $2$ children. Colors $q(k)+2,\dots$ would give symmetric copies.
oc-f-546 · level facile · notes
In the DSATUR branch-and-bound for coloring, on which vertex does node $k$ branch, and why?
  1. On a random vertex, to diversify the search
  2. On the uncolored vertex of maximum saturation degree: it is the most constrained and has the fewest children
  3. On the uncolored vertex of minimum degree, the easiest to color
  4. On the vertex of smallest index, to have a fixed order
Explanation. The most saturated vertex has $|F_k(v)|+1$ children, the fewest possible; ties go to the one with more uncolored neighbours, then the smallest index.
oc-f-547 · level facile · notes
In the DSATUR branch-and-bound, a clique $K=\{k_1,\dots,k_r\}$ found greedily is pre-colored at the root, with color $i$ on vertex $k_i$. What is it for?
  1. It gives the lower bound $r$ and removes the symmetry of the first $r$ colors, whose names are arbitrary
  2. It gives the initial upper bound, better than that of $DSATUR$
  3. It guarantees that the optimal coloring uses exactly $r$ colors
  4. It only speeds up the computation of the degrees
Explanation. The vertices of a clique have different colors in every coloring, so at least $r$ colors are needed; fixing their names excludes no solution up to renaming the colors.
oc-f-548 · level facile · notes
In the DSATUR branch-and-bound, the $DSATUR$ heuristic gives the upper bound $\mathrm{UB}$ and a greedy clique $K$ the lower bound $|K|$. What happens if $\mathrm{UB}=|K|$ already at the root?
  1. The whole tree is explored anyway for confirmation
  2. A larger clique is searched, because the bounds cannot coincide
  3. $\mathrm{UB}$ is decreased by one and the search restarts
  4. The algorithm stops: the $DSATUR$ coloring is optimal
Explanation. Lower and upper bounds coincide, hence $\chi(G)=\mathrm{UB}$: no branching is needed.
oc-f-549 · level facile · notes
In the DSATUR branch-and-bound, a node $k$ with all vertices colored and $q(k)<\mathrm{UB}$ colors is a new incumbent solution. What is done next with the list of open nodes?
  1. Nothing changes: nodes are checked only when extracted
  2. Remove the nodes with $q<\mathrm{UB}$, now useless
  3. Set $\mathrm{UB}\leftarrow q(k)$ and remove the nodes with $q\ge\mathrm{UB}$
  4. Empty the list: the first complete coloring is optimal
Explanation. With the new $\mathrm{UB}$ the nodes already using at least $\mathrm{UB}$ colors cannot lead to better solutions. If $\mathrm{UB}=|K|$ the search ends.
oc-f-550 · level facile · notes
In the DSATUR branch-and-bound, how is the tree explored and in which order are the children of a node extracted?
  1. At random, to diversify
  2. Depth first (stack list); children are evaluated by increasing color and the one with the smallest color is extracted first
  3. Breadth first (queue list); the child with the new color is extracted first
  4. By best bound; the node with most colors is extracted
Explanation. Depth-first search reaches complete colorings early and hence new upper bounds; the smallest color first tries not to add colors.
oc-f-551 · level teorema · notes
Why is the DSATUR branch-and-bound for coloring an exact algorithm, although it prunes nodes?
  1. It prunes only nodes with $q(k)\ge\mathrm{UB}$, and every complete coloring below node $k$ uses at least $q(k)$ colors
  2. Because $DSATUR$ is optimal and branch-and-bound confirms it
  3. Because it prunes only nodes with no vertex left to color
  4. Because the chromatic number always equals the maximum clique
Explanation. The colors of a node are never removed in its subtree, so $q(k)$ is a lower bound of the subtree; with integer values, no coloring with fewer than $\mathrm{UB}$ colors is lost.
oc-f-552 · level facile · notes
In the DSATUR branch-and-bound, Sewell's rule (1996) changes the choice of the branching vertex among those of maximum saturation degree. Which criterion does it use?
  1. It chooses the vertex of smallest index
  2. It chooses the vertex with fewest uncolored neighbours
  3. It chooses a random vertex among the tied ones
  4. It chooses the vertex maximizing the number of feasible colors it shares with its uncolored neighbours
Explanation. The rule aims at reducing the neighbours' options most: the chosen color removes options from many uncolored vertices, and the tree shrinks. PASS applies it only to some vertices, to limit its cost.
oc-f-553 · level facile · notes
The remarks on the DSATUR branch-and-bound cite Brélaz (1979), Sewell (1996) and PASS (San Segundo, 2012). What do the Sewell and PASS variants have in common?
  1. They branch on colors instead of vertices
  2. They remove the pre-coloring of the clique
  3. They change only the tie-breaking rule in the choice of the branching vertex, leaving bounds and pruning unchanged
  4. They replace pruning by bound with the LP relaxation
Explanation. The scheme stays Brélaz's: most saturated vertex, used colors plus one new color, pruning with $q(k)\ge\mathrm{UB}$. The variants choose better among tied vertices.

1. Combinatorial optimization problems (2)

Example 1 · oc-g-co-gap · level facile · notes
Instance of a combinatorial optimization problem in minimization; a dual bound is $\mathrm{LB}=27$ and a feasible solution is worth $\mathrm{UB}=37$. The relative gap is $\frac{\mathrm{UB}-\mathrm{LB}}{|\mathrm{LB}|}$. What is it?
  1. $10$
  2. $\frac{10}{37}$
  3. $\frac{10}{27}$
  4. $\frac{37}{27}$
Explanation. $\frac{37-27}{27}=\frac{10}{27}$. $10$ is the absolute gap; dividing by $\mathrm{UB}$ gives $\frac{10}{37}$.
Example 2 · oc-g-co-gap · level facile · notes

  2. $4$
  3. $\frac{1}{10}$
  4. $\frac{1}{9}$
Explanation. $\frac{40-36}{36}=\frac{1}{9}$. $4$ is the absolute gap; dividing by $\mathrm{UB}$ gives $\frac{1}{10}$.

  4. $2520$
Explanation. The starting city is fixed and a tour is identified with its reverse: $\frac{(n-1)!}{2}=360$.

2. Knapsack problems (2)

Example 1 · oc-g-kp-card · level facile · notes
0-1 knapsack with capacity $c=11$ and items $\begin{array}{c|cccccc}j & 1 & 2 & 3 & 4 & 5 & 6\\ \hline p_j & 8 & 10 & 4 & 9 & 11 & 11\\ w_j & 6 & 8 & 8 & 6 & 2 & 3\end{array}$. Let $\bar k$ be the largest integer such that the $\bar k$ lightest items weigh in total at most $c$; the cardinality bound is the sum of the $\bar k$ largest profits. What is it?
  1. $30$
  2. $41$
  3. $3$
  4. $32$
Explanation. The sorted weights give $\bar k=3$; the $3$ largest profits add up to $32$. Adding the profits of the lightest items ($30$) does not give an upper bound.

Example 1 · oc-g-kp-brute · level media · notes

  2. $\{1,2,5\}$
  3. $\{5\}$
  4. $\{2,4,5\}$

3. Packing problems (2)

Example 1 · oc-g-bpp-lb · level facile · notes
Bin packing (BPP): the items of weights $w=(9,3,4,2,2,4,6,3)$ must be packed into the minimum number of bins of capacity $c=10$. What is the weight bound $\left\lceil \sum_j w_j / c \right\rceil$?
  1. $4$
  2. $2$
  3. $5$
  4. $3$
Explanation. $\sum_j w_j=33$, hence $\lceil 33/10\rceil=4$. The value is rounded up because the number of bins is an integer.

  3. $4$
  4. $6$
Explanation. $\sum_j w_j=49$, hence $\lceil 49/12\rceil=5$. The value is rounded up because the number of bins is an integer.

4. Coloring problems (2)

Example 1 · oc-g-vcp-bounds · level facile · notes
Graph $G=(V,E)$ with $V=\{1,\dots,6\}$ and $E=\{\{1,6\},\{3,4\},\{3,6\},\{4,5\},\{4,6\},\{5,6\}\}$. For the chromatic number $\chi(G)$ the clique bound $\omega(G)$ (maximum clique) and the stable set bound $\lceil n/\alpha(G)\rceil$ ($\alpha(G)$ maximum stable set) hold. What are they, in this order?
  1. $4$, $2$
  2. $3$, $2$
  3. $3$, $1$
  4. $5$, $2$
Explanation. $\omega(G)=3$ and $\alpha(G)=4$, hence $\lceil 6/4\rceil=2$: $\chi(G)\ge3$. The largest degree gives instead the upper bound $\Delta+1=5$.

Example 1 · oc-g-vcp-chi · level media · notes

  3. $2$

5. Clique problems (2)

Example 1 · oc-g-mcp-bounds · level facile · notes
Graph $G=(V,E)$ with $V=\{1,\dots,7\}$ and $E=\{\{1,6\},\{2,6\},\{3,4\},\{3,5\},\{3,6\},\{3,7\},\{4,5\},\{4,7\},\{5,6\}\}$. For the maximum clique $\omega(G)$ the degree bound $\Delta+1$ ($\Delta$ largest degree) and the bound $k^{\max}$, the largest $k$ such that at least $k$ vertices have degree at least $k-1$, hold. What are they, in this order?

  3. $4$, $4$
  4. $5$, $4$

Example 1 · oc-g-mcp-omega · level media · notes
Graph $G=(V,E)$ with $V=\{1,\dots,7\}$ and $E=\{\{1,6\},\{2,6\},\{3,4\},\{3,5\},\{3,6\},\{3,7\},\{4,5\},\{4,6\},\{4,7\},\{5,6\},\{5,7\}\}$. A clique is a set of pairwise adjacent vertices. How many vertices does a maximum clique have, i.e., what is $\omega(G)$?
  1. $3$
  2. $5$
  3. $6$
  4. $4$
Explanation. $\omega(G)=4$. The bounds $k^{\max}=4$ and $\Delta+1=6$ are only upper bounds; $3$ is the maximum stable set.
Example 2 · oc-g-mcp-omega · level media · notes
Graph $G=(V,E)$ with $V=\{1,\dots,7\}$ and $E=\{\{1,2\},\{1,4\},\{1,5\},\{1,6\},\{3,6\},\{5,6\},\{6,7\}\}$. A clique is a set of pairwise adjacent vertices. How many vertices does a maximum clique have, i.e., what is $\omega(G)$?
  1. $5$
  2. $4$
  3. $2$
  4. $3$
Explanation. $\omega(G)=3$. The bounds $k^{\max}=3$ and $\Delta+1=5$ are only upper bounds; $5$ is the maximum stable set.

6. Routing problems (2)

Example 1 · oc-g-tsp-lb · level facile · notes
Symmetric TSP with $5$ cities and distances $\begin{array}{c|ccccc} d_{ij} & 1 & 2 & 3 & 4 & 5\\ \hline 1 & - & 9 & 10 & 13 & 14\\ 2 & 9 & - & 3 & 13 & 10\\ 3 & 10 & 3 & - & 10 & 14\\ 4 & 13 & 13 & 10 & - & 2\\ 5 & 14 & 10 & 14 & 2 & -\end{array}$. With $a_i$ and $b_i$ the two smallest distances from city $i$ to the others, the lower bound is $\left\lceil\frac12\sum_i(a_i+b_i)\right\rceil$. What is it?
  1. $68$
  2. $19$
  3. $34$
  4. $35$
Explanation. $\sum_i(a_i+b_i)=68$, and half of it rounded up is $34$: every tour edge is counted by both endpoints.
Example 2 · oc-g-tsp-lb · level facile · notes
Symmetric TSP with $5$ cities and distances $\begin{array}{c|ccccc} d_{ij} & 1 & 2 & 3 & 4 & 5\\ \hline 1 & - & 7 & 13 & 6 & 3\\ 2 & 7 & - & 7 & 14 & 13\\ 3 & 13 & 7 & - & 11 & 14\\ 4 & 6 & 14 & 11 & - & 12\\ 5 & 3 & 13 & 14 & 12 & -\end{array}$. With $a_i$ and $b_i$ the two smallest distances from city $i$ to the others, the lower bound is $\left\lceil\frac12\sum_i(a_i+b_i)\right\rceil$. What is it?
  1. $37$
  2. $73$
  3. $38$
  4. $26$
Explanation. $\sum_i(a_i+b_i)=73$, and half of it rounded up is $37$: every tour edge is counted by both endpoints.
Example 1 · oc-g-tsp-opt · level media · notes
Symmetric TSP with $5$ cities and distances $\begin{array}{c|ccccc} d_{ij} & 1 & 2 & 3 & 4 & 5\\ \hline 1 & - & 5 & 15 & 6 & 6\\ 2 & 5 & - & 3 & 5 & 9\\ 3 & 15 & 3 & - & 12 & 10\\ 4 & 6 & 5 & 12 & - & 8\\ 5 & 6 & 9 & 10 & 8 & -\end{array}$. How long is the shortest tour $z(I)$ (closed route visiting every city once)?
  1. $29$
  2. $32$
  3. $31$
  4. $30$
Explanation. Listing the $12$ distinct tours, the shortest has length $30$. Nearest neighbour from city $1$ gives $32$, and the two-smallest-distances bound $29$.
Example 2 · oc-g-tsp-opt · level media · notes
Symmetric TSP with $5$ cities and distances $\begin{array}{c|ccccc} d_{ij} & 1 & 2 & 3 & 4 & 5\\ \hline 1 & - & 10 & 3 & 14 & 14\\ 2 & 10 & - & 10 & 9 & 14\\ 3 & 3 & 10 & - & 4 & 12\\ 4 & 14 & 9 & 4 & - & 11\\ 5 & 14 & 14 & 12 & 11 & -\end{array}$. How long is the shortest tour $z(I)$ (closed route visiting every city once)?
  1. $44$
  2. $42$
  3. $43$
  4. $38$
Explanation. Listing the $12$ distinct tours, the shortest has length $42$. Nearest neighbour from city $1$ gives $44$, and the two-smallest-distances bound $38$.

7. Scheduling problems (2)

Example 1 · oc-g-pcmax-bounds · level facile · notes
Scheduling $P\,||\,C_{\max}$: $m=3$ identical machines, times $t=(6,8,8,1,8,6)$, the makespan (maximum load) is minimized. The jobs are assigned in the order $1,\dots,6$, each to the machine with the smallest load (ties: smallest index), and the combinatorial lower bound $\max\{\max_j t_j,\lceil\sum_j t_j/m\rceil\}$ is used. Which interval is obtained for the optimal makespan $z(I)$?
  1. $12\le z(I)\le 15$
  2. $13\le z(I)\le 15$
  3. $13\le z(I)\le 37$
  4. $8\le z(I)\le 15$
Explanation. Lower bound: $\max\{8,\lceil 37/3\rceil\}=13$; the schedule built has makespan $15$, an upper bound. Hence $13\le z(I)\le 15$.
Example 2 · oc-g-pcmax-bounds · level facile · notes
Scheduling $P\,||\,C_{\max}$: $m=3$ identical machines, times $t=(9,7,6,8,8,2)$, the makespan (maximum load) is minimized. The jobs are assigned in the order $1,\dots,6$, each to the machine with the smallest load (ties: smallest index), and the combinatorial lower bound $\max\{\max_j t_j,\lceil\sum_j t_j/m\rceil\}$ is used. Which interval is obtained for the optimal makespan $z(I)$?
  1. $14\le z(I)\le 15$
  2. $13\le z(I)\le 15$
  3. $9\le z(I)\le 15$
  4. $14\le z(I)\le 40$
Explanation. Lower bound: $\max\{9,\lceil 40/3\rceil\}=14$; the schedule built has makespan $15$, an upper bound. Hence $14\le z(I)\le 15$.
Example 1 · oc-g-pcmax-opt · level media · notes
Scheduling $P\,||\,C_{\max}$: jobs of processing time $t=(6,7,9,3,3,5)$ on $m=3$ identical machines; the makespan is the largest load of a machine. What is the optimal makespan $z(I)$?
  1. $12$
  2. $13$
  3. $11$
  4. $14$
Explanation. $z(I)=12$. The lower bound $\max\{\max_jt_j,\lceil\sum_jt_j/m\rceil\}$ is $11$; $LPT$ gives $12$.
Example 2 · oc-g-pcmax-opt · level media · notes
Scheduling $P\,||\,C_{\max}$: jobs of processing time $t=(1,9,8,6,5,8)$ on $m=3$ identical machines; the makespan is the largest load of a machine. What is the optimal makespan $z(I)$?
  1. $15$
  2. $13$
  3. $16$
  4. $14$
Explanation. $z(I)=14$. The lower bound $\max\{\max_jt_j,\lceil\sum_jt_j/m\rceil\}$ is $13$; $LPT$ gives $14$.

8. Lot sizing problems (2)

Example 1 · oc-g-ls-cost · level facile · notes
Lot sizing over $4$ periods, zero initial stock: demand $d_t$, production $x_t$ of the plan, setup cost $sc_t$ (paid only if $x_t>0$), variable cost $vc_t$ per unit produced and cost $hc_t$ per unit of stock at the end of a period: $\begin{array}{c|cccc}t & 1 & 2 & 3 & 4\\ \hline d_t & 4 & 1 & 6 & 2\\ x_t & 5 & 0 & 6 & 2\\ sc_t & 14 & 17 & 5 & 5\\ vc_t & 2 & 1 & 2 & 0\\ hc_t & 3 & 2 & 1 & 1\end{array}$. What does the plan cost?
  1. $27$
  2. $49$
  3. $66$
  4. $46$
Explanation. Setups, production and stocks add up to $49$. Without holding costs one would get $46$; paying the setup in every period gives $66$.
Example 2 · oc-g-ls-cost · level facile · notes
Lot sizing over $4$ periods, zero initial stock: demand $d_t$, production $x_t$ of the plan, setup cost $sc_t$ (paid only if $x_t>0$), variable cost $vc_t$ per unit produced and cost $hc_t$ per unit of stock at the end of a period: $\begin{array}{c|cccc}t & 1 & 2 & 3 & 4\\ \hline d_t & 3 & 3 & 6 & 5\\ x_t & 12 & 0 & 0 & 5\\ sc_t & 18 & 18 & 17 & 7\\ vc_t & 0 & 2 & 1 & 2\\ hc_t & 1 & 2 & 3 & 3\end{array}$. What does the plan cost?
  1. $46$
  2. $56$
  3. $35$
  4. $91$
Explanation. Setups, production and stocks add up to $56$. Without holding costs one would get $35$; paying the setup in every period gives $91$.
Example 1 · oc-g-ls-lb · level facile · notes
Capacitated lot sizing (CLS) over $5$ periods with total demand $D=128$, production capacities $cap_t$ and setup costs $sc_t$ (paid in the periods with production), zero initial stock: $\begin{array}{c|ccccc}t & 1 & 2 & 3 & 4 & 5\\ \hline cap_t & 29 & 35 & 17 & 31 & 37\\ sc_t & 59 & 73 & 71 & 61 & 31\end{array}$. The combinatorial lower bound adds the $k$ smallest setup costs, with $k$ the minimum number of periods whose capacities cover $D$. What is it?
  1. $295$
  2. $264$
  3. $151$
  4. $222$
Explanation. With the largest capacities $k=4$ periods are needed; the $4$ cheapest setups add up to $222$. Using the smallest capacities overestimates $k$.
Example 2 · oc-g-ls-lb · level facile · notes
Capacitated lot sizing (CLS) over $5$ periods with total demand $D=104$, production capacities $cap_t$ and setup costs $sc_t$ (paid in the periods with production), zero initial stock: $\begin{array}{c|ccccc}t & 1 & 2 & 3 & 4 & 5\\ \hline cap_t & 24 & 23 & 25 & 35 & 39\\ sc_t & 72 & 34 & 73 & 63 & 68\end{array}$. The combinatorial lower bound adds the $k$ smallest setup costs, with $k$ the minimum number of periods whose capacities cover $D$. What is it?
  1. $276$
  2. $237$
  3. $165$
  4. $310$
Explanation. With the largest capacities $k=4$ periods are needed; the $4$ cheapest setups add up to $237$. Using the smallest capacities overestimates $k$.

9. Formulations of knapsack problems (4)

Example 1 · oc-g-kp-split · level facile · notes
0-1 knapsack problem (KP: choose a subset of items of maximum profit with total weight at most $c$) with capacity $c=13$ and the following items: $\begin{array}{c|ccccc}j & 1 & 2 & 3 & 4 & 5\\ \hline p_j & 22 & 12 & 23 & 9 & 4\\ w_j & 6 & 8 & 6 & 2 & 4\end{array}$. With the items sorted by non-increasing efficiency $p_j/w_j$ (ties: smaller index) and inserted in this order, the split item $s$ is the first one that does not fit entirely and $\mathit{rc}$ is the capacity left free by the previous items. What are $s$ and $\mathit{rc}$?
  1. $s=2$, $\mathit{rc}=7$
  2. $s=3$, $\mathit{rc}=1$
  3. $s=1$, $\mathit{rc}=5$
  4. $s=2$, $\mathit{rc}=1$
Explanation. Order by efficiency $p_j/w_j$: $4,3,1,2,5$. The first item that does not fit entirely is $s=1$ and $\mathit{rc}=c-\sum_{j<s} w_j=5$. Typical mistakes: using the order of the indices, of the profits or of the weights.
Example 2 · oc-g-kp-split · level facile · notes
0-1 knapsack problem (KP: choose a subset of items of maximum profit with total weight at most $c$) with capacity $c=20$ and the following items: $\begin{array}{c|ccccc}j & 1 & 2 & 3 & 4 & 5\\ \hline p_j & 21 & 9 & 29 & 29 & 13\\ w_j & 5 & 2 & 8 & 8 & 8\end{array}$. With the items sorted by non-increasing efficiency $p_j/w_j$ (ties: smaller index) and inserted in this order, the split item $s$ is the first one that does not fit entirely and $\mathit{rc}$ is the capacity left free by the previous items. What are $s$ and $\mathit{rc}$?
  1. $s=1$, $\mathit{rc}=4$
  2. $s=4$, $\mathit{rc}=5$
  3. $s=3$, $\mathit{rc}=13$
  4. $s=4$, $\mathit{rc}=3$
Explanation. Order by efficiency $p_j/w_j$: $2,1,3,4,5$. The first item that does not fit entirely is $s=4$ and $\mathit{rc}=c-\sum_{j<s} w_j=5$. Typical mistakes: using the order of the indices, of the profits or of the weights.
Example 1 · oc-g-kp-lp · level facile · notes
0-1 knapsack problem (KP: choose a subset of items of maximum profit with total weight at most $c$) with capacity $c=12$ and items already sorted by non-increasing efficiency: $\begin{array}{c|cccc}j & 1 & 2 & 3 & 4\\ \hline p_j & 27 & 16 & 7 & 5\\ w_j & 7 & 6 & 4 & 4\end{array}$. What is $z(\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{KP}})$, the optimal value of the LP relaxation with $0\le x_j\le 1$?
  1. $\frac{121}{3}$
  2. $\frac{324}{7}$
  3. $43$
  4. $32$
Explanation. $s=2$, $\mathit{rc}=5$, hence $z(\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{KP}})=\sum_{j<s}p_j+p_s\,\mathit{rc}/w_s=27+16\cdot\frac{5}{6}=\frac{121}{3}$. The value $p_1 c/w_1$ is that of $\mathrm{LP}\text{-}0_{\mathrm{KP}}$, without the constraints $x_j\le 1$.
Example 2 · oc-g-kp-lp · level facile · notes
0-1 knapsack problem (KP: choose a subset of items of maximum profit with total weight at most $c$) with capacity $c=15$ and items already sorted by non-increasing efficiency: $\begin{array}{c|cccc}j & 1 & 2 & 3 & 4\\ \hline p_j & 10 & 14 & 20 & 20\\ w_j & 2 & 4 & 7 & 7\end{array}$. What is $z(\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{KP}})$, the optimal value of the LP relaxation with $0\le x_j\le 1$?
  1. $46$
  2. $75$
  3. $64$
  4. $\frac{348}{7}$
Explanation. $s=4$, $\mathit{rc}=2$, hence $z(\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{KP}})=\sum_{j<s}p_j+p_s\,\mathit{rc}/w_s=44+20\cdot\frac{2}{7}=\frac{348}{7}$. The value $p_1 c/w_1$ is that of $\mathrm{LP}\text{-}0_{\mathrm{KP}}$, without the constraints $x_j\le 1$.
Example 1 · oc-g-kp-dantzig · level facile · notes
0-1 knapsack problem (KP: choose a subset of items of maximum profit with total weight at most $c$) with capacity $c=15$ and the following items: $\begin{array}{c|ccccc}j & 1 & 2 & 3 & 4 & 5\\ \hline p_j & 30 & 20 & 10 & 21 & 32\\ w_j & 8 & 5 & 8 & 7 & 9\end{array}$. What is the Dantzig upper bound $\mathrm{UB}_{\mathrm{D}}$, the integer part of the LP relaxation value (with $0\le x_j\le 1$)?
  1. $57$
  2. $58$
  3. $52$
  4. $82$
Explanation. Order by efficiency: $2,1,5,4,3$; split item $s=5$, $\mathit{rc}=2$. $\mathrm{UB}_{\mathrm{D}}=50+\lfloor 32\cdot 2/9\rfloor=57$. The items must be sorted by efficiency, and the value is rounded down.
Example 2 · oc-g-kp-dantzig · level facile · notes
0-1 knapsack problem (KP: choose a subset of items of maximum profit with total weight at most $c$) with capacity $c=20$ and the following items: $\begin{array}{c|ccccc}j & 1 & 2 & 3 & 4 & 5\\ \hline p_j & 17 & 10 & 23 & 22 & 15\\ w_j & 5 & 2 & 8 & 8 & 8\end{array}$. What is the Dantzig upper bound $\mathrm{UB}_{\mathrm{D}}$, the integer part of the LP relaxation value (with $0\le x_j\le 1$)?
  1. $64$
  2. $63$
  3. $58$
  4. $72$
Explanation. Order by efficiency: $2,1,3,4,5$; split item $s=4$, $\mathit{rc}=5$. $\mathrm{UB}_{\mathrm{D}}=50+\lfloor 22\cdot 5/8\rfloor=63$. The items must be sorted by efficiency, and the value is rounded down.
Example 1 · oc-g-kp-mt · level media · notes
0-1 knapsack problem (KP: choose a subset of items of maximum profit with total weight at most $c$) with capacity $c=13$ and items already sorted by non-increasing efficiency: $\begin{array}{c|ccccc}j & 1 & 2 & 3 & 4 & 5\\ \hline p_j & 7 & 19 & 18 & 14 & 4\\ w_j & 2 & 6 & 6 & 8 & 4\end{array}$. Let $s$ be the split item (the first one that does not fit entirely). What is the Martello-Toth upper bound $\mathrm{UB}_{\mathrm{MT}}=\max\{\mathrm{UB}_0,\mathrm{UB}_1\}$, where $\mathrm{UB}_0$ imposes $x_s=0$ and $\mathrm{UB}_1$ imposes $x_s=1$?
  1. $34$
  2. $42$
  3. $40$
  4. $41$
Explanation. $s=3$, $\mathit{rc}=5$; $\mathrm{UB}_0=26+\lfloor 5\cdot 14/8\rfloor=34$, $\mathrm{UB}_1=26+\lfloor 18-(6-5)\cdot 19/6\rfloor=40$, $\mathrm{UB}_{\mathrm{MT}}=\max\{\mathrm{UB}_0,\mathrm{UB}_1\}=40$ (Dantzig: $41$). The maximum is taken, not the minimum.
Example 2 · oc-g-kp-mt · level media · notes
0-1 knapsack problem (KP: choose a subset of items of maximum profit with total weight at most $c$) with capacity $c=17$ and items already sorted by non-increasing efficiency: $\begin{array}{c|ccccc}j & 1 & 2 & 3 & 4 & 5\\ \hline p_j & 10 & 14 & 22 & 14 & 4\\ w_j & 2 & 4 & 7 & 7 & 3\end{array}$. Let $s$ be the split item (the first one that does not fit entirely). What is the Martello-Toth upper bound $\mathrm{UB}_{\mathrm{MT}}=\max\{\mathrm{UB}_0,\mathrm{UB}_1\}$, where $\mathrm{UB}_0$ imposes $x_s=0$ and $\mathrm{UB}_1$ imposes $x_s=1$?
  1. $50$
  2. $58$
  3. $54$
  4. $51$
Explanation. $s=4$, $\mathit{rc}=4$; $\mathrm{UB}_0=46+\lfloor 4\cdot 4/3\rfloor=51$, $\mathrm{UB}_1=46+\lfloor 14-(7-4)\cdot 22/7\rfloor=50$, $\mathrm{UB}_{\mathrm{MT}}=\max\{\mathrm{UB}_0,\mathrm{UB}_1\}=51$ (Dantzig: $54$). The maximum is taken, not the minimum.

10. Formulations of packing problems (2)

Example 1 · oc-g-bpp-lp · level facile · notes
Bin packing with capacity $c=10$ and items of weight $w=(6,8,8,2,8)$. The LP relaxation $\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{BPP}}$ of the model with variables $y_b$ (bin used) and $x_{jb}$ (item $j$ in bin $b$) equals $\sum_j w_j/c$. What are $z(\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{BPP}})$ and the optimal integer value, in this order?
  1. $\frac{16}{5}$ and $4$
  2. $\frac{16}{5}$ and $5$
  3. $\frac{5}{2}$ and $4$
  4. $\frac{16}{25}$ and $4$
Explanation. $z(\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{BPP}})=32/10=\frac{16}{5}$ and the optimal value is $4$; the rounded relaxation, $4$, is only a lower bound.
Example 2 · oc-g-bpp-lp · level facile · notes
Bin packing with capacity $c=9$ and items of weight $w=(7,4,2,4,8)$. The LP relaxation $\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{BPP}}$ of the model with variables $y_b$ (bin used) and $x_{jb}$ (item $j$ in bin $b$) equals $\sum_j w_j/c$. What are $z(\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{BPP}})$ and the optimal integer value, in this order?
  1. $\frac{25}{9}$ and $3$
  2. $\frac{5}{9}$ and $3$
  3. $\frac{5}{2}$ and $3$
  4. $\frac{25}{9}$ and $4$
Explanation. $z(\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{BPP}})=25/9=\frac{25}{9}$ and the optimal value is $3$; the rounded relaxation, $3$, is only a lower bound.
Example 1 · oc-g-bpp-lpgap · level media · notes
Bin packing with capacity $c=16$ and $5$ items of weight $w=(12,12,9,12,11)$, all larger than $c/2$. The LP relaxation of the model equals $\sum_j w_j/c$. What is the ratio $\lceil z(\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{BPP}})\rceil/z(\mathrm{ILP}_{\mathrm{BPP}})$?
  1. $\frac{7}{10}$
  2. $\frac{4}{5}$
  3. $\frac{5}{4}$
  4. $\frac{1}{2}$
Explanation. Two items heavier than $c/2$ do not fit together, so the optimum is $5$; $\lceil 56/16\rceil=4$. The ratio $\frac{4}{5}$ is at least $\frac12$, as guaranteed.
Example 2 · oc-g-bpp-lpgap · level media · notes
Bin packing with capacity $c=14$ and $6$ items of weight $w=(8,8,9,10,10,9)$, all larger than $c/2$. The LP relaxation of the model equals $\sum_j w_j/c$. What is the ratio $\lceil z(\mathrm{LP}_{\mathrm{BPP}})\rceil/z(\mathrm{ILP}_{\mathrm{BPP}})$?
  1. $\frac{2}{3}$
  2. $\frac{9}{14}$
  3. $\frac{1}{2}$
  4. $\frac{3}{2}$
Explanation. Two items heavier than $c/2$ do not fit together, so the optimum is $6$; $\lceil 54/14\rceil=4$. The ratio $\frac{2}{3}$ is at least $\frac12$, as guaranteed.

14. Formulations of scheduling problems (2)

Example 1 · oc-g-pcmax-lp · level media · notes
Scheduling $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ with $m=3$ identical machines and times $t=(7,10,11,2,10)$. The model has binary variables $x_{jh}$ (job $j$ on machine $h$) and the variable $C_{\max}\ge\sum_jt_jx_{jh}$. What are the LP relaxation and the optimal makespan, in this order?
  1. $\frac{40}{3}$ and $17$
  2. $40$ and $17$
  3. $11$ and $17$
  4. $\frac{40}{3}$ and $14$
Explanation. The relaxation splits the jobs equally: $\sum_jt_j/m=\frac{40}{3}$. The optimal makespan, with indivisible jobs, is $17$.
Example 2 · oc-g-pcmax-lp · level media · notes
Scheduling $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ with $m=2$ identical machines and times $t=(11,4,2,5,11)$. The model has binary variables $x_{jh}$ (job $j$ on machine $h$) and the variable $C_{\max}\ge\sum_jt_jx_{jh}$. What are the LP relaxation and the optimal makespan, in this order?
  1. $\frac{33}{2}$ and $17$
  2. $33$ and $17$
  3. $11$ and $17$
  4. $\frac{33}{2}$ and $18$
Explanation. The relaxation splits the jobs equally: $\sum_jt_j/m=\frac{33}{2}$. The optimal makespan, with indivisible jobs, is $17$.
Example 1 · oc-g-pcmax-lpratio · level facile · notes
Scheduling $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ with $m=3$ identical machines and times $t=(22,3,3,1)$. The LP relaxation of the model with assignment variables equals $\frac1m\sum_jt_j$. What is the ratio between the relaxation and the optimal makespan?
  1. $\frac{29}{66}$
  2. $\frac{66}{29}$
  3. $\frac{1}{3}$
  4. $\frac{29}{22}$
Explanation. The job of length $22$ dominates: the optimal makespan is $22$, the relaxation $\frac{29}{3}$. The ratio $\frac{29}{66}$ is at least $\frac1m=\frac{1}{3}$, and gets close to it with one long job and short ones.
Example 2 · oc-g-pcmax-lpratio · level facile · notes
Scheduling $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ with $m=3$ identical machines and times $t=(27,1,1,2)$. The LP relaxation of the model with assignment variables equals $\frac1m\sum_jt_j$. What is the ratio between the relaxation and the optimal makespan?
  1. $\frac{31}{81}$
  2. $\frac{1}{3}$
  3. $\frac{81}{31}$
  4. $\frac{31}{27}$
Explanation. The job of length $27$ dominates: the optimal makespan is $27$, the relaxation $\frac{31}{3}$. The ratio $\frac{31}{81}$ is at least $\frac1m=\frac{1}{3}$, and gets close to it with one long job and short ones.

15. Formulations of lot sizing problems (2)

Example 1 · oc-g-ls-bigm · level facile · notes
Capacitated lot sizing (CLS) over $5$ periods, zero final stock: $\begin{array}{c|ccccc}t & 1 & 2 & 3 & 4 & 5\\ \hline d_t & 12 & 13 & 9 & 12 & 10\\ cap_t & 40 & 20 & 37 & 60 & 56\end{array}$. In the big-M formulation production and setup are linked by $x_t\le M_t\,y_t$, and the strongest valid value is $M_t=\min\{cap_t,\ D_{t,5}\}$, with $D_{t,5}$ the demand of periods $t$ to $5$. What is $M_4$?
  1. $56$
  2. $12$
  3. $22$
  4. $60$
Explanation. $D_{4,5}=22$ and $cap_4=60$, hence $M_4=22$. A larger value is valid but weakens the LP relaxation.
Example 2 · oc-g-ls-bigm · level facile · notes
Capacitated lot sizing (CLS) over $5$ periods, zero final stock: $\begin{array}{c|ccccc}t & 1 & 2 & 3 & 4 & 5\\ \hline d_t & 16 & 15 & 16 & 6 & 5\\ cap_t & 53 & 25 & 45 & 66 & 55\end{array}$. In the big-M formulation production and setup are linked by $x_t\le M_t\,y_t$, and the strongest valid value is $M_t=\min\{cap_t,\ D_{t,5}\}$, with $D_{t,5}$ the demand of periods $t$ to $5$. What is $M_4$?
  1. $6$
  2. $66$
  3. $11$
  4. $58$
Explanation. $D_{4,5}=11$ and $cap_4=66$, hence $M_4=11$. A larger value is valid but weakens the LP relaxation.
Example 1 · oc-g-ls-lpsetup · level facile · notes
In the LP relaxation of the big-M lot sizing formulation ($x_t\le M_t\,y_t$, $0\le y_t\le 1$, setup cost $sc_t\,y_t$) the production is $x_t=34$ with $sc_t=89$. How much setup does it pay at least, with $M_t=43$ and with $M_t=82$?
  1. $\frac{1462}{89}$ and $\frac{2788}{89}$
  2. $\frac{1513}{41}$ and $\frac{3026}{43}$
  3. $89$ and $89$
  4. $\frac{3026}{43}$ and $\frac{1513}{41}$
Explanation. The constraint gives $y_t\ge x_t/M_t$, and the relaxation pays $sc_t\,x_t/M_t$: $\frac{3026}{43}$ and $\frac{1513}{41}$. A larger big-M makes the relaxation pay less setup, i.e., weakens it.
Example 2 · oc-g-ls-lpsetup · level facile · notes
In the LP relaxation of the big-M lot sizing formulation ($x_t\le M_t\,y_t$, $0\le y_t\le 1$, setup cost $sc_t\,y_t$) the production is $x_t=12$ with $sc_t=27$. How much setup does it pay at least, with $M_t=35$ and with $M_t=87$?
  1. $\frac{324}{35}$ and $\frac{108}{29}$
  2. $\frac{140}{9}$ and $\frac{116}{3}$
  3. $27$ and $27$
  4. $\frac{108}{29}$ and $\frac{324}{35}$
Explanation. The constraint gives $y_t\ge x_t/M_t$, and the relaxation pays $sc_t\,x_t/M_t$: $\frac{324}{35}$ and $\frac{108}{29}$. A larger big-M makes the relaxation pay less setup, i.e., weakens it.

17. DP for the 0/1 knapsack problem (2)

Example 1 · oc-g-kp-dp-cella · level facile · notes
0-1 knapsack problem (KP) with items $\begin{array}{c|cccc}j & 1 & 2 & 3 & 4\\ \hline p_j & 4 & 3 & 6 & 4\\ w_j & 6 & 3 & 2 & 4\end{array}$ and capacity $c=9$; $v(j,k)$ is the largest profit with items $1,\dots,j$ and capacity $k$, with $v(0,k)=0$. With the recursion $v(j,k)=\max\{v(j-1,k),\,p_j+v(j-1,k-w_j)\}$ (the second term only if $w_j\le k$), what is $v(3,7)$?
  1. $13$
  2. $4$
  3. $9$
  4. $18$
Explanation. $v(3,7)$ is the largest profit with the items $\{1,2,3\}$ and capacity $7$: it is $9$. Each item is used at most once (with repetitions one would get $18$, the value of the unbounded knapsack).
Example 2 · oc-g-kp-dp-cella · level facile · notes
0-1 knapsack problem (KP) with items $\begin{array}{c|cccc}j & 1 & 2 & 3 & 4\\ \hline p_j & 11 & 3 & 11 & 9\\ w_j & 3 & 3 & 3 & 5\end{array}$ and capacity $c=11$; $v(j,k)$ is the largest profit with items $1,\dots,j$ and capacity $k$, with $v(0,k)=0$. With the recursion $v(j,k)=\max\{v(j-1,k),\,p_j+v(j-1,k-w_j)\}$ (the second term only if $w_j\le k$), what is $v(3,10)$?
  1. $25$
  2. $14$
  3. $34$
  4. $33$
Explanation. $v(3,10)$ is the largest profit with the items $\{1,2,3\}$ and capacity $10$: it is $25$. Each item is used at most once (with repetitions one would get $33$, the value of the unbounded knapsack).
Example 1 · oc-g-kp-dp-completa · level complessa · notes
0-1 knapsack problem (KP: choose a subset of items of maximum profit with total weight at most $c$) with capacity $c=11$: $\begin{array}{c|cccc}j & 1 & 2 & 3 & 4\\ \hline p_j & 10 & 14 & 13 & 11\\ w_j & 4 & 5 & 2 & 5\end{array}$. The DP table, $v(j,k)$ = largest profit with items $1,\dots,j$ and capacity $k$, is computed and an optimal solution is reconstructed going back from $(4,11)$. What are the optimal value and the optimal set $\tilde S$?
  1. $v(4,11)=27$, $\tilde S=\{2,3\}$
  2. $v(4,11)=25$, $\tilde S=\{2,4\}$
  3. $v(4,11)=34$, $\tilde S=\{1,3,4\}$
  4. $v(4,11)=37$, $\tilde S=\{1,2,3\}$
Explanation. The table gives $v(4,11)=37$; going back from $(4,11)$ (item $j$ is selected if $v(j,k)\ne v(j-1,k)$) gives $\tilde S=\{1,2,3\}$. The greedy by efficiency only gives $37$.
Example 2 · oc-g-kp-dp-completa · level complessa · notes
0-1 knapsack problem (KP: choose a subset of items of maximum profit with total weight at most $c$) with capacity $c=11$: $\begin{array}{c|cccc}j & 1 & 2 & 3 & 4\\ \hline p_j & 13 & 4 & 14 & 11\\ w_j & 3 & 3 & 3 & 5\end{array}$. The DP table, $v(j,k)$ = largest profit with items $1,\dots,j$ and capacity $k$, is computed and an optimal solution is reconstructed going back from $(4,11)$. What are the optimal value and the optimal set $\tilde S$?
  1. $v(4,11)=38$, $\tilde S=\{1,3,4\}$
  2. $v(4,11)=29$, $\tilde S=\{2,3,4\}$
  3. $v(4,11)=31$, $\tilde S=\{1,2,3\}$
  4. $v(4,11)=28$, $\tilde S=\{1,2,4\}$
Explanation. The table gives $v(4,11)=38$; going back from $(4,11)$ (item $j$ is selected if $v(j,k)\ne v(j-1,k)$) gives $\tilde S=\{1,3,4\}$. The greedy by efficiency only gives $38$.

18. DP for the unbounded knapsack problem (2)

Example 1 · oc-g-ukp-dp · level facile · notes
Unbounded knapsack (UKP): $n=3$ item types, each available in unlimited copies, $\begin{array}{c|ccc}j & 1 & 2 & 3\\ \hline p_j & 10 & 18 & 21\\ w_j & 3 & 5 & 7\end{array}$, capacity $c=12$. $v(k)$ is the largest profit with capacity $k$: $v(0)=0$ and $v(k)=\max_{j:\,w_j\le k}\{p_j+v(k-w_j)\}$ (equal to $0$ if no type fits). What is $v(12)$?
  1. $36$
  2. $40$
  3. $39$
  4. $38$
Explanation. The table gives $v(12)=40$. Each type can be used several times: the value $39$ is that of the 0/1 KP, and the greedy by efficiency with repetitions ($36$) is not optimal in general.
Example 2 · oc-g-ukp-dp · level facile · notes
Unbounded knapsack (UKP): $n=3$ item types, each available in unlimited copies, $\begin{array}{c|ccc}j & 1 & 2 & 3\\ \hline p_j & 10 & 12 & 7\\ w_j & 4 & 6 & 3\end{array}$, capacity $c=14$. $v(k)$ is the largest profit with capacity $k$: $v(0)=0$ and $v(k)=\max_{j:\,w_j\le k}\{p_j+v(k-w_j)\}$ (equal to $0$ if no type fits). What is $v(14)$?
  1. $29$
  2. $31$
  3. $34$
  4. $30$
Explanation. The table gives $v(14)=34$. Each type can be used several times: the value $29$ is that of the 0/1 KP, and the greedy by efficiency with repetitions ($30$) is not optimal in general.
Example 1 · oc-g-ukp-sol · level media · notes
Unbounded knapsack (UKP): $n=3$ item types, each in unlimited copies, $\begin{array}{c|ccc}j & 1 & 2 & 3\\ \hline p_j & 11 & 9 & 13\\ w_j & 4 & 6 & 5\end{array}$, capacity $c=10$; $x_j$ is the number of copies of type $j$. What is the optimal value, and with which (unique) vector $x$ is it obtained?
  1. $22$ with $x=(2,0,0)$
  2. $24$ with $x=(1,0,1)$
  3. $26$ with $x=(0,0,2)$
  4. $26$ with $x=(2,0,0)$
Explanation. The DP $v(k)=\max_j\{p_j+v(k-w_j)\}$ gives $v(10)=26$, attained by $x=(0,0,2)$. The greedy by efficiency with repetitions gives $22$ and the 0-1 knapsack $24$.
Example 2 · oc-g-ukp-sol · level media · notes
Unbounded knapsack (UKP): $n=3$ item types, each in unlimited copies, $\begin{array}{c|ccc}j & 1 & 2 & 3\\ \hline p_j & 11 & 6 & 12\\ w_j & 4 & 5 & 5\end{array}$, capacity $c=15$; $x_j$ is the number of copies of type $j$. What is the optimal value, and with which (unique) vector $x$ is it obtained?
  1. $36$ with $x=(0,0,3)$
  2. $33$ with $x=(3,0,0)$
  3. $29$ with $x=(1,1,1)$
  4. $36$ with $x=(3,0,0)$
Explanation. The DP $v(k)=\max_j\{p_j+v(k-w_j)\}$ gives $v(15)=36$, attained by $x=(0,0,3)$. The greedy by efficiency with repetitions gives $33$ and the 0-1 knapsack $29$.

19. DP for the bounded knapsack problem (2)

Example 1 · oc-g-bkp-dp · level facile · notes
Bounded knapsack (BKP): type $j$ has profit $p_j$, weight $w_j$ and $b_j$ available copies, $\begin{array}{c|ccc}j & 1 & 2 & 3\\ \hline p_j & 4 & 3 & 4\\ w_j & 5 & 2 & 4\\ b_j & 1 & 1 & 2\end{array}$, capacity $c=10$. With $v(j,k)=\max_{0\le d\le\min\{b_j,\lfloor k/w_j\rfloor\}}\{d\,p_j+v(j-1,k-d\,w_j)\}$ and $v(0,k)=0$, what is the optimal value $v(3,10)$?
  1. $12$
  2. $8$
  3. $11$
  4. $15$
Explanation. The DP gives $v(3,10)=11$. With one copy per type (KP) one would get $8$, with unlimited copies (UKP) $15$.
Example 2 · oc-g-bkp-dp · level facile · notes
Bounded knapsack (BKP): type $j$ has profit $p_j$, weight $w_j$ and $b_j$ available copies, $\begin{array}{c|ccc}j & 1 & 2 & 3\\ \hline p_j & 11 & 6 & 9\\ w_j & 3 & 2 & 5\\ b_j & 1 & 3 & 3\end{array}$, capacity $c=11$. With $v(j,k)=\max_{0\le d\le\min\{b_j,\lfloor k/w_j\rfloor\}}\{d\,p_j+v(j-1,k-d\,w_j)\}$ and $v(0,k)=0$, what is the optimal value $v(3,11)$?
  1. $29$
  2. $26$
  3. $30$
  4. $39$
Explanation. The DP gives $v(3,11)=29$. With one copy per type (KP) one would get $26$, with unlimited copies (UKP) $39$.
Example 1 · oc-g-bkp-cella · level media · notes
Bounded knapsack (BKP): type $j$ has profit $p_j$, weight $w_j$ and $b_j$ copies, $\begin{array}{c|ccc}j & 1 & 2 & 3\\ \hline p_j & 5 & 6 & 7\\ w_j & 2 & 2 & 2\\ b_j & 1 & 2 & 2\end{array}$, capacity $c=11$. $v(j,k)$ is the largest profit with types $1,\dots,j$ only and capacity $k$, with at most $b_j$ copies of type $j$. What is $v(2,5)$?
  1. $11$
  2. $5$
  3. $12$
  4. $14$
Explanation. With types $1$ and $2$ and capacity $5$, respecting the bounds $b_1=1$ and $b_2=2$, the maximum is $12$. Without bounds on the copies one would get $12$.
Example 2 · oc-g-bkp-cella · level media · notes
Bounded knapsack (BKP): type $j$ has profit $p_j$, weight $w_j$ and $b_j$ copies, $\begin{array}{c|ccc}j & 1 & 2 & 3\\ \hline p_j & 11 & 6 & 9\\ w_j & 3 & 2 & 5\\ b_j & 1 & 3 & 3\end{array}$, capacity $c=12$. $v(j,k)$ is the largest profit with types $1,\dots,j$ only and capacity $k$, with at most $b_j$ copies of type $j$. What is $v(2,10)$?
  1. $11$
  2. $29$
  3. $34$
  4. $17$
Explanation. With types $1$ and $2$ and capacity $10$, respecting the bounds $b_1=1$ and $b_2=3$, the maximum is $29$. Without bounds on the copies one would get $34$.

20. DP for the subset sum problem (2)

Example 1 · oc-g-ssp-dp · level facile · notes
Subset sum: weights $w=(12,3,6,11,9)$ and target $c=28$. $r(j,k)$ is true if some subset of the first $j$ weights sums to exactly $k$: $r(0,0)$ true, $r(0,k)$ false for $k\ge 1$, and $r(j,k)=r(j-1,k)\lor r(j-1,k-w_j)$ (the second term only if $w_j\le k$). What is the largest $k\le c$ with $r(5,k)=\textit{true}$?
  1. $27$
  2. $26$
  3. $21$
  4. $18$
Explanation. The reachable sums not larger than $28$ have maximum $27$; $c=28$ is not reachable. The greedy choices (by non-increasing weight: $26$) do not guarantee the maximum.
Example 2 · oc-g-ssp-dp · level facile · notes
Subset sum: weights $w=(12,10,14,12,10)$ and target $c=27$. $r(j,k)$ is true if some subset of the first $j$ weights sums to exactly $k$: $r(0,0)$ true, $r(0,k)$ false for $k\ge 1$, and $r(j,k)=r(j-1,k)\lor r(j-1,k-w_j)$ (the second term only if $w_j\le k$). What is the largest $k\le c$ with $r(5,k)=\textit{true}$?
  1. $20$
  2. $22$
  3. $26$
  4. $27$
Explanation. The reachable sums not larger than $27$ have maximum $26$; $c=27$ is not reachable. The greedy choices (by non-increasing weight: $26$) do not guarantee the maximum.
Example 1 · oc-g-ssp-count · level media · notes
Subset sum with weights $w=(6,6,8,8)$ and target $c=10$. $r(j,k)$ is true if a subset of the first $j$ weights sums to exactly $k$ (the empty subset gives $k=0$). For how many values $k\in\{0,1,\dots,10\}$ is the cell $r(4,k)$ true?
  1. $3$
  2. $5$
  3. $2$
  4. $11$
Explanation. The reachable sums up to $10$ are $3$, including $k=0$. Different subsets may give the same sum: the subsets with sum at most $10$ are $5$.
Example 2 · oc-g-ssp-count · level media · notes
Subset sum with weights $w=(5,8,4,2)$ and target $c=13$. $r(j,k)$ is true if a subset of the first $j$ weights sums to exactly $k$ (the empty subset gives $k=0$). For how many values $k\in\{0,1,\dots,13\}$ is the cell $r(4,k)$ true?
  1. $12$
  2. $11$
  3. $14$
  4. $13$
Explanation. The reachable sums up to $13$ are $12$, including $k=0$. Different subsets may give the same sum: the subsets with sum at most $13$ are $12$.

21. DP for the multiple-choice knapsack problem (2)

Example 1 · oc-g-mckp-dp · level facile · notes
Multiple-choice knapsack (MCKP): classes $\mathscr{J}_1=\{1,2\}$, $\mathscr{J}_2=\{3,4\}$, $\mathscr{J}_3=\{5,6\}$, items $\begin{array}{c|cccccc}j & 1 & 2 & 3 & 4 & 5 & 6\\ \hline p_j & 2 & 4 & 3 & 5 & 2 & 3\\ w_j & 1 & 4 & 3 & 5 & 4 & 6\end{array}$, capacity $c=10$; exactly one item per class is chosen. With $v(h,k)=\max_{j\in\mathscr{J}_h:\,w_j\le k}\{p_j+v(h-1,k-w_j)\}$, $v(0,k)=0$, what is the optimal value $v(3,10)$?
  1. $8$
  2. $7$
  3. $9$
  4. $12$
Explanation. The optimal value is $9$. Ignoring the capacity one would take the best item of every class ($12$); with «at most one item per class» another problem would be solved ($9$).
Example 2 · oc-g-mckp-dp · level facile · notes
Multiple-choice knapsack (MCKP): classes $\mathscr{J}_1=\{1,2\}$, $\mathscr{J}_2=\{3,4\}$, $\mathscr{J}_3=\{5,6\}$, items $\begin{array}{c|cccccc}j & 1 & 2 & 3 & 4 & 5 & 6\\ \hline p_j & 2 & 6 & 7 & 2 & 6 & 9\\ w_j & 6 & 5 & 5 & 1 & 1 & 2\end{array}$, capacity $c=9$; exactly one item per class is chosen. With $v(h,k)=\max_{j\in\mathscr{J}_h:\,w_j\le k}\{p_j+v(h-1,k-w_j)\}$, $v(0,k)=0$, what is the optimal value $v(3,9)$?
  1. $17$
  2. $16$
  3. $22$
  4. $14$
Explanation. The optimal value is $17$. Ignoring the capacity one would take the best item of every class ($22$); with «at most one item per class» another problem would be solved ($17$).
Example 1 · oc-g-mckp-sol · level media · notes
Multiple-choice knapsack (MCKP): classes $\mathscr{J}_1=\{1,2\}$, $\mathscr{J}_2=\{3,4\}$, $\mathscr{J}_3=\{5,6\}$, items $\begin{array}{c|cccccc}j & 1 & 2 & 3 & 4 & 5 & 6\\ \hline p_j & 6 & 8 & 2 & 6 & 9 & 9\\ w_j & 4 & 6 & 5 & 4 & 1 & 2\end{array}$, capacity $c=11$; exactly one item per class is chosen. Which choice of items is optimal (the optimal solution is unique), and what is its value?
  1. $\{1,4,5\}$, value $21$
  2. $\{1,4,6\}$, value $21$
  3. $\{1,3,5\}$, value $17$
  4. $\{2,4,5\}$, value $23$
Explanation. The DP by classes and the backward reconstruction give $\{2,4,5\}$, of value $23$; the second best feasible choice is worth $21$.
Example 2 · oc-g-mckp-sol · level media · notes
Multiple-choice knapsack (MCKP): classes $\mathscr{J}_1=\{1,2\}$, $\mathscr{J}_2=\{3,4\}$, $\mathscr{J}_3=\{5,6\}$, items $\begin{array}{c|cccccc}j & 1 & 2 & 3 & 4 & 5 & 6\\ \hline p_j & 5 & 4 & 5 & 8 & 9 & 7\\ w_j & 6 & 1 & 6 & 4 & 5 & 6\end{array}$, capacity $c=15$; exactly one item per class is chosen. Which choice of items is optimal (the optimal solution is unique), and what is its value?
  1. $\{2,3,5\}$, value $18$
  2. $\{2,4,5\}$, value $21$
  3. $\{2,4,6\}$, value $19$
  4. $\{1,4,5\}$, value $22$
Explanation. The DP by classes and the backward reconstruction give $\{1,4,5\}$, of value $22$; the second best feasible choice is worth $21$.

22. DP for the multidimensional knapsack problem (2)

Example 1 · oc-g-mdkp-dp · level facile · notes
Multidimensional knapsack (MDKP) with two dimensions of capacity $c_1=8$ and $c_2=9$; item $j$ has profit $p_j$ and weights $w_{1j}$, $w_{2j}$: $\begin{array}{c|cccc}j & 1 & 2 & 3 & 4\\ \hline p_j & 4 & 8 & 3 & 8\\ w_{1j} & 4 & 3 & 4 & 6\\ w_{2j} & 5 & 2 & 4 & 2\end{array}$. Every item is taken at most once. With the DP $v(j,(k_1,k_2))$ on both capacities, what is the optimal value?
  1. $12$
  2. $13$
  3. $20$
  4. $16$
Explanation. The optimal value is $12$. Considering a single dimension one would get $12$ or $20$, and adding the constraints $16$: these are relaxations, not MDKP solutions.
Example 2 · oc-g-mdkp-dp · level facile · notes
Multidimensional knapsack (MDKP) with two dimensions of capacity $c_1=9$ and $c_2=11$; item $j$ has profit $p_j$ and weights $w_{1j}$, $w_{2j}$: $\begin{array}{c|cccc}j & 1 & 2 & 3 & 4\\ \hline p_j & 5 & 8 & 5 & 10\\ w_{1j} & 2 & 4 & 4 & 6\\ w_{2j} & 6 & 3 & 1 & 4\end{array}$. Every item is taken at most once. With the DP $v(j,(k_1,k_2))$ on both capacities, what is the optimal value?
  1. $16$
  2. $23$
  3. $18$
  4. $15$
Explanation. The optimal value is $15$. Considering a single dimension one would get $15$ or $23$, and adding the constraints $18$: these are relaxations, not MDKP solutions.
Example 1 · oc-g-mdkp-sol · level media · notes
Multidimensional knapsack (MDKP) with two dimensions of capacity $c_1=8$ and $c_2=7$; item $j$ has profit $p_j$ and weights $w_{1j}$, $w_{2j}$: $\begin{array}{c|cccc}j & 1 & 2 & 3 & 4\\ \hline p_j & 7 & 9 & 7 & 3\\ w_{1j} & 4 & 1 & 4 & 6\\ w_{2j} & 5 & 5 & 6 & 2\end{array}$. Every item is taken at most once. Which set of items is the (unique) optimal solution?
  1. $\{1\}$
  2. $\{2\}$
  3. $\{1,2\}$
  4. $\{2,4\}$
Explanation. $\{2,4\}$ respects both capacities and is worth $12$; the second best feasible solution is worth $9$.
Example 2 · oc-g-mdkp-sol · level media · notes
Multidimensional knapsack (MDKP) with two dimensions of capacity $c_1=9$ and $c_2=10$; item $j$ has profit $p_j$ and weights $w_{1j}$, $w_{2j}$: $\begin{array}{c|cccc}j & 1 & 2 & 3 & 4\\ \hline p_j & 10 & 8 & 7 & 5\\ w_{1j} & 6 & 2 & 1 & 2\\ w_{2j} & 5 & 4 & 1 & 1\end{array}$.   2. $\{2,3,4\}$
  3. $\{1,2\}$
